\partial_ {t} \rho_ {t} (u _ {1}, u _ {2}) = \operatorname {d i v} [ \rho_ {t} (u _ {1}, u _ {2}) G (u _ {1}, u _ {2}; \rho_ {t}) ],
$$

in which

$$
G \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}; \rho_ {t}\right) = \left[ \begin{array}{l} \xi_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \underline {{\Delta}} _ {1} ^ {w} \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}; Z, \rho_ {t}\right) \right] \\ \xi_ {2} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \underline {{\Delta}} _ {2} ^ {w} \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}; Z, \rho_ {t}\right) \right] \end{array} \right],
$$

and we define

$$
\underline {{H}} _ {2} \left(x; \rho_ {t}\right) = \int \phi_ {2} \left(u _ {2}, 0, \phi_ {1} \left(u _ {1}, x\right)\right) d \rho_ {t} \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}\right),
$$

$$
\hat {y} \left(x; \rho_ {t}\right) = \phi_ {3} \left(H _ {2} \left(x; \rho_ {t}\right)\right),
$$

$$
\underline {{\Delta}} _ {2} ^ {H} \left(z; \rho_ {t}\right) = \sigma_ {2} ^ {\mathbf {H}} \left(y, \underline {{\hat {y}}} \left(x; \rho_ {t}\right), \underline {{H}} _ {2} \left(x; \rho_ {t}\right)\right),
$$

$$
\underline {{\Delta}} _ {2} ^ {w} \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}; z, \rho_ {t}\right) = \sigma_ {2} ^ {\mathbf {w}} \left(\underline {{\Delta}} _ {2} ^ {H} \left(z; \rho_ {t}\right), u _ {2}, 0, \underline {{H}} _ {2} (x; \rho_ {t}), \phi_ {1} (u _ {1}, x)\right),
$$

$$
\underline {{\Delta}} _ {1} ^ {H} \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}; z, \rho_ {t}\right) = \sigma_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {H}} \left(\underline {{\Delta}} _ {2} ^ {H} \left(z; \rho_ {t}\right), u _ {2}, 0, \underline {{H}} _ {2} \left(x; \rho_ {t}\right), \phi_ {1} \left(u _ {1}, x\right)\right),
$$

$$
\underline {{\Delta}} _ {1} ^ {w} \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}; z, \rho_ {t}\right) = \sigma_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {w}} \left(\underline {{\Delta}} _ {1} ^ {H} \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}; z, \rho_ {t}\right), u _ {1}, x\right).
$$

In particular, for any  $\delta > 0$  and any  $K$ -Lipschitz and  $K$ -bounded test function  $\psi : \mathbb{W}_1 \times \mathbb{W}_2 \to \mathbb{S}$ , where  $\mathbb{S}$  is a separable Hilbert space,

$$
\sup  _ {t \leq T} \left| \frac {1}{n _ {1}} \sum_ {j _ {1} = 1} ^ {n _ {1}} \psi \left(\mathbf {w} _ {1} \left(\lfloor t / \epsilon \rfloor , j _ {1}\right), \mathbf {w} _ {2} \left(\lfloor t / \epsilon \rfloor , j _ {1}, 1\right)\right) - \int \psi \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}\right) d \rho_ {t} \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}\right) \right| = \tilde {\mathcal {O}} \left(n _ {1} ^ {- c _ {1}} + \epsilon^ {c _ {1}}\right).
$$

with probability at least  $1 - 3\delta - Kn_{1}\exp(-Kn_{1}^{c_{2}})$ , where  $\tilde{O}$  hides the dependency on  $T$  and  $\delta$  as well as the logarithmic factors  $\log n_{1}$  and  $\log (1 / \epsilon)$ . Similarly, for any test function  $\psi : \mathbb{Y} \times \hat{\mathbb{Y}} \to \mathbb{S}$  which is  $K$ -Lipschitz in the second variable, uniformly in the first variable,

$$
\sup  _ {t \leq T} \left| \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \psi \left(Y, \hat {\mathbf {y}} \left(\lfloor t / \epsilon \rfloor , X\right)\right) \right] - \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \psi \left(Y, \underline {{\hat {y}}} (X; \rho_ {t})\right) \right] \right| = \tilde {O} \left(n _ {1} ^ {- c _ {1}} + \epsilon^ {c _ {1}}\right),
$$

with probability at least  $1 - 3\delta -Kn_{1}\exp \left(-Kn_{1}^{c_{2}}\right)$

# 4.3 Proof of Theorem 15

We construct an auxiliary trajectory, which we call the particle ODEs:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\partial}{\partial t} \tilde {w} _ {1} (t, j _ {1}) = - \xi_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {w}} \left(Z, j _ {1}; \tilde {W} (t)\right) \right], \quad \forall j _ {1} \in [ n _ {1} ], \\ \frac {\partial}{\partial t} \tilde {w} _ {i} (t, j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}) = - \xi_ {i} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {i} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (Z, j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}; \tilde {W} (t)) \right], \\ \frac {\partial}{\partial t} \tilde {b} _ {i} (t, j _ {i}) = - \xi_ {i} ^ {\mathbf {b}} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {i} ^ {\mathbf {b}} \left(Z, j _ {i}; \tilde {W} (t)\right) \right], \quad \forall j _ {i - 1} \in [ n _ {i - 1} ], j _ {i} \in [ n _ {i} ], i = 2, \dots , L, \\ \end{array}
$$

in which  $\tilde{W}(t) = \left\{\tilde{w}_1(t,\cdot), \tilde{w}_i(t,\cdot,\cdot), \tilde{b}_i(t,\cdot), i = 2,\dots,L\right\}$ , and  $t \in \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ . We specify the initialization  $\tilde{W}(0)$ :  $\tilde{w}_1(0,j_1) = w_1^0(C_1(j_1))$ ,  $\tilde{w}_i(0,j_{i-1},j_i) = w_i^0(C_{i-1}(j_{i-1}),C_i(j_i))$  and  $\tilde{b}_i(0,j_i) = b_i^0(C_i(j_i))$ . That is, it shares the same initialization with the neural network one  $\mathbf{W}(0)$ , and hence is coupled with the neural network and the MF ODEs. Roughly speaking, the particle ODEs are continuous-time trajectories of finitely many neurons, averaged over the data distribution. We note that  $\tilde{W}(t)$  is random for all  $t \in \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$  due to the randomness of  $C_i(j_i)$ 's.

The existence and uniqueness of the solution to the particle ODEs follows from the same proof as in

Theorem 7, which we shall not repeat here. We equip  $\tilde{W}(t)$  with the norms:

$$
\| \tilde {w} _ {i} \| _ {t} = \left(\frac {1}{n _ {i - 1} n _ {i}} \sum_ {j _ {i - 1} = 1} ^ {n _ {i - 1}} \sum_ {j _ {i} = 1} ^ {n _ {i}} \sup _ {s \leq t} | \tilde {w} _ {i} (s, j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}) | ^ {5 0}\right) ^ {1 / 5 0},
$$

$$
\| \tilde {b} _ {i} \| _ {t} = \left(\frac {1}{n _ {i}} \sum_ {j _ {i} = 1} ^ {n _ {i}} \sup  _ {s \leq t} \left| \tilde {b} _ {i} (s, j _ {i}) \right| ^ {5 0}\right) ^ {1 / 5 0}, \qquad i = 2, \dots , L,
$$

$$
\| \tilde {w} _ {1} \| _ {t} = \left(\frac {1}{n _ {1}} \sum_ {j _ {1} = 1} ^ {n _ {1}} \sup  _ {s \leq t} | \tilde {w} _ {1} (s, j _ {1}) | ^ {5 0}\right) ^ {1 / 5 0}.
$$

as well as

$$
\| \tilde {W} \| _ {t} = \max  \left(\max  _ {1 \leq i \leq L} \| \tilde {w} _ {i} \| _ {t}, \max  _ {2 \leq i \leq L} \| \tilde {b} _ {i} \| _ {t}\right).
$$

One can also define the measures  $\mathcal{D}_T(W, \tilde{W})$  and  $\mathcal{D}_T(\tilde{W}, \mathbf{W})$  similar to Eq. (3):

$$
\mathcal {D} _ {T} \left(W, \tilde {W}\right) = \max  \left(\max  _ {2 \leq i \leq L} \left(\frac {1}{n _ {i - 1} n _ {i}} \sum_ {j _ {i - 1} = 1} ^ {n _ {i - 1}} \sum_ {j _ {i} = 1} ^ {n _ {i}} \left| w _ {i} \left(t, C _ {i - 1} \left(j _ {i - 1}\right), C _ {i} \left(j _ {i}\right)\right) - \tilde {w} _ {i} \left(t, j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}\right) \right| ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2}, \right.
$$

$$
\max  _ {2 \leq i \leq L} \left(\frac {1}{n _ {i}} \sum_ {j _ {i} = 1} ^ {n _ {i}} \sup  _ {t \leq T} \left| b _ {i} (t, C _ {i} (j _ {i})) - \tilde {b} _ {i} (t, j _ {i}) \right| ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2},
$$

$$
\left(\frac {1}{n _ {1}} \sum_ {j _ {1} = 1} ^ {n _ {1}} \sup  _ {t \leq T} \left| w _ {1} (t, C _ {1} (j _ {1})) - \tilde {w} _ {1} (t, j _ {1}) \right| ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2}\left. \right),
$$

$$
\mathcal {D} _ {T} \left(\tilde {W}, \mathbf {W}\right) = \max  \left(\max  _ {2 \leq i \leq L} \left(\frac {1}{n _ {i - 1} n _ {i}} \sum_ {j _ {i - 1} = 1} ^ {n _ {i - 1}} \sum_ {j _ {i} = 1} ^ {n _ {i}} | \mathbf {w} _ {i} \left(\lfloor t / \epsilon \rfloor , j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}\right) - \tilde {w} _ {i} \left(t, j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}\right) | ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2}, \right.
$$

$$
\max  _ {2 \leq i \leq L} \left(\frac {1}{n _ {i}} \sum_ {j _ {i} = 1} ^ {n _ {i}} \sup  _ {t \leq T} \left| \mathbf {b} _ {i} \left(\lfloor t / \epsilon \rfloor , j _ {i}\right) - \tilde {b} _ {i} \left(t, j _ {i}\right) \right| ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2},
$$

$$
\left(\frac {1}{n _ {1}} \sum_ {j _ {1} = 1} ^ {n _ {1}} \sup  _ {t \leq T} \left| \mathbf {w} _ {1} \left(\lfloor t / \epsilon \rfloor , j _ {1}\right) - \tilde {w} _ {1} (t, j _ {1}) \right| ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2}\left. \right).
$$

We have the following results:

Theorem 20. Under the same setting as Theorem 15, there exist constants  $c_{1} \in (0,0.5)$  and  $c_{2} \in (0,1/52)$ , such that for any  $\delta > 0$ ,  $L \geq 1$  and  $T \geq 1$ , the following holds. There exists  $n^{*} = n^{*}(T,L,c_{1},c_{2}) \geq 1$  such that for any  $n_{\min} \geq n^{*}$ ,

$$
\mathbb {P} \left(\mathcal {D} _ {T} (W, \tilde {W}) \geq K n _ {\min } ^ {- c _ {1}} \sqrt {\log \left(\frac {1}{\delta} n _ {\max } ^ {2} + e\right)}\right) \leq \delta + K L n _ {\max } \exp (- K n _ {\min } ^ {c _ {2}}).
$$

Here  $n_{\mathrm{min}} = \min_{1 \leq j \leq L - 1} n_j$  and  $n_{\mathrm{max}} = \max_{1 \leq j \leq L} n_j$ .

Theorem 21. Under the same setting as Theorem 15, there exist constants  $c_1 \in (0, 0.5)$  and  $c_2 \in (0, 1/52)$ , such that for any  $\delta > 0$ ,  $L \geq 1$  and  $T \geq 1$ , the following holds. There exists  $\epsilon^* = \epsilon^* (T, L, c_1, c_2) \leq 1$  such that for any  $\epsilon \in (0, \epsilon^*)$ ,

$$
\mathbb {P} \bigg (\mathcal {D} _ {T} \left(\tilde {W}, \mathbf {W}\right) \geq K \epsilon^ {c _ {1}} \sqrt {\log \left(\frac {1}{\delta} n _ {\max} ^ {2} + e\right)} \bigg) \leq \delta + K L n _ {\max} \exp \left(- K n _ {\min} ^ {c _ {2}}\right).
$$

Here  $n_{\mathrm{min}} = \min_{1 \leq j \leq L - 1} n_j$  and  $n_{\mathrm{max}} = \max_{1 \leq j \leq L} n_j$ .

Proof of Theorem 15. Using the fact

$$
\mathcal {D} _ {T} \left(W, \mathbf {W}\right) \leq \mathcal {D} _ {T} \left(W, \tilde {W}\right) + \mathcal {D} _ {T} \left(\tilde {W}, \mathbf {W}\right),
$$

the thesis is immediate from Theorems 20 and 21.

![](images/6e4f1275769e9140ea12ddf27a4622c25d17f8a162b2588fe349ef40cc075e17.jpg)

# 4.4 Proof of Theorems 20 and 21

The proof of Theorem 20 rests in the following proposition, which is essentially a version of Theorem 20 with an extra boundedness condition at initialization.

Proposition 22. Under the same setting as Theorem 15, for a given  $B > 0$ , further assume that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {e s s - s u p m a x} _ {0} ^ {w} (W) = \operatorname {e s s - s u p} _ {2 \leq i \leq L} \max  _ {i} \left| w _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}\right) \right| \leq B, \\ \operatorname {e s s - s u p m a x} _ {0} ^ {b} (W) = \operatorname {e s s - s u p} _ {2 \leq i \leq L} \max  _ {i} \left| b _ {i} ^ {0} (C _ {i}) \right| \leq B. \\ \end{array}
$$

Then for any  $\delta > 0$ , with probability at least  $1 - \delta - KLn_{\max} \exp \left(-Kn_{\min}^{1/52}\right)$ ,

$$
\mathcal {D} _ {T} \left(W, \tilde {W}\right) \leq \sqrt {\frac {1}{n _ {\mathrm {m i n}}} \log \left(\frac {2 T L}{\delta} n _ {\mathrm {m a x}} ^ {2} + e\right)} \exp \left(K ^ {\bar {K}} \left(1 + T ^ {\bar {K}}\right) (1 + B)\right),
$$

in which  $n_{\mathrm{min}} = \min_{1\leq j\leq L - 1}n_j$ ,  $n_{\mathrm{max}} = \max_{1\leq j\leq L}n_j$ , and  $\bar{K}$  is a constant that depends on  $L$  such that  $\bar{K}\le K^L$  for some sufficiently large constant  $K$ .

Similar to Proposition 22, the following proposition is essentially a version of Theorem 21 with an extra boundedness condition at initialization.

Proposition 23. Under the same setting as Theorem 15, for a given  $B > 0$ , further assume that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {e s s - s u p m a x} _ {0} ^ {w} (W) = \operatorname {e s s - s u p} _ {2 \leq i \leq L} \max  _ {i = 1} ^ {w} \left| w _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}\right) \right| \leq B, \\ \operatorname {e s s - s u p m a x} _ {0} ^ {b} (W) = \operatorname {e s s - s u p} _ {2 \leq i \leq L} \max  _ {i} \left| b _ {i} ^ {0} (C _ {i}) \right| \leq B. \\ \end{array}
$$

Then for any  $\delta > 0$  and  $\epsilon < 1$ , with probability at least  $1 - \delta - KLn_{\max} \exp \left(-Kn_{\min}^{1/52}\right)$ ,

$$
\mathcal {D} _ {T} \left(\tilde {W}, \mathbf {W}\right) \leq \sqrt {\epsilon \log \left(\frac {2 L}{\delta} n _ {\max} ^ {2} + e\right)} \exp \left(K ^ {\bar {K}} \left(1 + T ^ {\bar {K}}\right) (1 + B)\right),
$$

in which  $n_{\mathrm{min}} = \min_{1\leq j\leq L - 1}n_j$ ,  $n_{\mathrm{max}} = \max_{1\leq j\leq L}n_j$ , and  $\bar{K}$  is a constant that depends on  $L$  such that  $\bar{K}\le K^L$  for some sufficiently large constant  $K$ .

The following proposition bridges the last two propositions with their respective theorems.

Proposition 24. Assume the same setting as Theorem 15. Let  $\underline{W}(t) = \{\underline{w}_1(t,\cdot), \underline{w}_i(t,\cdot,\cdot), \underline{b}_i(t,\cdot), i = 2,\dots,L\}$  be the MF ODEs' solution for which its initialization  $\underline{W}(0)$  is a truncated version of  $W(0)$ , for a given  $B > 0$ :

$$
\underline {{w}} _ {1} \left(0, c _ {1}\right) = w _ {1} ^ {0} \left(c _ {1}\right),
$$

$$
\underline {{w}} _ {i} \left(0, c _ {i - 1}, c _ {i}\right) = \operatorname {T r u n c} _ {B} \left(w _ {i} ^ {0} \left(c _ {i - 1}, c _ {i}\right)\right),
$$

$$
\underline {{b}} _ {i} \left(0, c _ {i}\right) = \operatorname {T r u n c} _ {B} \left(b _ {i} ^ {0} \left(c _ {i}\right)\right),
$$

for  $2\leq i\leq L$  , where  $\mathrm{Trunc}_B(u) = u\mathbb{I}(|u|\leq B) + B\mathrm{sign}(u)\mathbb{I}(|u| > B)$  . Then:

$$
\left\| W - \underline {{W}} \right\| _ {T} \leq K \exp \left(- K B ^ {2} + K ^ {\bar {K}} \left(1 + T ^ {\bar {K}}\right) (1 + B)\right),
$$

for  $\bar{K}$  a constant that depends on  $L$  such that  $\bar{K} \leq K^L$  for some sufficiently large constant  $K$ . Similarly let  $\underline{\tilde{W}}$  and  $\underline{\mathbf{W}}$  be the particle ODEs' solution and the neural network's dynamics with a similarly truncated initialization:

$$
\underline {{\underline {{w}}}} _ {1} (0, j _ {1}) = \underline {{\underline {{w}}}} _ {1} (0, j _ {1}) = w _ {1} ^ {0} \left(C _ {1} (j _ {1})\right),
$$

$$
\underline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} _ {i} \left(0, j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}\right) = \underline {{\mathbf {w}}} _ {i} \left(0, j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}\right) = \operatorname {T r u n c} _ {B} \left(w _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i - 1} \left(j _ {i - 1}\right), C _ {i} \left(j _ {i}\right)\right)\right),
$$

$$
\tilde {\underline {{b}}} _ {i} \left(0, j _ {i}\right) = \underline {{\mathbf {b}}} _ {i} \left(0, j _ {i}\right) = \operatorname {T r u n c} _ {B} \left(b _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i} \left(j _ {i}\right)\right)\right).
$$

Then with probability at least  $1 - KLn_{\mathrm{max}}\exp \left(-Ke^{-KB^2}n_{\mathrm{min}}^{1 / 52}\right)$

$$
\left\| \tilde {W} - \underline {{\tilde {W}}} \right\| _ {T}, \| \mathbf {W} - \underline {{\mathbf {W}}} \| _ {T} \leq K \exp \left(- K B ^ {2} + K ^ {\bar {K}} \left(1 + T ^ {\bar {K}}\right) (1 + B)\right).
$$

Here  $n_{\mathrm{max}} = \max (n_1,\dots,n_L)$ ,  $n_{\mathrm{min}} = \min (n_1,\dots,n_{L - 1})$

$$
\left\| \mathbf {W} - \underline {{\mathbf {W}}} \right\| _ {t} = \max  \left(\max  _ {2 \leq i \leq L} \left\| \mathbf {w} _ {i} - \underline {{\mathbf {w}}} _ {i} \right\| _ {t}, \max  _ {2 \leq i \leq L} \left\| \mathbf {b} _ {i} - \underline {{\mathbf {b}}} _ {i} \right\| _ {t}, \left\| \mathbf {w} _ {1} - \underline {{\mathbf {w}}} _ {1} \right\| _ {t}\right),
$$

$$
\left\| \mathbf {w} _ {i} - \underline {{\mathbf {w}}} _ {i} \right\| _ {t} = \left(\frac {1}{n _ {i - 1} n _ {i}} \sum_ {j _ {i - 1} = 1} ^ {n _ {i - 1}} \sum_ {j _ {i} = 1} ^ {n _ {i}} \sup _ {s \leq t} \left| \mathbf {w} _ {i} \left(\lfloor s / \epsilon \rfloor , j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}\right) - \underline {{\mathbf {w}}} _ {i} \left(\lfloor s / \epsilon \rfloor , j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}\right) \right| ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2},
$$

$$
\| \mathbf {b} _ {i} - \underline {{\mathbf {b}}} _ {i} \| _ {t} = \left(\frac {1}{n _ {i}} \sum_ {j _ {i} = 1} ^ {n _ {i}} \sup _ {s \leq t} | \mathbf {b} _ {i} (\lfloor s / \epsilon \rfloor , j _ {i}) - \underline {{\mathbf {b}}} _ {i} (\lfloor s / \epsilon \rfloor , j _ {i}) | ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2}, \qquad i = 2, \dots , L,
$$

$$
\left\| \mathbf {w} _ {1} - \underline {{\mathbf {w}}} _ {1} \right\| _ {t} = \left(\frac {1}{n _ {1}} \sum_ {j _ {1} = 1} ^ {n _ {1}} \sup  _ {s \leq t} \left| \mathbf {w} _ {1} \left(\lfloor s / \epsilon \rfloor , j _ {1}\right) - \underline {{\mathbf {w}}} _ {1} \left(\lfloor s / \epsilon \rfloor , j _ {1}\right) \right| ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2},
$$

and  $\left\| \tilde{W} - \underline{\tilde{W}} \right\|_t$  is defined similarly.

We can now prove Theorems 20 and 21.

Proof of Theorem 20. Let  $K_{T} = K^{\bar{K}}\left(1 + T^{\bar{K}}\right)$ . For a given  $B > 0$ , let  $\underline{W}$  and  $\tilde{W}$  be the initialization-truncated versions of  $W$  and  $\tilde{W}$  respectively, as per Proposition 24. Then Proposition 22 states that for any  $\delta > 0$ , with probability at least  $1 - \delta - KLn_{\mathrm{max}}\exp\left(-Kn_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/52}\right)$ ,

$$
\mathcal {D} _ {T} \left(\underline {{W}}, \underline {{\tilde {W}}}\right) \leq \sqrt {\frac {1}{n _ {\mathrm {m i n}}} \log \left(\frac {2 T L}{\delta} n _ {\mathrm {m a x}} ^ {2} + e\right)} e ^ {K _ {T} (1 + B)}.
$$

Proposition 24 further gives that with probability at least  $1 - KLn_{\mathrm{max}}\exp \left(-Ke^{-KB^2}n_{\mathrm{min}}^{1 / 52}\right)$

$$
\left\| \tilde {W} - \underline {{\tilde {W}}} \right\| _ {T}, \| W - \underline {{W}} \| _ {T} \leq K e ^ {- K B ^ {2} + K _ {T} (1 + B)}.
$$

Also notice that

$$
\mathcal {D} _ {T} \left(W, \tilde {W}\right) \leq \mathcal {D} _ {T} \left(\underline {{W}}, \underline {{\tilde {W}}}\right) + \| W - \underline {{W}} \| _ {T} + \left\| \tilde {W} - \underline {{\tilde {W}}} \right\| _ {T}.
$$

As such,

$$
\mathcal {D} _ {T} \left(W, \tilde {W}\right) \leq \left(\sqrt {\frac {1}{n _ {\mathrm {m i n}}} \log \left(\frac {2 T L}{\delta} n _ {\mathrm {m a x}} ^ {2} + e\right)} + e ^ {- K B ^ {2}}\right) e ^ {K _ {T} (1 + B)},
$$

with probability at least  $1 - \delta - KLn_{\max} \exp \left(-Ke^{-KB^2}n_{\min}^{1/52}\right)$ , for any fixed  $B > 0$ . Then upon choosing  $B = c_0\sqrt{\log n_{\min}}$  for some suitable constant  $c_0 > 0$  independent of  $T$ , it is easy to see that there exist constants  $c_1 \in (0, 0.5)$  and  $c_2 \in (0, 1/52)$  independent of  $T$  and some  $n^* = n^*(T, L, c_1, c_2) \geq 1$  such that for any  $n_{\min} \geq n^*$ , we have:

$$
\mathbb {P} \left(\mathcal {D} _ {T} (W, \tilde {W}) \geq K n _ {\min } ^ {- c _ {1}} \sqrt {\log \left(\frac {1}{\delta} n _ {\max } ^ {2} + e\right)}\right) \leq \delta + K L n _ {\max } \exp (- K n _ {\min } ^ {c _ {2}}).
$$

Proof of Theorem 21. This comes from Propositions 23 and 24, similar to the proof of Theorem 20.

![](images/87ff0c664fd0be8c48da0d719f28e47d3a613fa62614b72d9b424e950467a0dd.jpg)

Let us mention again the correspondence between Theorem 20 and Proposition 22, and that between Theorem 21 and Proposition 23. The truncation at initialization allows for technical feasibility and is then bridged by Proposition 24. The proofs of Propositions 22 and 23 are necessarily lengthy, so let us defer them (as well as missing proofs of other results) to Appendix C. Let us describe briefly the argument for Proposition 22. One recalls that  $w_{i}\left(0,C_{i - 1}(j_{i - 1}),C_{i}(j_{i})\right) = \tilde{w}_{i}\left(0,j_{i - 1},j_{i}\right)$  at initialization  $t = 0$ , and hence one hopes to prove:

$$
w _ {i} \left(t, C _ {i - 1} \left(j _ {i - 1}\right), C _ {i} \left(j _ {i}\right)\right) \approx \tilde {w} _ {i} \left(t, j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}\right)
$$

at any finite  $t$ . In other words, we would like to show

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {i} ^ {w} \left(Z, C _ {i - 1} (j _ {i - 1}), C _ {i} (j _ {i}); W (t)\right) \right] \approx \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {i} ^ {\mathbf {w}} \left(Z, j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}; \tilde {W} (t)\right) \right].
$$

Both of these quantities share very similar structures. Roughly speaking, the left-hand side involves quantities that assume the form of an expectation  $\mathbb{E}_{C_r}[g(C_r)]$  and the right-hand side correspondingly involves quantities of the form of an empirical average  $(1 / n_{r})\cdot \sum_{j_{r} = 1}^{n_{r}}g(C_{r}(j_{r}))$ , for some function  $g$ . An invocation of concentration of measure bounds links the two sides, and if done correctly over the training horizon (i.e. over  $t\leq T$ ), the depth of the network (i.e. over index  $i\leq L$ ) and the width at each layer (i.e. over neuron  $j_{i}\leq n_{i}$ ), it gives the desired estimation. One also recognizes that the neural network  $\mathbf{W}$  is essentially a time discretization version of  $\tilde{W}$  where the learning rate  $\epsilon$  plays the role of the discretization level. A martingale-type argument then suffices to prove Proposition 23 for small  $\epsilon$ .

# 5 Simplifications under Independent and Identically Distributed Initialization

In this section, we prove that the MF limit under an independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) initialization degenerates to a simple structured dynamics. Let us first state the definition of i.i.d. initializations.

Definition 25. An initialization law  $\rho$  for a neural network of size  $\{n_1,\dots,n_L\}$  is called  $(\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{1},\dots,\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{L},\rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{2},\dots,\rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{L})$ -i.i.d. initialization (or i.i.d. initialization, for brevity), where  $\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^i$  is a probability measure over  $\mathbb{W}_i$  and  $\rho_{\mathbf{b}}^i$  is a probability measure over  $\mathbb{B}_i$ , if it satisfies the following:

-  $\{\mathbf{w}_1(0,j_1)\}_{j_1\in [n_1]}$  are generated i.i.d. according to  $\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{1}$ ,  
- for each  $i = 2, \dots, L$ ,  $\{\mathbf{w}_i(0,j_{i-1},j_i)\}_{j_{i-1} \in [n_{i-1}], j_i \in [n_i]}$  are generated i.i.d. according to  $\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^i$ , and  $\{\mathbf{b}_i(0,j_i)\}_{j_i \in [n_i]}$  are generated i.i.d. according to  $\rho_{\mathbf{b}}^i$ ,  
- all these generations are independent of each other, and  $\rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{L}$  is a single point mass.

Observe that given  $\left(\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{1}, \ldots, \rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{L}, \rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{2}, \ldots, \rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{L}\right)$ , one can build a family Init of i.i.d. initialization laws that contains any index tuple  $\{n_1, \ldots, n_L\}$ .

In the following, we construct a canonical MF limit under i.i.d. initialization and show that the MF dynamics can be significantly simplified. Our plan is as follows:

1. We first construct a sequence (in increasing  $M$ ) of neuronal embeddings, which we call canonical neuronal embeddings. In particular, each of these - indexed by  $M$  - allows to embed i.i.d.-initialized neural networks of sizes at most  $M$ . Each canonical neuronal embedding is associated with a MF limit, which we call a canonical MF limit.  
2. We present a dynamics which is shown to be the infinite- $M$  limit of the canonical MF limits. This dynamics displays the simplifying properties that we wish to show. In particular, the dynamics of i.i.d.-initialized neural networks of large widths are well-approximated by the infinite- $M$  limit, and asymptotically displays the same simplifying properties.

This plan streamlines our studies of i.i.d.-initialized networks in the infinite-width limit. As we shall see, the construction of the canonical neuronal embedding is quite natural due to the cap at finite  $M$ . More importantly, on one hand, the fact that the canonical MF limit tracks closely the neural network of size less than  $M$  demonstrates flexibility of Theorem 15 from Section 4, in that its applicability is not limited to abstract infinite-width limits. On the other hand, the fact that the simplifying properties are shown in the infinite- $M$  limit demonstrates the advantage of working with these abstract infinite-width dynamics: they reveal properties that are virtually invisible at the finite-width level.

# 5.1 Neuronal embedding construction and main results

# 5.1.1 Canonical neuronal embeddings and canonical MF limits

We describe the construction in three steps with a given positive integer  $M$  and a set of measures  $\left(\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{1}, \ldots, \rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{L}, \rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{2}, \ldots, \rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{L}\right)$ .

Step 1. We first give a description of a  $\sigma$ -finite measure space. Consider a probability space  $(\Lambda, P_0)$  of the random processes  $\mathbb{W}_1$ -valued  $\mathfrak{p}_1(\theta_1)$ ,  $\mathbb{W}_i$ -valued  $\mathfrak{q}_i(\theta_{i-1}, \theta_i)$  and  $\mathbb{B}_i$ -valued  $\mathfrak{p}_i(\theta_i)$  for  $2 \leq i \leq L$ . These processes are indexed by  $\theta_i \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$  and satisfy the following property. Let  $m_1, \ldots, m_{L-1}$  be  $L-1$  arbitrary finite positive integers and, with these integers, let  $\left\{\theta_i^{(k_i)} : k_i \in [m_i], i = 1, \ldots, L-1\right\}$  be an arbitrary collection. Let  $m_L = 1$  and  $\theta_L^{(1)} = 1$ . For each  $i = 1, \ldots, L$ , let  $S_i$  be the set of unique elements in  $\left\{\theta_i^{(k_i)} : k_i \in [m_i]\right\}$ . Similarly, for each  $i = 2, \ldots, L$ , let  $R_i$  be the set of unique pairs in  $\left\{\left(\theta_{i-1}^{(k_{i-1})}, \theta_i^{(k_i)}\right) : k_{i-1} \in [m_{i-1}], k_i \in [m_i]\right\}$ . The space  $(\Lambda, P_0)$  satisfies that  $\{\mathfrak{p}_i(\theta_i) : \theta_i \in S_i, i = 1, \ldots, L\}$  and  $\{\mathfrak{q}_i(\theta_{i-1}, \theta_i) : (\theta_{i-1}, \theta_i) \in R_i, i = 2, \ldots, L\}$  are all mutually independent. In addition, we also have

$$
\operatorname {L a w} \left(\mathfrak {p} _ {1} \left(\theta_ {1}\right)\right) = \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {1}, \quad \operatorname {L a w} \left(\mathfrak {p} _ {i} \left(\theta_ {i}\right)\right) = \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i}, \quad \operatorname {L a w} \left(\mathfrak {q} _ {i} \left(\theta_ {i - 1} ^ {\prime}, \theta_ {i} ^ {\prime}\right)\right) = \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {i}
$$

for any  $\theta_{1} \in S_{1}$ ,  $\theta_{i} \in S_{i}$  and  $(\theta_{i-1}', \theta_{i}') \in R_{i}$ , for  $i = 2, \dots, L$ . Such a space  $(\Lambda, P_{0})$  exists by Kolmogorov's extension theorem.

Step 2. With this space, given the integer  $M$ , for each  $i \in [L - 1]$ , we define  $\Omega_i^M = \Lambda \times [M]$  equipped with the product measure  $P_i^M = P_0 \times \mathrm{Unif}([M])$ , where  $\mathrm{Unif}([M])$  is the uniform measure over the finite set  $[M]$ . We also let  $\Omega_L^M = \{1\}$  and  $P_L^M = \mathbb{I}_{\Omega_L^M}$ . We construct  $\Omega^M = \prod_{i=1}^{L} \Omega_i^M$ , equipped with the product measure  $P^M = \prod_{i=1}^{L} P_i^M$ . The space  $(\Omega^M, P^M)$  gives a canonical neuronal ensemble.

Step 3. Let  $\Omega_i = \Lambda \times \mathbb{N}_{>0}$  and observe  $\Omega_i^M \subset \Omega_i$  for any  $M$ . We define the deterministic functions  $w_1^0: \Omega_1 \to \mathbb{W}_1$ ,  $w_i^0: \Omega_{i-1} \times \Omega_i \to \mathbb{W}_i$  and  $b_i^0: \Omega_i \to \mathbb{B}_i$ , for  $i = 2, \ldots, L$ :

$$
w _ {1} ^ {0} \left(\left(\lambda_ {1}, \theta_ {1}\right)\right) = \mathfrak {p} _ {1} \left(\theta_ {1}\right) \left(\lambda_ {1}\right), \tag {4}
$$

$$
w _ {i} ^ {0} \left(\left(\lambda_ {i - 1}, \theta_ {i - 1}\right), \left(\lambda_ {i}, \theta_ {i}\right)\right) = \mathfrak {q} _ {i} \left(\theta_ {i - 1}, \theta_ {i}\right) (\lambda_ {i}), \quad i = 2, \dots , L - 1, \tag {5}
$$

$$
w _ {L} ^ {0} \left(\left(\lambda_ {L - 1}, \theta_ {L - 1}\right), 1\right) = \mathfrak {q} _ {L} \left(\theta_ {L - 1}, 1\right) \left(\lambda_ {L - 1}\right), \tag {6}
$$

$$
b _ {i} ^ {0} \left(\left(\lambda_ {i}, \theta_ {i}\right)\right) = \mathfrak {p} _ {i} \left(\theta_ {i}\right) (\lambda_ {i}), \quad i = 2, \dots , L - 1, \tag {7}
$$

$$
b _ {L} ^ {0} (1) = \mathfrak {p} _ {L} (1). \tag {8}
$$

These functions, together with  $(\Omega^M,P^M)$ , give a canonical neuronal embedding. Per Section 2.2, given this neuronal embedding, one obtains a canonical  $MF$  limit  $W^{M}(t) = \{w_{1}^{M}(t,\cdot),w_{i}^{M}(t,\cdot ,\cdot),b_{i}^{M}(t,\cdot),i = 2,\dots,L\}$ , defined on  $(\Omega^{M},P^{M})$ , with initialization  $W^{M}(0) = \{w_{1}^{0},w_{i}^{0},b_{i}^{0}:i = 2,\dots,L\}$ . With  $(C_1,\ldots ,C_L)\sim P^M$ , one observes that

$$
\mathrm {L a w} \left(w _ {1} ^ {0} (C _ {1}), w _ {2} ^ {0} (C _ {1}, C _ {2}), b _ {2} ^ {0} (C _ {2}),..., w _ {L} ^ {0} (C _ {L - 1}, 1), b _ {L} ^ {0} (1)\right) = \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {1} \times \prod_ {i = 2} ^ {L} \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {i} \times \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i}.
$$

We also consider the sampling rule  $\overline{P}_{\mathbf{n}}^{M}$  defined for each  $\mathbf{n} = (n_{1},\dots,n_{L})$  with  $n_i\leq M$  for  $i\in [L - 1]$  and  $n_L = 1$  by independently sampling  $\{C_i(j_i)\}_{j_i\in [n_i]}$  from  $(P_i^M)^{n_i}$  conditioned on that  $\{\theta_i(j_i)\}_{j_i\in [n_i]}$  are all distinct, where  $C_i(j_i) = (\lambda_i(j_i),\theta_i(j_i))$ , for each  $i\in [L]$ .

The constructed embedding indeed gives a valid neuronal embedding for neural networks of sizes at most  $M$ .

Proposition 26. For  $\mathbf{n} = \{n_1,\dots,n_L\}$  with  $n_i\leq M$  and  $n_{L} = 1$ , the space  $(\Omega^{M},P^{M})$  together with the functions  $\left(\left\{w_i^0\right\}_{i\in [L]},\left\{b_i^0\right\}_{2\leq i\leq L}\right)$  form a neuronal embedding for the neural network of size  $\mathbf{n}$  under  $\left(\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{1},\dots,\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{L},\rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{2},\dots,\rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{L}\right)$ -i.i.d. initialization, in which the associated sampling rule is  $\overline{P}_{\mathbf{n}}^{M}$ . Furthermore  $\overline{P}_{\mathbf{n}}^{M}$  is  $(2n_{\mathrm{max}} / M)$ -independent, where  $n_{\mathrm{max}} = \max (n_1,\dots,n_L)$ .

The proof of the proposition is deferred to Appendix D. This result, together with Theorem 15, suggests that for large  $M$ , the canonical MF limit tracks closely the trajectory of an i.i.d.-initialized neural network, as long as its (large) size is much smaller than  $M$ . Equivalently, an i.i.d.-initialized large neural network can be closely tracked by any canonical MF limit with sufficiently large  $M$ . This motivates the studies of the canonical MF limits in the limit  $M \to \infty$ , which display simplified structures.

# 5.1.2 Infinite-  $M$  limit of canonical MF limits

Recall that the space  $(\Omega^M, P^M)$  depends on  $M$  and only gives an embedding of networks whose widths are at most  $M$ . More specifically, while the space  $(\Lambda, P_0)$  is independent of  $M$  and  $\Omega^M$  can be extended to infinite  $M$ , the measure  $P^M$  would become an improper probability measure for infinite  $M$ . Nevertheless one can still define a dynamics that is independent of  $M$ .

Let  $W^{*}(t) = \{w_{1}^{*}(t,\cdot),w_{i}^{*}(t,\cdot),b_{i}^{*}(t,\cdot),i = 2,\dots,L\}$  be a dynamics to be described shortly, which we shall prove to be the "infinite-  $M$ " limit of  $W^{M}$ . The full description is lengthy and is deferred to Appendix D.1; let us give a snapshot description for  $L\geq 5$  and  $i = 3,\ldots ,L - 2$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {i} ^ {*} (t, u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, v _ {i}) = - \xi_ {i} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} [ \Delta_ {i} ^ {w *} (t, Z, u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, v _ {i}) ], \\ \frac {\partial}{\partial t} b _ {i} ^ {*} (t, v _ {i}) = - \xi_ {i} ^ {\mathbf {b}} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {i} ^ {b *} (t, Z) \right], \quad \forall u _ {i} \in \mathrm {s u p p} (\rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {i}), v _ {i} \in \mathrm {s u p p} (\rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i}), \\ \end{array}
$$

with the initialization  $w_{i}^{*}(0,u_{i},\cdot ,\cdot) = u_{i}$  and  $b_{i}^{*}(0,v_{i}) = v_{i}$ . Here the quantities are defined by:

$$
H _ {i} ^ {*} (t, x, v _ {i}) = \int \phi_ {i} \left(w _ {i} ^ {*} (t, u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, v _ {i}), b _ {i} ^ {*} (t, v _ {i}), H _ {i - 1} ^ {*} (t, x, v _ {i - 1})\right) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {i} (d u _ {i}) \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i - 1} (d v _ {i - 1}),
$$

$$
\Delta_ {i} ^ {w *} (t, z, u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, v _ {i}) = \sigma_ {i} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (\Delta_ {i} ^ {H *} (t, z, v _ {i}), w _ {i} ^ {*} (t, u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, v _ {i}), b _ {i} ^ {*} (t, v _ {i}), H _ {i} ^ {*} (t, x, v _ {i}), H _ {i - 1} ^ {*} (t, x, v _ {i - 1})),
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \Delta_ {i} ^ {b *} (t, z, v _ {i}) = \int \sigma_ {i} ^ {\mathbf {b}} \left(\Delta_ {i} ^ {H *} (t, z, v _ {i}), w _ {i} ^ {*} (t, u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, v _ {i}), b _ {i} ^ {*} (t, v _ {i}), H _ {i} ^ {*} (t, x, v _ {i}), H _ {i - 1} ^ {*} (t, x, v _ {i - 1})\right) \\ \times \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {i} (d u _ {i}) \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i - 1} (d v _ {i - 1}), \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \Delta_ {i - 1} ^ {H *} (t, z, v _ {i - 1}) = \int \sigma_ {i - 1} ^ {\mathbf {H}} (\Delta_ {i} ^ {H *} (t, z, v _ {i}), w _ {i} ^ {*} (t, u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, v _ {i}), b _ {i} ^ {*} (t, v _ {i}), H _ {i} ^ {*} (t, x, v _ {i}), H _ {i - 1} ^ {*} (t, x, v _ {i - 1})) \\ \times \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {i} (d u _ {i}) \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i} (d v _ {i}). \\ \end{array}
$$

The existence and uniqueness of such dynamics follow similarly to the proof of Theorem 7. We state the main result of this section, which shows that the dynamics  $W^{*}$  is the infinite- $M$  limit of  $W^{M}$ . (Again we refer to Appendix D.1, specifically Theorem 54, for the complete statement of this theorem.)

Theorem 27 (Snapshot statement). Given  $\left(\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{1}, \ldots, \rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{L}, \rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{2}, \ldots, \rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{L}\right)$  and an integer  $M$ , construct the canonical neuronal ensemble  $\left(\Omega^{M}, P^{M}\right)$ , the random variables  $(C_{1}, \ldots, C_{L}) \sim P^{M} = \prod_{i=1}^{L} P_{i}^{M}$  and the canonical MF limit  $W^{M}$  as described in Section 5.1.1. Also construct the dynamics  $W^{*}$  described in Section 5.1.2. Define the following:

$$
\begin{array}{l} w _ {i} ^ {\infty} \left(t, c _ {i - 1}, c _ {i}\right) = w _ {i} ^ {*} \left(t, w _ {i} ^ {0} \left(c _ {i - 1}, c _ {i}\right), b _ {i - 1} ^ {0} \left(c _ {i - 1}\right), b _ {i} ^ {0} \left(c _ {i}\right)\right), \\ b _ {i} ^ {\infty} \left(t, c _ {i}\right) = b _ {i} ^ {*} \left(t, b _ {i} ^ {0} \left(c _ {i}\right)\right), \quad \forall c _ {i} \in \Omega_ {i} = \Lambda \times \mathbb {N} _ {> 0}, \quad i = 3, \dots , L - 2. \\ \end{array}
$$

We also let  $W^{\infty}(t) = \{w_{1}^{\infty}(t,\cdot),w_{i}^{\infty}(t,\cdot ,\cdot),b_{i}^{\infty}(t,\cdot),i = 2,\dots,L\}$ . Let us consider:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\langle W ^ {M} - W ^ {\infty} \right\rangle_ {t} = \max \left(\max _ {1 \leq i \leq L} \left\langle w _ {i} ^ {M} - w _ {i} ^ {\infty} \right\rangle_ {t}, \max _ {2 \leq i \leq L} \left\langle b _ {i} ^ {M} - b _ {i} ^ {\infty} \right\rangle_ {t}\right), \\ \left\langle w _ {i} ^ {M} - w _ {i} ^ {\infty} \right\rangle_ {t} = \mathbb {E} \left[ \left| w _ {i} ^ {M} (t, C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}) - w _ {i} ^ {\infty} (t, C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}) \right| ^ {2} \right] ^ {1 / 2}, \\ \left\langle b _ {i} ^ {M} - b _ {i} ^ {\infty} \right\rangle_ {t} = \mathbb {E} \left[ \left| b _ {i} ^ {M} (t, C _ {i}) - b _ {i} ^ {\infty} (t, C _ {i}) \right| ^ {2} \right] ^ {1 / 2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Then under Assumptions 1-3 and 5, for any  $T \geq 0$  and  $L \geq 2$ ,

$$
\sup  _ {t \leq T} \left\langle W ^ {M} - W ^ {\infty} \right\rangle_ {t} \leq \frac {K _ {T , L}}{M ^ {0 . 4 9 9}},
$$

for sufficiently large  $M = M(T, L)$ , where  $K_{T,L}$  is a constant that depends on  $T$  and  $L$ . Furthermore, for  $L \geq 4$  and  $2 \leq i \leq L - 2$ ,

$$
\sup  _ {t \leq T} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left| H _ {i} \left(X, C _ {i}; W ^ {M} (t)\right) - H _ {i} ^ {*} \left(t, X, b _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i}\right)\right) \right| ^ {2} \right] ^ {1 / 2} \leq \frac {K _ {T , L}}{M ^ {0 . 4 9 9}}.
$$

We give a sketch of the proof in Section 5.2. We now discuss the implications of Theorem 27, and in particular, the simplifying properties induced by i.i.d. initializations. The complete proofs of this theorem and its corollaries are deferred to Appendix D.

Tracking i.i.d.-initialized neural nets via  $W^{*}$ . For large  $M$ , the canonical MF limit  $W^{M}$  is well approximated by  $W^{*}$  (and equivalently by  $W^{\infty}$  as defined in Theorem 27), while we recall from Theorem 15 that  $W^{M}$  tracks closely the trajectory  $\mathbf{W}$  of a large-width i.i.d.-initialized neural network. As such, viewing the bridge through  $W^{M}$  as an intermediate step and taking  $M \to \infty$ , one can track  $\mathbf{W}$  via  $W^{*}$ . To be precise, by combining Proposition 26 and Corollary 17 with Theorem 27, we immediately obtain the following result.

Corollary 28. Under Assumptions 1-3 and for a set of probability measures  $\left(\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{1}, \ldots, \rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{L}, \rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{2}, \ldots, \rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{L}\right)$  such that

$$
\max  _ {1 \leq i \leq L} \sup  _ {m \geq 1} \frac {1}{\sqrt {m}} \left(\int | u | ^ {m} \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {i} (d u)\right) ^ {1 / m} \leq K, \quad \max  _ {2 \leq i \leq L} \sup  _ {m \geq 1} \frac {1}{\sqrt {m}} \left(\int | v | ^ {m} \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i} (d v)\right) ^ {1 / m} \leq K,
$$

there exist constants  $c_{1}\in (0,0.5)$  and  $c_{2}\in (0,1 / 52)$  such that the following statements hold.

Consider any positive integer  $L \geq 2$  and a tuple of positive integers  $\mathbf{n} = \{n_1, \dots, n_L\}$  with  $n_{L=1} = n_{\min} = \min_{1 \leq j \leq L-1} n_j$  and  $n_{\max} = \max_{1 \leq j \leq L} n_j$ . Consider a neural network (1) of size  $\mathbf{n}$  under  $(\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^1, \dots, \rho_{\mathbf{w}}^L, \rho_{\mathbf{b}}^2, \dots, \rho_{\mathbf{b}}^L)$ -i.i.d. initialization, and let  $\mathbf{W}$  be its trajectory. Also construct the dynamics  $W^*$ , as well as the associated quantities, described in Section 5.1.2. Then for any  $\delta > 0$  and  $T \in \epsilon \mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}$ , there exist  $n^* = n^* (T, L, c_1, c_2) \geq 1$  and  $\epsilon^* = \epsilon^* (T, L, c_1, c_2) \leq 1$  such that if  $n_{\min} \geq n^*$  and the learning rate  $\epsilon \in (0, \epsilon^*)$ , for  $3 \leq i \leq L-2$ , for any  $K$ -Lipschitz and  $K$ -bounded test function  $\psi: \mathbb{H}_i \to \mathbb{S}$  (where  $\mathbb{S}$  is a separable Hilbert space), for any  $\delta > 0$ , we have with probability at least  $1 - 3\delta - KLn_{\max} c_2 \exp(-Kn_{\min}^{c_2})$ ,

$$
\sup  _ {t \leq T} \left| \frac {1}{n _ {i}} \sum_ {j _ {i} = 1} ^ {n _ {i}} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \psi \left(\mathbf {H} _ {i} \left(\lfloor t / \epsilon \rfloor , X, j _ {i}\right)\right) \right] - \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \int \psi \left(H _ {i} ^ {*} (t, X, v)\right) \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i} (d v) \right] \right| = \tilde {O} \left(n _ {\min } ^ {- c _ {1}} + \epsilon^ {c _ {1}}\right),
$$

where  $\tilde{O}$  hides the dependency on  $T$ ,  $L$  and  $\delta$  as well as the logarithmic factors  $\log n_{\mathrm{max}}$  and  $\log (1 / \epsilon)$ . A similar statement holds for  $i = 1,2,L - 1,L$ . In addition, for any test function  $\psi :\mathbb{Y}\times \hat{\mathbb{Y}}\to \mathbb{S}$  which is  $K$ -Lipschitz in the second variable, uniformly in the first variable,

$$
\sup  _ {t \leq T} | \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \psi \left(Y, \hat {\mathbf {y}} \left(\lfloor t / \epsilon \rfloor , X\right)\right) \right] - \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \psi \left(Y, \hat {y} ^ {*} (t, X)\right) \right] | = \tilde {O} \left(n _ {\min } ^ {- c _ {1}} + \epsilon^ {c _ {1}}\right),
$$

with probability at least  $1 - 2\delta - KLn_{\max} \exp \left(-Kn_{\min}^{c_2}\right)$ .

Degeneracy of the dynamics. By looking closely at  $W^{*}$ , we observe a simplifying property. By Theorem 27, under i.i.d. initialization, for each intermediate layer  $i = 3,\dots ,L - 2$ , the weight  $w_{i}^{\infty}(t,C_{i - 1},C_{i})$  is a function of only the time  $t$ , its own initialization  $w_{i}^{0}(C_{i - 1},C_{i})$  and the initializations of the adjacent biases  $b_{i - 1}^{0}(C_{i - 1})$  and  $b_{i}^{0}(C_{i})$ , and the bias  $b_{i}^{\infty}(t,C_i)$  is a function of only the time  $t$  and its own initialization  $b_{i}^{0}(C_{i})$ . When we further assume constant initial biases (i.e.  $b_{i}^{0}(C_{i}) = B_{i}$  a constant almost surely for all  $i\geq 2$ ),  $w_{i}^{\infty}(t,C_{i - 1},C_{i})$  is a function of only the time  $t$  and its own initialization, and  $b_{i}^{\infty}(t,C_{i})$  is almost surely only a function of time  $t$ , regardless of  $C_i$ . Consequently, in this scenario, because the initialization is independent across layers, the weights of intermediate layers remain mutually independent at all time, for depth  $L\geq 5$ , in the infinite-width limit.

The theorem in fact further asserts that degeneracy can already be observed for  $L \geq 4$ . In particular, for  $2 \leq i \leq L - 2$ , if the initial bias  $b_{i}^{0}(\cdot) = B_{i}$  is a constant, then

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \left| H _ {i} (X, C _ {i}; W ^ {M} (t)) - H _ {i} ^ {*} (t, X, B _ {i}) \right| ^ {2} \right] ^ {1 / 2} \leq \frac {K _ {T , L}}{M ^ {0 . 4 9 9}}.
$$

Note that  $H_{i}^{*}(t,X,B_{i})$  is independent of  $C_i$ . This suggests that at any training time  $t$ , the neurons of each intermediate layer  $i$  compute the same function of the data input  $x\mapsto H_{i}^{*}(t,x,B_{i})$  in the infinite-width limit. This is formalized directly for the neural network  $\mathbf{W}$  in the following.

Corollary 29. Consider the same setting as Corollary 28 with  $L \geq 4$ . For  $2 \leq i \leq L - 2$ , supposing that  $b_i^0 (C_i) = B_i$  a constant almost surely, then we have for any  $t \leq T$ , with probability at least  $1 - 3\delta - KLn_{\max} \exp \left(-Kn_{\min}^{c_2}\right)$ ,

$$
\left(\frac {1}{n _ {i}} \sum_ {j _ {i} = 1} ^ {n _ {i}} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ | \mathbf {H} _ {i} (\lfloor t / \epsilon \rfloor , X, j _ {i}) - H _ {i} ^ {*} (t, X, B _ {i}) | ^ {2} \right]\right) ^ {1 / 2} = \tilde {\cal O} \left(n _ {\mathrm {m i n}} ^ {- c _ {1}} + \epsilon^ {c _ {1}}\right).
$$

Thus, by Markov's inequality, if one is to pick at random a neuron  $j_{i} \in [n_{i}]$  at layer  $i$  from the neural network  $\mathbf{W}$  at the training step  $\lfloor t / \epsilon \rfloor$ , for  $2 \leq i \leq L - 2$ , then with high probability, this neuron would compute the function  $x \mapsto H_{i}^{*}(t,x,B_{i})$  which is independent of the index  $j_{i}$ .

Collapse to effectively one parameter per layer. Further consideration to standard neural network architectures reveals a stronger simplifying property. The next consequence of Theorem 27 is that with i.i.d. initialization and constant initial biases, for each intermediate layer  $i = 3,\dots,L - 2$ , the weight  $w_{i}^{\infty}(t,c_{i - 1},c_{i})$  translates by a quantity that is independent of  $c_{i - 1}$  and  $c_{i}$ , provided that  $\sigma_{i}^{\mathbf{w}}$  satisfies a certain condition. This condition holds for unregularized standard fully-connected or convolutional neural networks (see Examples 3 and 4). Therefore, for these networks, in the infinite-width limit, with i.i.d. initialization and constant initial biases, the dynamics of the weight at each intermediate layer reduces to a single deterministic translation parameter.

Corollary 30. Under the same setting as Theorem 27 with  $L \geq 5$ , assume that  $b_i^0 (C_i) = B_i$  a constant almost surely for all  $i \geq 2$ . Further assume that for each  $i \in \{3,\dots ,L - 2\}$ , there exists a function  $\bar{\sigma}_i^{\mathbf{w}}$  that satisfies

$$
\sigma_ {i} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (\Delta , w, b, g, h) = \bar {\sigma} _ {i} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (\Delta , b, g, h),
$$

i.e.  $\sigma_{i}^{\mathbf{w}}$  does not depend on the second variable. Then there are differentiable functions  $w_{i}^{\#}(t)$  such that for  $3\leq i\leq L - 2$ , almost surely, for any  $t\geq 0$

$$
w _ {i} ^ {\infty} (t, C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}) - w _ {i} ^ {\infty} (0, C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}) = w _ {i} ^ {\#} (t).
$$

# 5.2 Proof sketch of Theorem 27

Sketch of proof for Theorem 27. We use  $K_{T,L}$  to denote a generic constant that depends on  $T$  and  $L$  and may change from line to line. The main argument exploits the construction in Section 5.1.1 of the canonical neuronal embedding in a suitable way. To illustrate the idea, consider

$$
D _ {i} (t) = \mathbb {E} \left[ \left| H _ {i} (X, C _ {i}; W ^ {\infty} (t)) - H _ {i} ^ {*} (t, X, b _ {i} ^ {0} (C _ {i})) \right| ^ {2} \right].
$$

We aim to show that for  $t \leq T$ ,

$$
D _ {i} (t) \leq \frac {K _ {T , L}}{M}.
$$

For brevity, define

$$
g \left(u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, v _ {i}\right) = \phi_ {i} \left(w _ {i} ^ {*} \left(t, u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, v _ {i}\right), b _ {i} ^ {*} (t, v _ {i}), H _ {i - 1} ^ {*} \left(t, x, v _ {i - 1}\right)\right).
$$

We recall  $w_{i}^{0}(C_{i - 1},C_{i}) = \mathfrak{q}_{i}(\theta_{i - 1},\theta_{i})(\lambda_{i})$ ,  $b_{i}^{0}(C_{i}) = \mathfrak{p}_{i}(\theta_{i})(\lambda_{i})$  and  $b_{i - 1}^{0}(C_{i - 1}) = \mathfrak{p}_{i - 1}(\theta_{i - 1})(\lambda_{i - 1})$  from the construction (4)-(8). To make use of canonical neuronal embedding's construction, we consider a

decomposition of the following squared quantity:

$\stackrel{(a)}{=} \mathbb{E}_{\theta_{i-1}, \lambda_{i-1}, \theta_{i-1}', \lambda_{i-1}', \theta_i, \lambda_i} \left[ \langle g(\mathfrak{q}_i(\theta_{i-1}, \theta_i)(\lambda_i), \mathfrak{p}_{i-1}(\theta_{i-1})(\lambda_{i-1}), \mathfrak{p}_i(\theta_i)(\lambda_i) \rangle, \right.$

$\stackrel{(b)}{=} \mathbb{E}_{\theta_{i-1}, \theta_{i-1}'} \left[ \mathbb{I}_{\theta_{i-1} = \theta_{i-1}'} \int \langle g(u_i, v_{i-1}, v_i), g(u_i, v_{i-1}', v_i) \rangle \rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{i-1}(dv_{i-1}) \rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{i-1}(dv_{i-1}') \rho_{\mathbf{b}}^i(dv_i) \rho_{\mathbf{w}}^i(du_i) \right]$

=  $\frac{1}{M}\int \left\langle g(u_i,v_{i - 1},v_i),g(u_i,v_{i - 1}^{\prime},v_i)\right\rangle \rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{i - 1}(dv_{i - 1})\rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{i - 1}(dv_{i - 1}^{\prime})\rho_{\mathbf{b}}^{i}(dv_i)\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{i}(du_i)$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left. \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i}} \left[ \left| \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i - 1}} \left[ g \left(w _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}\right), b _ {i - 1} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i - 1}\right), b _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i}\right)\right) \right] \right| ^ {2} \right] \right. \\ \left. \left. g \left(\mathfrak {q} _ {i} \left(\theta_ {i - 1} ^ {\prime}, \theta_ {i}\right) (\lambda_ {i}), \mathfrak {p} _ {i - 1} \left(\theta_ {i - 1} ^ {\prime}\right) \left(\lambda_ {i - 1} ^ {\prime}\right), \mathfrak {p} _ {i} \left(\theta_ {i}\right) (\lambda_ {i})\right) \right\rangle \right] \\ \left. + \mathbb {I} _ {\theta_ {i - 1} \neq \theta_ {i - 1} ^ {\prime}} \int \left\langle g \left(u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, v _ {i}\right), g \left(u _ {i} ^ {\prime}, v _ {i - 1} ^ {\prime}, v _ {i}\right) \right\rangle \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i - 1} \left(d v _ {i - 1}\right) \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i - 1} \left(d v _ {i - 1} ^ {\prime}\right) \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i} \left(d v _ {i}\right) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {i} \left(d u _ {i}\right) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {i} \left(d u _ {i} ^ {\prime}\right) \right] \\ + \frac {M - 1}{M} \int \left| \int g (u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, v _ {i}) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {i} (d u _ {i}) \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i - 1} (d v _ {i - 1}) \right| ^ {2} \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i} (d v _ {i}), \\ \end{array}
$$

where in step  $(a)$ ,  $\left(\theta_{i-1}^{\prime}, \lambda_{i-1}^{\prime}\right) \sim \mathrm{Unif}\left([M]\right) \times P_0$  is an independent copy of  $(\theta_{i-1}, \lambda_{i-1})$  and is independent of  $(\theta_i, \lambda_i)$ , and step  $(b)$  is by the construction of  $\mathfrak{p}_{i-1}$ ,  $\mathfrak{p}_i$  and  $\mathfrak{q}_i$ . We also notice that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i}} \left[ \left\langle \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i - 1}} \left[ g \left(w _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}\right), b _ {i - 1} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i - 1}\right), b _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i}\right)\right) \right], \int g \left(u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, b _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i}\right)\right) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {i} \left(d u _ {i}\right) \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i - 1} \left(d v _ {i - 1}\right) \right\rangle \right] \\ = \int \left| \int g (u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, v _ {i}) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {i} (d u _ {i}) \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i - 1} (d v _ {i - 1}) \right| ^ {2} \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i} (d v _ {i}). \\ \end{array}
$$

Putting the last two displays together, one easily arrives at the following:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i}} \left[ \left| \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i - 1}} \left[ g \left(w _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}\right), b _ {i - 1} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i - 1}\right), b _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i}\right)\right) \right] - H _ {i} ^ {*} (t, X, b _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i}\right)) \right| ^ {2} \right] \\ = \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i}} \left[ \left| \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i - 1}} \left[ g \left(w _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}\right), b _ {i - 1} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i - 1}\right), b _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i}\right)\right) \right] - \int g \left(u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, b _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i}\right)\right) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {i} \left(d u _ {i}\right) \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i - 1} \left(d v _ {i - 1}\right) \right| ^ {2} \right] \\ \leq \frac {K}{M} \int | g (u _ {i}, v _ {i - 1}, v _ {i}) | ^ {2} \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {i} (d u _ {i}) \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i - 1} (d v _ {i - 1}) \rho_ {\mathbf {b}} ^ {i} (d v _ {i}) \\ \leq \frac {K _ {T , L}}{M}. \\ \end{array}
$$

This illustrates the main use of the canonical neuronal embedding's construction. Now from Assumption 2, one can show that:

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \left| \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i - 1}} \left[ g \left(w _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}\right), b _ {i - 1} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i - 1}\right), b _ {i} ^ {0} \left(C _ {i}\right)\right) \right] - H _ {i} \left(X, C _ {i}; W ^ {\infty} (t)\right) \right| ^ {2} \right] \leq K _ {T, L} D _ {i - 1} (t).
$$

Therefore,

$$
D _ {i} (t) \leq \frac {K _ {T , L}}{M} + K _ {T, L} D _ {i - 1} (t).
$$

One arrives at the claim from this relation.

The rest of the proof involves similar estimates and Gronwall's inequality. Let us quickly describe the steps for completeness. Similar to the above argument, for

$$
D _ {i} ^ {H} (t) = \mathbb {E} \left[ \left| \Delta_ {i} ^ {H} (Z, C _ {i}; W ^ {\infty} (t)) - \Delta_ {i} ^ {H *} (t, Z, b _ {i} ^ {0} (C _ {i})) \right| ^ {2} \right],
$$

we can show that for  $t \leq T$ ,

$$
D _ {i} ^ {H} (t) \leq K _ {T, L} \frac {\log^ {1 / 2} M}{M}.
$$

With the previous two claims, one easily shows:

$$
D _ {i} ^ {w} (t) \leq K _ {T, L} \frac {\log^ {1 / 2} M}{M}, \qquad D _ {i} ^ {b} (t) \leq K _ {T, L} \frac {\log^ {1 / 2} M}{M},
$$

where we define

$$
\begin{array}{l} D _ {i} ^ {w} (t) = \mathbb {E} \left[ \left| \Delta_ {i} ^ {w} (Z, C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}; W ^ {\infty} (t)) - \Delta_ {i} ^ {w *} (t, Z, w _ {i} ^ {0} (C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}), b _ {i - 1} ^ {0} (C _ {i - 1}), b _ {i} ^ {0} (C _ {i})) \right| ^ {2} \right], \\ D _ {i} ^ {b} (t) = \mathbb {E} \left[ \left| \Delta_ {i} ^ {b} (Z, C _ {i}; W ^ {\infty} (t)) - \Delta_ {i} ^ {b *} (t, Z, b _ {i} ^ {0} (C _ {i})) \right| ^ {2} \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

The next step is to show that for  $2 \leq i \leq L$ , any  $t \leq T$  and any  $B \geq 0$ ,

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \left| \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {i} ^ {w} (Z, C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}; W ^ {M} (t)) - \Delta_ {i} ^ {w} (Z, C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}; W ^ {\infty} (t)) \right] \right| ^ {2} \right] ^ {1 / 2} \leq K _ {T, L} \left((1 + B) \left\langle W ^ {M} - W ^ {\infty} \right\rangle_ {t} + e ^ {- K B ^ {2}}\right).
$$

With this, we then arrive at the following:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left| \Delta_ {i} ^ {w} (Z, C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}; W ^ {M} (t)) - \Delta_ {i} ^ {w *} (t, Z, w _ {i} ^ {0} (C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}), b _ {i - 1} ^ {0} (C _ {i - 1}), b _ {i} ^ {0} (C _ {i})) \right| ^ {2} \right] ^ {1 / 2} \\ \leq \left| D _ {i} ^ {w} (t) \right| ^ {1 / 2} + \mathbb {E} \left[ \left| \Delta_ {i} ^ {w} (Z, C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}; W ^ {M} (t)) - \Delta_ {i} ^ {w} (Z, C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}; W ^ {\infty} (t)) \right| ^ {2} \right] ^ {1 / 2} \\ \leq K _ {T, L} \left(\frac {\log^ {1 / 4} M}{M ^ {1 / 2}} + (1 + B) \left\langle W ^ {M} - W ^ {\infty} \right\rangle_ {t} + e ^ {- K B ^ {2}}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

A similar result holds for  $\Delta_i^b$ . Hence, we obtain that for all  $t \leq T$

$$
\left\langle W ^ {M} - W ^ {\infty} \right\rangle_ {t} \leq K _ {T, L} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \left(\frac {\log^ {1 / 4} M}{M ^ {1 / 2}} + (1 + B) \left\langle W ^ {M} - W ^ {\infty} \right\rangle_ {t} + e ^ {- K B ^ {2}}\right) d s.
$$

Since  $\left\langle W^{M} - W^{\infty}\right\rangle_{0} = 0$ , Gronwall's inequality implies that

$$
\sup  _ {t \leq T} \left\langle W ^ {M} - W ^ {\infty} \right\rangle_ {t} \leq K _ {T, L} \inf  _ {B > 0} \left[ \left(\frac {\log^ {1 / 4} M}{M ^ {1 / 2}} + e ^ {- K B ^ {2}}\right) e ^ {K _ {T, L} (1 + B)} \right] \leq K _ {T, L} \frac {1}{M ^ {0 . 4 9 9}},
$$

for sufficiently large  $M$ . This proves the main statement in Theorem 27; the other statement follows easily.

# 6 Convergence to Global Optimum: Two-layer and Three-layer Networks with I.i.d. Initialization

In this section, we prove several global convergence guarantees for fully-connected neural networks (without biases) with  $L \leq 3$  and i.i.d. initializations. A key element here is a certain universal approximation property that holds at any finite training time. This is shown using a tool from algebraic topology.

# 6.1 Warm-up: The case  $L = 2$

Our first result is that in the case of two-layer fully-connected neural networks, the MF limit converges to the global optimum under some genericity assumptions on the initialization distribution. Before we proceed, we specify the two-layer network under consideration and its training:

$$
\hat {\mathbf {y}} (k, x) = \varphi_ {2} \left(\mathbf {H} _ {2} (k, x, 1)\right), \quad \mathbf {H} _ {2} (k, x, 1) = \frac {1}{n _ {1}} \sum_ {j _ {1} = 1} ^ {n _ {1}} \mathbf {w} _ {2} (k, j _ {1}, 1) \varphi_ {1} (\langle \mathbf {w} _ {1} (k, j _ {1}), x \rangle), \tag {9}
$$

in which  $\mathbf{w}_1(k,j_1)\in \mathbb{R}^d$ ,  $x\in \mathbb{R}^d$ ,  $\varphi_{1}:\mathbb{R}\to \mathbb{R}$ ,  $\mathbf{w}_2(k,j_1,1)\in \mathbb{R}$  and  $\varphi_{2}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow \mathbb{R}$ . We train the network with SGD w.r.t. the loss  $\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}\times \mathbb{R}\to \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$  and the data  $z(k) = (x(k),y(k))$  drawn independently at time  $k$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {w} _ {2} \left(k + 1, j _ {1}, 1\right) = \mathbf {w} _ {2} \left(k, j _ {1}, 1\right) - \epsilon \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} \left(y \left(k\right), \hat {\mathbf {y}} \left(t, x \left(k\right)\right)\right) \varphi_ {2} ^ {\prime} \left(\mathbf {H} _ {2} \left(t, x \left(k\right), 1\right)\right) \varphi_ {1} \left(\mathbf {w} _ {1} \left(k, j _ {1}\right), x \left(k\right)\right), \\ \mathbf {w} _ {1} (k + 1, j _ {1}) = \mathbf {w} _ {1} (k, j _ {1}) - \epsilon \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (y (k), \hat {\mathbf {y}} (t, x (k))) \varphi_ {2} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {H} _ {2} (t, x (k), 1)) \mathbf {w} _ {2} (k, j _ {1}, 1) \varphi_ {1} ^ {\prime} (\langle \mathbf {w} _ {1} (k, j _ {1}), x (k) \rangle) x (k). \\ \end{array}
$$

Here  $\epsilon \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$  is the learning rate. The corresponding MF ODEs are:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {2} (t, c _ {1}, 1) = - \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} (t, X)) \varphi_ {2} ^ {\prime} (H _ {2} (t, X, 1)) \varphi_ {1} (w _ {1} (t, c _ {1}), X) \right], \\ \frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {1} (t, c _ {1}) = - \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} (t, X)) \varphi_ {2} ^ {\prime} (H _ {2} (t, X, 1)) w _ {2} (t, c _ {1}, 1) \varphi_ {1} ^ {\prime} (\langle w _ {1} (t, c _ {1}), X \rangle) X \right], \\ \end{array}
$$

in which for  $f_{1}:\Omega_{1}\to \mathbb{R}^{d}$  and  $f_{2}:\Omega_{1}\rightarrow \mathbb{R}$ , we define:

$$
\hat {y} \left(x; f _ {1}, f _ {2}\right) = \varphi_ {2} \left(H _ {2} \left(x; f _ {1}, f _ {2}\right)\right), \quad H _ {2} \left(x; f _ {1}, f _ {2}\right) = \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {1}} \left[ f _ {2} \left(C _ {1}\right) \varphi_ {1} \left(\langle f _ {1} \left(C _ {1}\right), x \rangle\right) \right],
$$

and  $\hat{y}(t,x)$  and  $H_{2}(t,x,1)$  are short-hands notations when  $f_{1} = w_{1}(t,\cdot)$ ,  $f_{2} = w_{2}(t,\cdot,1)$ . It is easy to see that this network fits into our framework. In particular, under the coupling procedure in Section 4.1, our framework allows to study the following initialization scheme:

$$
\left. \right.\left\{\left(\mathbf {w} _ {1} (0, j _ {1}), \mathbf {w} _ {2} (0, j _ {1}, 1)\right)\right\} _ {j _ {1} \in [ n _ {1} ]} \sim \rho^ {0} \text {i . i . d .}
$$

for suitable probability measure  $\rho^0$  over  $\mathbb{R}^d\times \mathbb{R}$ . In this case,  $\rho^0 = \mathrm{Law}\left(w_1(0,C_1),w_2(0,C_1,1)\right)$ . To measure the training quality, we consider the population loss:

$$
\mathcal {L} \left(f _ {1}, f _ {2}\right) = \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \hat {y} \left(X; f _ {1}, f _ {2}\right)\right) \right].
$$

Assumption 6. Consider the MF limit corresponding to the network (9), such that they are coupled together by the coupling procedure in Section 4.1. We consider the following assumptions:

1. Initialization: The initialization law  $\rho^0$  satisfies

$$
\max \left(\sup _ {m \geq 1} \frac {1}{\sqrt {m}} \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {1}} [ | w _ {1} (0, C _ {1}) | ^ {m} ] ^ {1 / m}, \sup _ {m \geq 1} \frac {1}{\sqrt {m}} \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {1}} [ | w _ {2} (0, C _ {1}, 1) | ^ {m} ] ^ {1 / m}\right) \leq K.
$$

2. Diversity: The support of  $\rho^0$  contains the graph of a continuous function  $F:\mathbb{R}^d\to \mathbb{R}$  such that  $|F(u)|\leq K$  for all  $u\in \mathbb{R}^d$  
3. Regularity:  $\varphi_{1}$  is  $K$ -bounded,  $\varphi_{1}^{\prime}$  and  $\varphi_{2}^{\prime}$  are  $K$ -bounded and  $K$ -Lipschitz,  $\varphi_{2}^{\prime}$  is non-zero everywhere,  $\partial_{2}\mathcal{L}(\cdot ,\cdot)$  is  $K$ -Lipschitz in the second variable and  $K$ -bounded<sup>3</sup>, and  $|X|\leq K$  with probability 1.

4. Convergence: There exist limits  $\bar{w}_1$  and  $\bar{w}_2$  such that as  $t\to \infty$ , there exists a coupling  $\pi_t$  of  $P_{1}$  and itself such that

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {\pi_ {t}} \left[ | \bar {w} _ {2} (C _ {1}) | | w _ {1} (t, C _ {1} ^ {\prime}) - \bar {w} _ {1} (C _ {1}) | + | w _ {2} (t, C _ {1} ^ {\prime}, 1) - \bar {w} _ {2} (C _ {1}) | \right]\rightarrow 0
$$

for  $(C_1,C_1^{\prime})\sim \pi_t$  . Furthermore,ess-  $\sup_{t}\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial t} w_2(t,C_1,1)\right|\to 0.$

5. Universal approximation:  $\{\varphi_1(\langle u,\cdot \rangle):u\in \mathbb{R}^d\}$  has dense span in  $L^2 (\mathcal{P}_X)$  (the space of square integrable functions w.r.t. the measure  $\mathcal{P}_X$ , which is the distribution of the input  $X$ ).

Note that if  $(w_{1}(t,C_{1}),w_{2}(t,C_{1},1))$  converges to  $(\bar{w}_1(C_1),\bar{w}_2(C_1))$  in the Wasserstein-2 distance as  $t\to \infty$ , then one can prove the first part of the convergence condition in Assumption 6 via the initialization and regularity conditions and Lemma 8.

We state the main result.

Theorem 31. Consider the MF limit corresponding to the network (9), such that they are coupled together by the coupling procedure in Section 4.1. Under Assumption 6, the following hold:

- Case 1 (convex loss): If  $\mathcal{L}$  is convex in the second variable, then:

$$
\lim _ {t \to \infty} \mathcal {L} (W (t)) = \inf _ {f _ {1}, f _ {2}} \mathcal {L} (f _ {1}, f _ {2}) = \inf _ {\tilde {y}} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} [ \mathcal {L} (Y, \tilde {y} (X)) ].
$$

- Case 2 (generic non-negative loss): Suppose  $\partial_2\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$  implies  $\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$ . If  $y = y(x)$  a function of  $x$ , then  $\mathcal{L}(W(t)) = 0$  as  $t \to \infty$ .

The proof is deferred to Appendix E. We refer the readers to Section 6.2.1 where we present a high-level proof plan for the three-layer case, which is also applicable to the present two-layer case. The following result is straightforward from Theorem 31 and Corollary 17.

Corollary 32. Consider the neural network (9). Under the same setting as Theorem 31, in Case 1,

$$
\lim  _ {t \rightarrow \infty} \lim  _ {n _ {1} \rightarrow \infty} \lim  _ {\epsilon \rightarrow 0} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \hat {\mathbf {y}} \left(\lfloor t / \epsilon \rfloor , X\right)\right)\right] = \inf  _ {f _ {1}, f _ {2}} \mathcal {L} \left(f _ {1}, f _ {2}\right) = \inf  _ {\tilde {y}} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \tilde {y} \left(X\right)\right)\right]
$$

in probability, and in Case 2, the same holds with the right-hand side being 0.

Let us make a remark on the setting. Examples of suitable  $\varphi_{1}$  include sigmoid/tanh activation, sinusoids and Gaussian pdf, whose universal approximation is known [Cyb89, CC95] (where we assume the convention that the last entry of the data input  $x$  is 1). Examples of suitable  $\varphi_{2}$  include smoothened leaky-ReLU, sigmoid/tanh and linear activation. Examples of suitable (and convex) loss  $\mathcal{L}$  include Huber loss and exponential loss. Importantly  $\mathcal{L}$  needs not be convex. Assumption 6.4 is technical and does not seem removable. Note that this assumption specifies the mode of convergence and is not an assumption on the limits  $\bar{w}_{1}$  and  $\bar{w}_{2}$ . In particular, the first condition (convergence in moment) of in Assumption 6.4 is a common assumption in the literature [CB18b]. See also Section 9 where we further this discussion in the context of prior works.

Regarding the uniform convergence condition ess-sup  $\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial t} w_2(t,C_1,1)\right| \to 0$  in Assumption 6.4, there is a converse relation between global convergence and this condition. Thus this uniform convergence condition gives a sharp characterization of global convergence.

Proposition 33. Consider the MF limit corresponding to the network (9), such that they are coupled together by the coupling procedure in Section 4.1. Suppose that the initialization and regularity assumptions (i.e. the first and third assumptions) of Assumption 6 hold, and that  $\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y})\to \infty$  as  $|\hat{y} |\rightarrow \infty$  for each  $y$ . Then the following hold:

- Case 1 (convex loss): If  $\mathcal{L}$  is convex in the second variable and  $\mathcal{L}(W(t)) \to \inf_F \mathcal{L}(F)$  as  $t \to \infty$ , then it must be that

$$
\sup  _ {c _ {1} \in \Omega_ {1}} \left| \frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {2} (t, c _ {1}, 1) \right| \to 0 \quad a s   t \to \infty .
$$

- Case 2 (generic non-negative loss): Suppose that  $\partial_2\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$  implies  $\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$ , and  $y = y(x)$  is a function of  $x$ . If  $\mathcal{L}(W(t)) \to 0$  as  $t \to \infty$ , then the same conclusion also holds.

Such a converse result was shown in the work [Woj20] for two-layer neural networks. It is also a special case of Proposition 39, which is a similar converse for multilayer networks; hence we shall omit the proof of Proposition 33.

# 6.2 The case  $L = 3$

We now turn to the case of three-layer networks,  $L = 3$ . Our development here applies insights already seen in the case  $L = 2$ , most notably the universal approximation property at the first layer and the topology argument. Our present case is complicated by the presence of a third layer, which requires extra conditions to ensure the same proof technique to be applicable. We again stress that, similar to the case  $L = 2$ , here we do not rely critically on any convexity property, and the same proof of global convergence should extend beyond the specific network architecture to be considered here (the network (10) below).

Before we proceed, we specify the three-layer network under consideration and its training. We also follow the development for i.i.d. initialization in Section 5; in particular, we work with the infinite- $M$  MF limit.

Three-layer network. For  $\mathbf{n} = \{n_1, n_2\}$ , we consider the following neural network:

$$
\hat {\mathbf {y}} (k, x) = \varphi_ {3} \left(\mathbf {H} _ {3} (k, x, 1)\right), \tag {10}
$$

$$
\mathbf {H} _ {3} (k, x, 1) = \frac {1}{n _ {2}} \sum_ {j _ {2} = 1} ^ {n _ {2}} \mathbf {w} _ {3} (k, j _ {2}, 1) \varphi_ {2} (\mathbf {H} _ {2} (k, x, j _ {2})),
$$

$$
\mathbf {H} _ {2} (k, x, j _ {2}) = \frac {1}{n _ {1}} \sum_ {j _ {1} = 1} ^ {n _ {1}} \mathbf {w} _ {2} (k, j _ {1}, j _ {2}) \varphi_ {1} (\langle \mathbf {w} _ {1} (k, j _ {1}), x \rangle),
$$

in which  $x\in \mathbb{R}^d$ $\mathbf{w}_1(k,j_1)\in \mathbb{R}^d$ $\mathbf{w}_2(k,j_1,j_2)\in \mathbb{R}$ $\mathbf{w}_3(k,j_2,1)\in \mathbb{R}$ $\varphi_{1}:\mathbb{R}\to \mathbb{R},\varphi_{2}:\mathbb{R}\to \mathbb{R},$ $\varphi_{3}:\mathbb{R}\to \mathbb{R}$  and  $k\in \mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}$  indicating the discrete time. We train the network with SGD w.r.t. the loss  $\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}\times \mathbb{R}\rightarrow \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$  and the data  $z(k) = (x(k),y(k))$  drawn independently at time  $k$ :

$$
\mathbf {w} _ {3} \left(k + 1, j _ {2}, 1\right) = \mathbf {w} _ {3} \left(k, j _ {2}, 1\right) - \epsilon \xi_ {3} \left(k \epsilon\right) \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} \left(y (k), \hat {\mathbf {y}} \left(k, x (k)\right)\right) \varphi_ {3} ^ {\prime} \left(\mathbf {H} _ {3} \left(k, x (k), 1\right)\right) \varphi_ {2} \left(\mathbf {H} _ {2} \left(k, x (k), j _ {2}\right)\right),
$$

$$
\mathbf {w} _ {2} (k + 1, j _ {1}, j _ {2}) = \mathbf {w} _ {2} (k, j _ {1}, j _ {2}) - \epsilon \Delta_ {2} ^ {\mathbf {H}} (k, z (k), j _ {2}) \varphi_ {1} (\langle \mathbf {w} _ {1} (k, j _ {1}), x \rangle),
$$

$$
\mathbf {w} _ {1} (k + 1, j _ {1}) = \mathbf {w} _ {1} (k, j _ {1}) - \epsilon \left(\frac {1}{n _ {2}} \sum_ {j _ {2} = 1} ^ {n _ {2}} \Delta_ {2} ^ {\mathbf {H}} (k, z (k), j _ {2}) \mathbf {w} _ {2} (k, j _ {1}, j _ {2})\right) \varphi_ {1} ^ {\prime} (\langle \mathbf {w} _ {1} (k, j _ {1}), x (k) \rangle) x (k),
$$

in which

$$
\Delta_ {2} ^ {\mathbf {H}} (k, z, j _ {2}) = \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (y, \hat {\mathbf {y}} (k, x)) \varphi_ {3} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {H} _ {3} (k, x, 1)) \mathbf {w} _ {3} (k, j _ {2}, 1) \varphi_ {2} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {H} _ {2} (k, x, j _ {2})).
$$

Here  $\epsilon \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$  is the learning rate and  $\xi_3: \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0} \mapsto \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$  is the learning rate schedule for the third layer. Note that here we only consider non-negative  $\xi_3$ . We consider  $(\rho^1, \rho^2, \rho^3)$ -i.i.d. initialization:

$$
\{\mathbf {w} _ {1} (0, j _ {1}) \} _ {j _ {1} \in [ n _ {1} ]} \sim \rho^ {1} \text {i . i . d .}, \quad \{\mathbf {w} _ {2} (0, j _ {1}, j _ {2}) \} _ {j _ {1} \in [ n _ {1} ], j _ {2} \in [ n _ {2} ]} \sim \rho^ {2} \text {i . i . d .}, \quad \{\mathbf {w} _ {3} (0, j _ {2}, 1) \} _ {j _ {2} \in [ n _ {2} ]} \sim \rho^ {3} \text {i . i . d .}.
$$

all independently.

Infinite-  $M$  MF limit. Following Section 5.1.2, in the current context of the three-layer neural network (10), we define the dynamics  $w_{1}^{*}$ ,  $w_{2}^{*}$  and  $w_{3}^{*}$  as follows:

$$
\begin{array}{l} w _ {3} ^ {*} (t, u _ {3}) = u _ {3} - \int_ {0} ^ {t} \xi_ {3} (s) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \hat {y} ^ {*} (s, X)\right) \varphi_ {3} ^ {\prime} \left(H _ {3} ^ {*} (s, X)\right) \varphi_ {2} \left(H _ {2} ^ {*} (s, X, u _ {3})\right) \right] d s, \\ w _ {2} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}) = u _ {2} - \int_ {0} ^ {t} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (s, Z, u _ {3}) \varphi_ {1} \left(\langle w _ {1} ^ {*} (s, u _ {1}), X \rangle\right) \right] d s, \\ w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}) = u _ {1} - \int_ {0} ^ {t} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \left(\int \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (s, Z, u _ {3}) w _ {2} ^ {*} (s, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}) \rho^ {2} (d u _ {2}) \rho^ {3} (d u _ {3})\right) \varphi_ {1} ^ {\prime} (\langle w _ {1} ^ {*} (s, u _ {1}), X \rangle) X \right] d s, \\ \forall u _ {i} \in \operatorname {s u p p} \left(\rho^ {i}\right) \text {f o r} i = 1, 2, 3, \\ \end{array}
$$

in which

$$
\hat {y} ^ {*} \left(x; f _ {1}, f _ {2}, f _ {3}\right) = \varphi_ {3} \left(H _ {3} ^ {*} \left(x; f _ {1}, f _ {2}, f _ {3}\right)\right),
$$

$$
H _ {3} ^ {*} \left(x; f _ {1}, f _ {2}, f _ {3}\right) = \int f _ {3} \left(u _ {3}\right) \varphi_ {2} \left(H _ {2} ^ {*} \left(x, u _ {3}; f _ {1}, f _ {2}\right)\right) \rho^ {3} \left(d u _ {3}\right),
$$

$$
H _ {2} ^ {*} \left(x, u _ {3}; f _ {1}, f _ {2}\right) = \int f _ {2} \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}\right) \varphi_ {1} \left(\left\langle f _ {1} \left(u _ {1}\right), x \right\rangle\right) \rho^ {1} \left(d u _ {1}\right) \rho^ {2} \left(d u _ {2}\right),
$$

$$
\Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} \left(z, u _ {3}; f _ {1}, f _ {2}, f _ {3}\right) = \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} \left(y, \hat {y} ^ {*} \left(x; f _ {1}, f _ {2}, f _ {3}\right)\right) \varphi_ {3} ^ {\prime} \left(H _ {3} ^ {*} \left(x; f _ {1}, f _ {2}, f _ {3}\right)\right) f _ {3} \left(u _ {3}\right) \varphi_ {2} ^ {\prime} \left(H _ {2} ^ {*} \left(x, u _ {3}; f _ {1}, f _ {2}\right)\right),
$$

and  $\hat{y}^{*}(t,x),H_{3}^{*}(t,x),H_{2}^{*}(t,x,u_{3})$  and  $\Delta_2^{H*}(t,z,u_3)$  are their short-hand notations for when  $f_{1} = w_{1}^{*}(t,\cdot)$ $f_{2} = w_{2}^{*}(t,\cdot ,\cdot ,\cdot)$  and  $f_{3} = w_{3}^{*}(t,\cdot)$ . Let us also define  $W^{*}(t) = \{w_{1}^{*}(t,\cdot),w_{2}^{*}(t,\cdot ,\cdot ,\cdot),w_{3}^{*}(t,\cdot)\}$ . To measure the training quality for  $W^{*}(t)$ , we consider the population loss  $\mathcal{L}(W^{*}(t))$  in which

$$
\mathscr {L} \left(f _ {1}, f _ {2}, f _ {3}\right) = \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathscr {L} \left(Y, \hat {y} ^ {*} (X; f _ {1}, f _ {2}, f _ {3})\right) \right].
$$

This gives the infinite-  $M$  MF limit for the neural network (10).

Assumption 7. Consider the infinite-  $M$  MF limit  $W^{*}(t)$  corresponding to the network (10). We consider the following assumptions:

1. Initialization: The functions  $\{w_i^0\}_{i = 1,2,3}$  satisfy

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sup  _ {m \geq 1} \frac {1}{\sqrt {m}} \left(\int | u _ {1} | ^ {m} \rho^ {1} (d u _ {1})\right) ^ {1 / m} \leq K, \quad \sup  _ {m \geq 1} \frac {1}{\sqrt {m}} \left(\int | u _ {2} | ^ {m} \rho^ {2} (d u _ {2})\right) ^ {1 / m} \leq K, \\ \sup  _ {m \geq 1} \frac {1}{\sqrt {m}} \left(\int | u _ {3} | ^ {m} \rho^ {3} (d u _ {3})\right) ^ {1 / m} \leq K. \\ \end{array}
$$

2. Diversity: The support of  $\rho^1$  is  $\mathbb{R}^d$ .  
3. Regularity:  $\varphi_{1}$  and  $\varphi_{2}$  are  $K$ -bounded,  $\varphi_{1}^{\prime}$ ,  $\varphi_{2}^{\prime}$  and  $\varphi_{3}^{\prime}$  are  $K$ -bounded and  $K$ -Lipschitz,  $\varphi_{2}^{\prime}$  and  $\varphi_{3}^{\prime}$  are non-zero everywhere,  $\partial_2\mathcal{L}(\cdot ,\cdot)$  is  $K$ -Lipschitz in the second variable and  $K$ -bounded, and  $|X|\leq K$  with probability 1.  
4. Convergence: There exist functions  $\bar{w}_1$ ,  $\bar{w}_2$  and  $\bar{w}_3$  such that as  $t \to \infty$ , there exists a coupling  $\pi_t$  of  $\rho^1 \times \rho^2 \times \rho^3$  and itself such that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \int \left(1 + | \bar {w} _ {3} (u _ {3}) |\right) | \bar {w} _ {3} (u _ {3}) | | \bar {w} _ {2} (u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}) | \left| w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}) - \bar {w} _ {1} (u _ {1}) \right| d \pi_ {t} (u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}, u _ {2} ^ {\prime}, u _ {3} ^ {\prime}) \rightarrow 0, \\ \int \left(1 + | \bar {w} _ {3} (u _ {3}) |\right) | \bar {w} _ {3} (u _ {3}) | \left| w _ {2} ^ {*} \left(t, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}, u _ {2} ^ {\prime}, u _ {3} ^ {\prime}\right) - \bar {w} _ {2} \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}\right)\right| d \pi_ {t} \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}, u _ {2} ^ {\prime}, u _ {3} ^ {\prime}\right)\rightarrow 0, \\ \int \left(1 + | \bar {w} _ {3} (u _ {3}) |\right)\left| w _ {3} ^ {*} \left(t, u _ {3} ^ {\prime}\right) - \bar {w} _ {3} (u _ {3}) \right| d \pi_ {t} \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}, u _ {2} ^ {\prime}, u _ {3} ^ {\prime}\right)\rightarrow 0. \\ \end{array}
$$

Furthermore,

$$
\operatorname * {e s s - s u p} _ {U _ {i} \sim \rho^ {i}} \left| \frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {2} ^ {*} (t, U _ {1}, U _ {2}, U _ {3}) \right| \to 0.
$$

5. Universal approximation:  $\{\varphi_1(\langle u,\cdot \rangle):u\in \mathbb{R}^d\}$  has dense span in  $L^2 (\mathcal{P}_X)$  (the space of square integrable functions w.r.t. the measure  $\mathcal{P}_X$ , which is the distribution of the input  $X$ ).

As a remark, the first part of the convergence assumption follows from the convergence of the tuple  $(w_1^* (t,\cdot),w_2^* (t,\cdot ,\cdot),w_3^* (t,\cdot))$  to  $(\bar{w}_1,\bar{w}_2,\bar{w}_3)$  in the Wasserstein-4 distance, i.e.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \inf _ {\pi} \int \Big (| w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}) - \bar {w} _ {1} (u _ {1}) | ^ {4} + | w _ {2} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}, u _ {2} ^ {\prime}, u _ {3} ^ {\prime}) - \bar {w} _ {2} (u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}) | ^ {4} \\ \left. + \left| w _ {3} ^ {*} (t, u _ {3} ^ {\prime}) - \bar {w} _ {3} (u _ {3}) \right| ^ {4}\right) d \pi \left(u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}, u _ {2} ^ {\prime}, u _ {3} ^ {\prime}\right)\rightarrow 0, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the infimum is over all couplings  $\pi$  of  $\rho^1\times \rho^2\times \rho^3$  and itself. In particular, one can prove so with the initialization and regularity conditions and Lemma 8.

Theorem 34. Consider the infinite- $M$  MF limit  $W^{*}(t)$  corresponding to the network (10), under Assumption 7. Further assume either:

- (untrained third layer)  $\int \mathbb{I}(u_3 \neq 0) \rho^3(du_3) > 0$  and  $\xi_3(\cdot) = 0$  (and hence  $w_3^*(t, u_3) = u_3$  unchanged at all  $t \geq 0$ ), or  
- (trained third layer)  $\xi_3(\cdot) = 1$  and the initialization satisfies that  $\mathcal{L}\left(w_1^0, w_2^0, w_3^0\right) < \mathbb{E}_Z[\mathcal{L}(Y, \varphi_3(0))]$ . Then the following hold:  
- Case 1 (convex loss): If  $\mathcal{L}$  is convex in the second variable, then:

$$
\lim  _ {t \rightarrow \infty} \mathcal {L} \left(W ^ {*} (t)\right) = \inf  _ {\tilde {y}} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \tilde {y} (X)\right)\right].
$$

- Case 2 (generic non-negative loss): Suppose that  $\partial_2\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$  implies  $\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$ . If  $y = y(x)$  a function of  $x$ , then  $\mathcal{L}(W^{*}(t)) \to 0$  as  $t \to \infty$ .

The proof is deferred to Section 6.3. While global convergence is proven via the infinite- $M$  MF limit  $W^{*}$ , it is easy to adapt the proof to prove the same for the canonical MF limits that are described in Section 5.1.1, giving a statement similar to the two-layer case (Theorem 31). By working with the infinite- $M$  limit, specifically combining Theorem 34 with Corollary 28, we immediately obtain the following result.

Corollary 35. Consider the neural network (10). Under the same setting as Theorem 34, in Case 1,

$$
\lim  _ {t \to \infty} \lim  _ {n _ {1}, n _ {2} \to \infty} \lim  _ {\epsilon \to 0} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \hat {\mathbf {y}} \left(\lfloor t / \epsilon \rfloor , X\right)\right) \right] = \inf  _ {\tilde {y}} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \tilde {y} \left(X\right)\right) \right].
$$

in probability, and in Case 2, the same holds with the right-hand side being 0. Here  $n_1, n_2 \to \infty$  in such a way that  $n_{\mathrm{min}} \to \infty$  and  $n_{\mathrm{min}}^{-c} \log n_{\mathrm{max}} \to 0$  for any  $c > 0$ , with  $n_{\mathrm{min}} = \min \{n_1, n_2\}$  and  $n_{\mathrm{max}} = \max \{n_1, n_2\}$ .

Similar to Section 6.1, here we also have a converse relation between global convergence and the essential supremum condition in Assumption 7.4.

Proposition 36. Consider the infinite-  $M$  MF limit  $W^{*}(t)$  corresponding to the network (10). Suppose that the initialization and regularity assumptions (i.e. the first and third assumptions) of Assumption 7 hold, and that  $\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y})\to \infty$  as  $|\hat{y} |\rightarrow \infty$  for each  $y$ . Further assume that there exists  $\bar{w}_3$  such that as  $t\to \infty$ , there is a coupling  $\pi_t^3$  of  $\rho^3$  and itself such that

$$
\int \left| w _ {3} ^ {*} \left(t, u _ {3} ^ {\prime}\right) - \bar {w} _ {3} (u _ {3}) \right| d \pi_ {t} ^ {3} \left(u _ {3}, u _ {3} ^ {\prime}\right)\rightarrow 0.
$$

Then the following hold:

- Case 1 (convex loss): If  $\mathcal{L}$  is convex in the second variable and

$$
\lim _ {t \to \infty} \mathcal {L} \left(W ^ {*} (t)\right) = \inf _ {\tilde {y}} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \tilde {y} (X)\right) \right],
$$

then it must be that

$$
\sup _ {u _ {1} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {d}, u _ {2} \in \mathrm {s u p p} (\rho^ {2})} \mathbb {E} _ {U _ {3} \sim \rho^ {3}} \left[ \left| \frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {2} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, U _ {3}) \right| \right] \to 0 a s t \to \infty .
$$

- Case 2 (generic non-negative loss): Suppose that  $\partial_2\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$  implies  $\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$ , and  $y = y(x)$  is a function of  $x$ . If  $\mathcal{L}(W^{*}(t)) \to 0$  as  $t \to \infty$ , then the same conclusion also holds.

We prove Proposition 36 in Appendix E.

# 6.2.1 High-level idea of the proof

Before we proceed, we give a high-level discussion of the proof of Theorem 34. This is meant to provide intuitions and explain the technical crux, so our discussion may simplify and deviate from the actual proof. Our first insight is to look at the second layer's weight  $w_{2}^{*}$ . Recall that

$$
\frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {2} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}) = - \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (Z, u _ {3}; W ^ {*} (t)) \varphi_ {1} (\langle w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}), X \rangle) \right].
$$

At convergence time  $t = \infty$ , we expect to have zero movement and hence, denoting  $\bar{W} = \{\bar{w}_1,\bar{w}_2,\bar{w}_3\}$ :

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (Z, u _ {3}; \bar {W}) \varphi_ {1} (\langle \bar {w} _ {1} (u _ {1}), X \rangle) \right] = 0
$$

for  $u_{1} \in \operatorname{supp}\left(\rho^{1}\right)$ ,  $u_{3} \in \operatorname{supp}\left(\rho^{3}\right)$ . Suppose for the moment that we are allowed to make an additional (strong) assumption on the limit  $\bar{w}_{1}$ :  $\operatorname{supp}(\bar{w}_{1}(U_{1})) = \mathbb{R}^{d}$  for  $U_{1} \sim \rho^{1}$ . It implies that the universal approximation property, described in Assumption 7.5, holds at  $t = \infty$ ; more specifically, it implies  $\{\varphi_{1}(\langle \bar{w}_{1}(u_{1}),\cdot \rangle):u_{1} \in \operatorname{supp}\left(\rho^{1}\right)\}$  has dense span in  $L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{X})$ . This thus yields

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} \left(Z, u _ {3}; \bar {W}\right) \big | X = x \right] = 0
$$

for  $\mathcal{P}$ -almost every  $x$ . Recalling the definition of  $\Delta_2^{H*}$ , one can then easily show that

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \hat {y} ^ {*} (x; \bar {W})\right) | X = x \right] = 0.
$$

Global convergence follows immediately; for example, in Case 2 of Theorem 34, this is equivalent to that  $\partial_2\mathcal{L}\left(y(x),\hat{y}^*\left(x;\bar{W}\right)\right) = 0$  and hence  $\mathcal{L}\left(y(x),\hat{y}^*\left(x;\bar{W}\right)\right) = 0$  for  $\mathcal{P}$ -almost every  $x$ . In short, the gradient flow structure of the dynamics of  $w_{2}^{*}$  provides a seamless way to obtain global convergence. Furthermore there is no critical reliance on convexity.

However this plan of attack has a potential flaw in the strong assumption that  $\mathrm{supp}(\bar{w}_1(U_1)) = \mathbb{R}^d$ , i.e. the universal approximation property holds at convergence time.
