Indeed there are setups where it is desirable that  $\mathrm{supp}(\bar{w}_1(U_1)) \neq \mathbb{R}^d$  [MMN18, Chi19]; for instance, it is the case where the neural network is to learn some "sparse and spiky" solution, and hence the weight distribution at convergence time, if successfully trained, cannot have full support. On the other hand, one can entirely expect that if  $\mathrm{supp}(w_1^*(0,U_1)) = \mathbb{R}^d$  initially at  $t = 0$ , then  $\mathrm{supp}(w_1^*(t,U_1)) = \mathbb{R}^d$  at any finite  $t \geq 0$ . The crux of our proof is to show the latter without assuming  $\mathrm{supp}(\bar{w}_1(U_1)) = \mathbb{R}^d$ . This is done via an algebraic topology argument, in which the mapping  $(t,u) \mapsto M(t,u)$  that maps from  $(t,w_1^*(0,u_1)) = (t,u_1)$  to  $w_1^*(t,u_1)$  is shown to preserve a homotopic structure through time.

# 6.3 Proof of Theorem 34

First using an algebraic topology argument, we show that if  $w_1^*(0, U_1) = U_1 \sim \rho^1$  has full support, then so is  $w_1^*(t, U_1)$  at any time  $t$ . Note that the following result holds beyond the setting of Theorem 34.

Lemma 37. Assume  $L = 3$  and  $\mathbb{W}_1 = \mathbb{R}^d$  (for some positive integer  $d$ ), along with Assumptions 1-3 and 5. Under a  $(\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^1, \rho_{\mathbf{w}}^2, \rho_{\mathbf{w}}^3)$ -i.i.d. initialization, consider the infinite-M limit of the canonical MF limits as described in Section 5.1.2, and in particular the dynamics  $\{w_i^*\}_{i=1,2,3}$ . Here we disregard the biases by considering  $\xi_2^{\mathbf{b}}(\cdot) = \xi_3^{\mathbf{b}}(\cdot) = 0$  and  $b_2(0,\cdot) = b_3(0,\cdot) = 0$ . Assume that

$$
\sigma_ {2} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (\Delta , w, b, g, h) = \bar {\sigma} _ {2} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (\Delta , g, h),
$$

for some function  $\bar{\sigma}_2^{\mathbf{w}}$ , i.e.  $\sigma_2^{\mathbf{w}}$  does not depend on the second and third variables. Suppose that the support of  $\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{1}$  is  $\mathbb{W}_1$ . Then for all finite time  $t$ , the support of  $\mathrm{Law}_{U_1\sim \rho_{\mathbf{w}}^1}(w_1^* (t,U_1))$  is  $\mathbb{W}_1$ .

Proof. Specialized to the current setting,  $w_1^*$  and  $w_2^*$  satisfy

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}) = - \xi_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \sigma_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {w}} \left(\int h (t, Z, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {2} (d u _ {2}) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {3} (d u _ {3}), w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}), X\right) \right], \\ \frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {2} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}) = - \xi_ {2} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} [ g (t, Z, u _ {1}, u _ {3}) ], \\ \end{array}
$$

for all  $u_{1} \in \operatorname{supp}\left(\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{1}\right)$ ,  $u_{2} \in \operatorname{supp}\left(\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{2}\right)$  and  $u_{3} \in \operatorname{supp}\left(\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{3}\right)$  where

$$
g \left(t, z, u _ {1}, u _ {3}\right) = \bar {\sigma} _ {2} ^ {\mathbf {w}} \left(\Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} \left(t, z, u _ {3}\right), H _ {2} ^ {*} \left(t, x, u _ {3}\right), H _ {1} ^ {*} \left(t, x, u _ {1}\right)\right),
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} h \left(t, z, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}\right) = \sigma_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {H}} \left(\Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} \left(t, z, u _ {3}\right), w _ {2} ^ {*} \left(t, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}\right), 0, H _ {2} ^ {*} \left(t, x, u _ {3}\right), H _ {1} ^ {*} \left(t, x, u _ {1}\right)\right) \\ = \sigma_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {H}} \left(\Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (t, z, u _ {3}), u _ {2} - \int_ {0} ^ {t} \xi_ {2} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (s) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} [ g (s, Z, u _ {1}, u _ {3}) ] d s, 0, H _ {2} ^ {*} (t, x, u _ {3}), H _ {1} ^ {*} (t, x, u _ {1})\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

Here we have shortened the notations to remove dependency on the biases:

$$
w _ {2} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}) \equiv w _ {2} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}, 0),
$$

$$
\Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} \left(t, z, u _ {3}\right) \equiv \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} \left(t, z, u _ {3}, 0\right),
$$

$$
H _ {2} ^ {*} (t, x, u _ {3}) \equiv H _ {2} ^ {*} (t, x, u _ {3}, 0).
$$

We recall the initialization

$$
w _ {1} ^ {*} (0, u _ {1}) = u _ {1}, \quad w _ {2} ^ {*} (0, \cdot , u _ {2}, \cdot) = u _ {2}, \quad w _ {2} ^ {*} (0, u _ {3}, \cdot) = u _ {3}.
$$

In the following, we define  $K_{t}$  to be a generic constant that changes with  $t$  and is finite with finite  $t$ . We proceed in several steps.

Step 1. We study the function  $h$ . We have from Assumptions 3 and 1:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \Delta_ {3} ^ {w *} (t, z, \cdot , \cdot) \right| \leq K \left(1 + \left| \Delta_ {3} ^ {H *} (t, z) \right|\right) \leq K, \\ \left| w _ {3} ^ {*} (t, u _ {3}, \cdot) \right| \leq \left| w _ {3} ^ {*} (0, u _ {3}, \cdot) \right| + K _ {t} = \left| u _ {3} \right| + K _ {t}, \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\left| \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (t, z, u _ {3}) \right| \leq K \left(1 + \left| \Delta_ {3} ^ {H *} (t, z) \right|\right) \left(1 + | w _ {3} ^ {*} (t, u _ {3}, \cdot) |\right) \leq K _ {t} \left(1 + | u _ {3} |\right).
$$

Consequently by Assumption 3,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| g \left(t, z, u _ {1}, u _ {3}\right) \right| \leq K \left(1 + \left| \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} \left(t, z, u _ {3}\right) \right|\right) \\ \leq K _ {t} \left(1 + | u _ {3} |\right), \\ \left| g \left(t, z, u _ {1}, u _ {3}\right) - g \left(t, z, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}, u _ {3}\right) \right| \leq K \left(1 + \left| \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} \left(t, z, u _ {3}\right) \right|\right) \left| H _ {1} ^ {*} \left(t, x, u _ {1}\right) - H _ {1} ^ {*} \left(t, x, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}\right) \right| \\ \leq K _ {t} \left(1 + | u _ {3} |\right) \left| H _ {1} ^ {*} (t, x, u _ {1}) - H _ {1} ^ {*} \left(t, x, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}\right) \right|, \\ \end{array}
$$

for all  $u_{1} \in \operatorname{supp}\left(\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{1}\right)$  and  $u_{3} \in \operatorname{supp}\left(\rho_{\mathbf{w}}^{3}\right)$ . Using these bounds and Assumption 3, we obtain:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| h \left(t, z, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}\right) - h \left(t, z, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}\right) \right| \\ \leq K \left(1 + \left| \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (t, z, u _ {3}) \right|\right) \int_ {0} ^ {t} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \left| g (s, Z, u _ {1}, u _ {3}) - g (s, Z, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}, u _ {3}) \right| \right] d s \\ + K \left(1 + \left| \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (t, z, u _ {3}) \right|\right) \left(1 + | u _ {2} | + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} [ | g (s, Z, u _ {1}, u _ {3}) | ] d s\right) \left| H _ {1} ^ {*} (t, x, u _ {1}) - H _ {1} ^ {*} (t, x, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}) \right| \\ \leq K _ {t} \left(1 + | u _ {2} | ^ {2} + | u _ {3} | ^ {2}\right) \sup  _ {s <   t} \left| H _ {1} ^ {*} (s, x, u _ {1}) - H _ {1} ^ {*} (s, x, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}) \right|, \\ \end{array}
$$

as well as that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| h \left(t, z, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}\right) \right| \leq K \left(1 + \left| \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} \left(t, z, u _ {3}\right) \right|\right) \left(1 + \left| u _ {2} \right| + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \left| g \left(s, Z, u _ {1}, u _ {3}\right) \right| \right] d s\right) \\ \leq K _ {t} \left(1 + | u _ {2} | ^ {2} + | u _ {3} | ^ {2}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

These are the desired bounds for  $h$ .

Step 2. We show that for an arbitrary  $T \geq 0$ ,  $w_1^* : [0, T] \times \mathbb{W}_1 \to \mathbb{W}_1$  is continuous. Using the bound for the function  $h$  in Step 1 and Assumptions 2-3, for  $u_1, u_1' \in \mathbb{W}_1$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \frac {d}{d t} \left(w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}) - w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1} ^ {\prime})\right) \right| \\ = \left| \xi_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \sigma_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {w}} \left(\int h (t, Z, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {2} (d u _ {2}) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {3} (d u _ {3}), w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}), X\right) \right. \right. \\ \left. \left. - \sigma_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {w}} \left(\int h (t, Z, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {2} (d u _ {2}) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {3} (d u _ {3}), w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}), X\right) \right] \right| \\ \leq K \left| w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}) - w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}) \right| \\ + K _ {t} \left(1 + \int | u _ {2} | ^ {2} \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {2} (d u _ {2}) + \int | u _ {3} | ^ {2} \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {3} (d u _ {3})\right) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \sup  _ {s \leq t} \left| H _ {1} ^ {*} (s, x, u _ {1}) - H _ {1} ^ {*} (s, x, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}) \right| \right] \\ \leq K \left| w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}) - w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}) \right| + K _ {t} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \sup  _ {s \leq t} \left| H _ {1} ^ {*} (s, x, u _ {1}) - H _ {1} ^ {*} (s, x, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}) \right| \right] \\ = K \left| w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1}) - w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}) \right| + K _ {t} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \sup  _ {s \leq t} \left| \phi_ {1} \left(w _ {1} ^ {*} (s, u _ {1}), X\right) - \phi_ {1} \left(w _ {1} ^ {*} (s, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}), X\right) \right| \right] \\ \leq K_{t}\sup_{s\leq t}\left|w_{1}^{*}\left(s,u_{1}\right) - w_{1}^{*}\left(s,u_{1}^{\prime}\right)\right|. \\ \end{array}
$$

By Gronwall's inequality,

$$
\sup  _ {s \leq t} \left| w _ {1} ^ {*} (s, u _ {1}) - w _ {1} ^ {*} (s, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}) \right| \leq e ^ {K _ {t} t} \left| w _ {1} ^ {*} (0, u _ {1}) - w _ {1} ^ {*} (0, u _ {1} ^ {\prime}) \right| = e ^ {K _ {t} t} \left| u _ {1} - u _ {1} ^ {\prime} \right|.
$$

Furthermore, by Assumptions 1 and 3, for  $t' \leq t$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| w _ {1} ^ {*} \left(t, u _ {1}\right) - w _ {1} ^ {*} \left(t ^ {\prime}, u _ {1}\right) \right| \leq \int_ {t ^ {\prime}} ^ {t} \left| \xi_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (s) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \sigma_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {w}} \left(\int h (s, Z, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {2} (d u _ {2}) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {3} (d u _ {3}), w _ {1} ^ {*} (s, u _ {1}), X\right) \right] \right| d s \\ \leq K \int_ {t ^ {\prime}} ^ {t} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ 1 + \int | h (s, Z, u _ {1}, u _ {2}, u _ {3}) | \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {2} (d u _ {2}) \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {3} (d u _ {3}) \right] d s \\ \leq K _ {t} \left(1 + \int | u _ {2} | ^ {2} \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {2} (d u _ {2}) + \int | u _ {3} | ^ {2} \rho_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {3} (d u _ {3})\right) | t - t ^ {\prime} | \\ \leq K _ {t} \left| t - t ^ {\prime} \right|. \\ \end{array}
$$

This shows that  $w_{1}^{*}$  defines a continuous function  $w_{1}^{*}:[0,T]\times \mathbb{W}_{1}\to \mathbb{W}_{1}$ .

Step 3. Consider the sphere  $\mathbb{S}^d$  which is a compactification of  $\mathbb{R}^d$ . We can extend  $w_1^*$  to a function  $M: [0, T] \times \mathbb{S}^d \to \mathbb{S}^d$  fixing the point at infinity, which remains a continuous map since  $|M(t, u_1) - u_1| = |M(t, u_1) - M(0, u_1)| \leq K_T t$ . Let  $M_t: \mathbb{R}^d \to \mathbb{R}^d$  be defined by  $M_t(u_1) = M(t, u_1)$ . We claim that  $M_t$  is surjective for all finite  $t$ . Indeed, if  $M_t$  fails to be surjective for some  $t$ , then for some  $p \in \mathbb{S}^d$ ,  $M_t: \mathbb{S}^d \to \mathbb{S}^d \setminus \{p\} \to \mathbb{S}^d$  is homotopic to the constant map, but  $M$  then gives a homotopy from the identity map  $M_0$  on the sphere to a constant map, which is a contradiction as the sphere  $\mathbb{S}^d$  is not contractible. Hence  $w_1^*(t, \cdot)$  is surjective for all finite  $t$ . Now let  $U_1 \sim \rho_{\mathbf{w}}^1$ , which has full support, and consider  $w_1^*(t, U_1)$ . Let us assume that  $w_1^*(t, U_1)$  does not have full support at some time  $t$ , which implies there is an open ball  $B$  in  $\mathbb{R}^d$  for which  $\mathbb{P}(w_1^*(t, U_1) \in B) = 0$ . Due to surjectivity and continuity of  $u_1 \mapsto w_1^*(t, u_1)$ , there is an open set  $U$  such that  $w_1^*(t, u_1) \in B$  for all  $u_1 \in U$ . Then  $\mathbb{P}(U_1 \in U) = 0$ , contradicting the assumption that  $U_1$  has full support. Therefore  $w_1^*(t, U_1)$  must have full support at all  $t \geq 0$ .

With this lemma, we are ready to prove Theorem 34. We recall the setting of Theorem 34, and in particular, the neural network (10).

Proof of Theorem 34. Let  $U_{i} \sim \rho^{i}$ ,  $i = 1,2,3$  independently. It is easy to check that Assumptions 1-3, as well as the conditions of Lemma 37, hold. Therefore, by Lemma 37, the support of  $\operatorname{Law}\left(w_1^* (t,U_1)\right)$  is  $\mathbb{R}^d$  at all  $t$ . We recall from the convergence assumption the limits  $\bar{w}_1$ ,  $\bar{w}_2$  and  $\bar{w}_3$ , and we shall first prove  $(\bar{w}_1,\bar{w}_2,\bar{w}_3)$  is a global minimizer of  $\mathcal{L}$  in Case 1 and  $\mathcal{L}(\bar{w}_1,\bar{w}_2,\bar{w}_3) = 0$  in Case 2.

By the convergence assumption, we have that for any  $\epsilon > 0$ , there exists  $T(\epsilon)$  such that for all  $t \geq T(\epsilon)$  and almost surely:

$$
\epsilon \geq \left| \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (t, Z, U _ {3}) \varphi_ {1} (\langle w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, U _ {1}), X \rangle) \right] \right| = \left| \left\langle \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (t, Z, U _ {3}) | X = x \right], \varphi_ {1} (\langle w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, U _ {1}), x \rangle) \right\rangle_ {L ^ {2} (\mathcal {P} _ {X})} \right|.
$$

Since  $\operatorname{Law}\left(w_1^* (t,U_1)\right)$  has full support, we obtain that for  $u$  in a dense subset of  $\mathbb{R}^d$ ,

$$
\operatorname {e s s - s u p} \left| \left\langle \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (t, Z, U _ {3}) | X = x \right], \varphi_ {1} (\langle u, x \rangle) \right\rangle_ {L ^ {2} (\mathcal {P} _ {X})} \right| \leq \epsilon .
$$

By continuity of  $u \mapsto \varphi_1(\langle u, \cdot \rangle)$  in  $L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$ , we extend the above to all  $u \in \mathbb{R}^d$ . Recall the couplings  $\pi_t$  in Assumption 7.4, since  $\varphi_1$  is bounded,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} _ {(U _ {3}, U _ {3} ^ {\prime}) \sim \pi_ {t}} \left[ \Big | \big \langle \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \big [ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (t, Z, U _ {3}) - \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (Z, U _ {3} ^ {\prime}; \bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2}, \bar {w} _ {3}) \big | | X = x \big ], \varphi_ {1} (\langle u, x \rangle) \big \rangle_ {L ^ {2} (\mathcal {P} _ {X})} \Big | \right] \\ \leq K \mathbb {E} _ {\pi_ {t}} \left[ \left| \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (t, Z, U _ {3}) - \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} \left(Z, U _ {3} ^ {\prime}; \bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2}, \bar {w} _ {3}\right) \right| \right] \\ \leq K \mathbb {E} _ {\pi_ {t}} \Big [ (1 + | \bar {w} _ {3} (U _ {3}) |) \left(\big | w _ {3} ^ {*} (t, U _ {3} ^ {\prime}) - \bar {w} _ {3} (U _ {3}) \big | + | \bar {w} _ {3} (U _ {3}) | \big | w _ {2} ^ {*} (t, U _ {1} ^ {\prime}, U _ {2} ^ {\prime}, U _ {3} ^ {\prime}) - \bar {w} _ {2} (U _ {1}, U _ {2}, U _ {3}) \big | \right. \\ \left. \left. + | \bar {w} _ {3} (U _ {2}) | | \bar {w} _ {2} (U _ {1}, U _ {2}, U _ {3}) | | w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, U _ {1} ^ {\prime}) - \bar {w} _ {1} (U _ {1}) |\right) \right], \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last step is by the regularity assumption, similar to the calculation in the proof of Theorem 31. Recall that the right-hand side converges to 0 as  $t \to \infty$ . We thus obtain that for all  $u \in \mathbb{R}^d$ ,

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {U _ {3}} \left[ \right.\left| \right.\left\langle \right. \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (Z, U _ {3}; \bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2}, \bar {w} _ {3}) \right.\left. \right\rvert X = x \left. \right], \varphi_ {1} (\langle u, x \rangle) \left. \right\rangle_ {L ^ {2} (\mathcal {P} _ {X})} \left. \right\rvert ] = 0,
$$

which yields that for all  $u\in \mathbb{R}^d$  and almost surely,

$$
\left| \right.\left\langle \right. \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \right. \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} (Z, U _ {3}; \bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2}, \bar {w} _ {3}) \left. \right\rvert X = x \left. \right], \varphi_ {1} (\langle u, x \rangle) \left. \right\rangle_ {L ^ {2} (\mathcal {P} _ {X})} \left. \right| = 0.
$$

Here we note that by the regularity assumption that

$$
\left| \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H *} \left(Z, U _ {3}; \bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2}, \bar {w} _ {3}\right) | X = x \right] \right| \leq K | \bar {w} _ {3} (U _ {3}) |,
$$

and so  $\mathbb{E}_Z\left[\Delta_2^{H^*}(Z,u_3;\bar{w}_1,\bar{w}_2,\bar{w}_3)|X = x\right]$  is in  $L^2 (\mathcal{P}_X)$  for almost every  $u_{3}$ . Since  $\{\varphi_1(\langle u,\cdot \rangle):u\in \mathbb{R}^d\}$  has dense span in  $L^2 (\mathcal{P}_X)$ , we have  $\mathbb{E}_Z\left[\Delta_2^{H^*}(Z,u_3;\bar{w}_1,\bar{w}_2,\bar{w}_3)|X = x\right] = 0$  for  $\mathcal{P}_X$ -almost every  $x$  and almost every  $u_{3}$ , and hence

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \hat {y} ^ {*} (X; \bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2}, \bar {w} _ {3})\right) | X = x \right] \varphi_ {3} ^ {\prime} \left(H _ {3} ^ {*} (x; \bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2}, \bar {w} _ {3})\right) \bar {w} _ {3} (u _ {3}) \varphi_ {2} ^ {\prime} \left(H _ {2} ^ {*} (x, u _ {3}; \bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2})\right) = 0.
$$

We note that our assumptions guarantee that  $\mathbb{P}(\bar{w}_3(U_3)\neq 0)$  is positive. Indeed:

- In the case  $\int \mathbb{I}(u_3 \neq 0) \rho^3 (du_3) > 0$  and  $\xi_3(\cdot) = 0$ , it is obvious that  $\mathbb{P}(\bar{w}_3(U_3) \neq 0) > 0$ .  
- In the case  $\mathcal{L}\left(w_1^0, w_2^0, w_3^0\right) < \mathbb{E}_Z[\mathcal{L}(Y, \varphi_3(0))]$ , it can be easily checked that

$$
\mathcal {L} \left(w _ {1} ^ {*} (t, \cdot), w _ {2} ^ {*} (t, \cdot , \cdot , \cdot), w _ {3} ^ {*} (t, \cdot)\right) \leq \mathcal {L} \left(w _ {1} ^ {*} (t ^ {\prime}, \cdot), w _ {2} ^ {*} (t ^ {\prime}, \cdot , \cdot), w _ {3} ^ {*} (t ^ {\prime}, \cdot)\right),
$$

for  $t \geq t'$ . This is in fact a standard property of gradient flows. In particular, setting  $t' = 0$  and taking  $t \to \infty$ , it is easy to see that

$$
\mathcal {L} \left(\bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2}, \bar {w} _ {3}\right) \leq \mathcal {L} \left(w _ {1} ^ {0}, w _ {2} ^ {0}, w _ {3} ^ {0}\right) <   \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \varphi_ {3} (0)\right) \right].
$$

If  $\mathbb{P}(\bar{w}_3(U_3) = 0) = 1$  then  $\mathcal{L}(\bar{w}_1,\bar{w}_2,\bar{w}_3) = \mathbb{E}_Z[\mathcal{L}(Y,\varphi_3(0))]$ , a contradiction.

Then since  $\varphi_2'$  and  $\varphi_3'$  are strictly non-zero, we have  $\mathbb{E}_Z[\partial_2\mathcal{L}(Y,\hat{y}^* (X;\bar{w}_1,\bar{w}_2,\bar{w}_3))|X = x] = 0$  for  $\mathcal{P}_{X}$ -almost every  $x$ .

In Case 1, since  $\mathcal{L}$  convex in the second variable, for any measurable function  $\tilde{y} (x)$

$$
\mathcal {L} \left(y, \tilde {y} \left(x\right)\right) - \mathcal {L} \left(y, \hat {y} \left(x; \bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2}, \bar {w} _ {3}\right)\right) \geq \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} \left(y, \hat {y} \left(x; \bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2}, \bar {w} _ {3}\right)\right) \left(\tilde {y} \left(x\right) - \hat {y} \left(x; \bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2}, \bar {w} _ {3}\right)\right).
$$

Taking expectation, we get  $\mathbb{E}_Z[\mathcal{L}(Y,\tilde{y} (X))] \geq \mathcal{L}(\bar{w}_1,\bar{w}_2,\bar{w}_3)$ , i.e.  $(\bar{w}_1,\bar{w}_2,\bar{w}_3)$  is a global minimizer of  $\mathcal{L}$ .

In Case 2, since  $y$  is a function of  $x$ , we obtain  $\partial_2\mathcal{L}\left(y,\hat{y}\left(x;\bar{w}_1,\bar{w}_2,\bar{w}_3\right)\right) = 0$  and hence  $\mathcal{L}\left(y,\hat{y}\left(x;\bar{w}_1,\bar{w}_2,\bar{w}_3\right)\right) = 0$  for  $\mathcal{P}_X$ -almost every  $x$ .

Finally to connect  $\mathcal{L}(\bar{w}_1,\bar{w}_2,\bar{w}_3)$  with  $\mathcal{L}(W^{*}(t))$  in the limit  $t\to \infty$ , we have:

$$
\begin{array}{l} | \mathcal {L} (W ^ {*} (t)) - \mathcal {L} (\bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2}, \bar {w} _ {3}) | = | \mathbb {E} _ {Z} [ \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} ^ {*} (t, X)) - \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} ^ {*} (X; \bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2}, \bar {w} _ {3})) ] | \\ \leq K \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \left| \hat {y} ^ {*} (t, X) - \hat {y} ^ {*} (X; \bar {w} _ {1}, \bar {w} _ {2}, \bar {w} _ {3}) \right| \right] \\ \leq K \mathbb {E} _ {\pi_ {t}} \Big [ \left| w _ {3} ^ {*} (t, U _ {3} ^ {\prime}) - \bar {w} _ {3} (U _ {3}) \right| + | \bar {w} _ {3} (U _ {3}) | \left| w _ {2} ^ {*} (t, U _ {1} ^ {\prime}, U _ {2} ^ {\prime}, U _ {3} ^ {\prime}) - \bar {w} _ {2} (U _ {1}, U _ {2}, U _ {3}) \right| \\ \left. \right.\left. + \left| \bar {w} _ {3} \left(U _ {3}\right)\right|\left| \bar {w} _ {2} \left(U _ {1}, U _ {2}, U _ {3}\right)\right|\left| w _ {1} ^ {*} \left(t, U _ {1} ^ {\prime}\right) - \bar {w} _ {1} \left(U _ {1}\right)\right|\right] \\ \end{array}
$$

which tends to 0 as  $t\to \infty$  . This completes the proof.

![](images/520fca3ad033d54d775825cb66c6a04fa4a29246c720aed25593e2d60e346f41.jpg)

# 7 Convergence to Global Optimum: Multilayer Networks with Correlated Initializations

In Section 6, we prove global convergence guarantees for networks with  $L \leq 3$  and i.i.d. initializations. Underlying these results is a universal approximation property that holds throughout the course of training, and this is shown for quite general data distributions. Recall from Section 5 that i.i.d. initializations cause a certain degenerate behavior in the network with  $L \geq 4$ . In particular, by Corollary 29, neurons at intermediate layers collapse to the same function of the input and therefore are not expected to span the space of functions of the input  $L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$ . In other words, these intermediate layers become a bottleneck that hinders universal approximation in the context of more than three layers and general data distributions.

To attain meaningful training, this suggests a departure from i.i.d. initializations. In particular, we propose a correlated initialization scheme that resolves the aforementioned bottleneck problem. To

be precise, the key idea lies in the new concept of bidirectional diversity. A similar concept has been encountered in Section 6; for instance, diversity in the two-layer case in Section 6.1 refers to the full support condition of the first layer's weight distribution in the Euclidean space, implied at initialization  $t = 0$  by Assumption 6.2 and shown to hold at any finite time  $t$  by Lemma 55. Here bidirectional diversity furthers this idea to the multilayer case with arbitrary depths. Firstly, it is realized in function spaces that are naturally described by our neuronal embedding framework. Secondly, it is bidirectional: roughly speaking, for intermediate layers, diversity holds in both the forward and backward passes. The effect of bidirectional diversity is that a certain universal approximation property, at any finite training time  $t$ , is propagated from the first layer to the second last one. Importantly the proposed correlated initialization only ensures bidirectional diversity at initialization  $t = 0$ , but it is the learning dynamics that automatically maintains bidirectional diversity at any finite  $t$ . This fact is again shown by a topological invariance argument.

In the following, we first describe the multilayer fully-connected neural network under consideration and its corresponding MF limit. We then describe the proposed correlated initialization, and state and prove the global convergence guarantee.

# 7.1 Multilayer fully-connected neural network

We consider the following  $L$ -layer fully-connected network:

$$
\hat {\mathbf {y}} (x; \mathbf {W} (k)) = \varphi_ {L} \left(\mathbf {H} _ {L} (x, 1; \mathbf {W} (k))\right), \tag {11}
$$

$$
\mathbf {H} _ {i} \left(x, j _ {i}; \mathbf {W} (k)\right) = \frac {1}{n _ {i - 1}} \sum_ {j _ {i - 1} = 1} ^ {n _ {i - 1}} \mathbf {w} _ {i} \left(k, j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}\right) \varphi_ {i - 1} \left(\mathbf {H} _ {i - 1} \left(x, j _ {i - 1}; \mathbf {W} (k)\right)\right), \qquad i = L, \dots , 2,
$$

$$
\mathbf {H} _ {1} \left(x, j _ {1}; \mathbf {W} (k)\right) = \left\langle \mathbf {w} _ {1} (k, j _ {1}), x \right\rangle ,
$$

in which  $x \in \mathbb{R}^d$  is the input,  $\mathbf{W}(k) = \{\mathbf{w}_1(k,\cdot), \mathbf{w}_i(k,\cdot,\cdot): i = 2,\dots,L\}$  is the weight with  $\mathbf{w}_1(k,j_1) \in \mathbb{R}^d$ ,  $\mathbf{w}_i(k,j_{i-1},j_i) \in \mathbb{R}$ ,  $\varphi_i: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$  is the activation. Here the network has widths  $\{n_i\}_{i \leq L}$  with  $n_L = 1$ . We train the network with stochastic gradient descent (SGD) w.r.t. the loss  $\mathcal{L}: \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$  and the data  $z(k) = (x(k),y(k)) \in \mathbb{R}^d \times \mathbb{R}$  drawn independently at time  $k$  from a training distribution  $\mathcal{P}$ . Given an initialization  $\mathbf{W}(0)$ , we update  $\mathbf{W}(k)$  according to

$$
\mathbf {w} _ {i} (k + 1, j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}) = \mathbf {w} _ {i} (k, j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}) - \epsilon \xi_ {i} (t \epsilon) \Delta_ {i} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (z (k), j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}; \mathbf {W} (k)), \qquad i = 2,..., L,
$$

$$
\mathbf {w} _ {1} (k + 1, j _ {1}) = \mathbf {w} _ {1} (k, j _ {1}) - \epsilon \xi_ {1} (t \epsilon) \Delta_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (z (k), j _ {1}; \mathbf {W} (k)),
$$

in which  $j_{i} \in [n_{i}]$ ,  $\epsilon \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$  is the learning rate,  $\xi_{i}: \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0} \mapsto \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$  is the learning rate schedule for  $\mathbf{w}_i$ , and for  $z = (x,y)$ , we define

$$
\Delta_ {L} ^ {\mathbf {H}} (z, 1; \mathbf {W} (k)) = \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (y, \hat {\mathbf {y}} (x; \mathbf {W} (k))) \varphi_ {L} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {H} _ {L} (x, 1; \mathbf {W} (k))),
$$

$$
\Delta_ {i - 1} ^ {\mathbf {H}} (z, j _ {i - 1}; \mathbf {W} (k)) = \frac {1}{n _ {i}} \sum_ {j _ {i} = 1} ^ {n _ {i}} \Delta_ {i} ^ {\mathbf {H}} (z, j _ {i}; \mathbf {W} (k)) \mathbf {w} _ {i} (k, j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}) \varphi_ {i - 1} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {H} _ {i - 1} (x, j _ {i - 1}; \mathbf {W} (k))), \qquad i = L, \dots , 2,
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \Delta_ {i} ^ {\mathbf {w}} \left(z, j _ {i - 1}, j _ {i}; \mathbf {W} (k)\right) = \Delta_ {i} ^ {\mathbf {H}} \left(z, j _ {i}; \mathbf {W} (k)\right) \varphi_ {i - 1} \left(\mathbf {H} _ {i - 1} \left(x, j _ {i - 1}; \mathbf {W} (k)\right)\right), \qquad i = L,..., 2, \\ \Delta_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {w}} (z, j _ {1}; \mathbf {W} (k)) = \Delta_ {1} ^ {\mathbf {H}} (z, j _ {1}; \mathbf {W} (k)) x. \\ \end{array}
$$

In short, for an initialization  $\mathbf{W}(0)$ , we obtain a SGD trajectory  $\mathbf{W}(k)$  of an  $L$ -layer network with size  $\{n_i\}_{i \leq L}$ . We also note that this neural network fits into the framework in Section 2.

# 7.2 Mean field limit

Given a neuronal ensemble  $(\Omega, P) = \prod_{i=1}^{L} (\Omega_i, P_i)$  (in which  $\Omega_L = \{1\}$ ), the MF limit that is associated with the network (11) is described by the continuous-time evolution of  $W(t) = \{w_1(t, \cdot), w_i(t, \cdot, \cdot): i = 2, \dots, L\}$ ,

given by the following MF ODEs:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {i} \left(t, c _ {i - 1}, c _ {i}\right) = - \xi_ {i} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {i} ^ {w} \left(Z, c _ {i - 1}, c _ {i}; W (t)\right) \right], \qquad i = 2, \dots , L, \\ \frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {1} (t, c _ {1}) = - \xi_ {1} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} [ \Delta_ {1} ^ {w} (Z, c _ {1}; W (t)) ], \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $w_{1}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times \Omega_{1}\to \mathbb{R}^{d}$  and  $w_{i}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times \Omega_{i - 1}\times \Omega_{i}\rightarrow \mathbb{R}$ . Here we define the forward quantities:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \hat {y} (x; W (t)) = \varphi_ {L} \left(H _ {L} (x, 1; W (t))\right), \\ H _ {i} (x, c _ {i}; W (t)) = \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i - 1}} \left[ w _ {i} (t, C _ {i - 1}, c _ {i}) \varphi_ {i - 1} \left(H _ {i - 1} (x, C _ {i - 1}; W (t))\right) \right], \quad i = L,..., 2, \\ H _ {1} \left(x, c _ {1}; W (t)\right) = \left\langle w _ {1} \left(t, c _ {1}\right), x \right\rangle , \\ \end{array}
$$

and the backward quantities:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \Delta_ {L} ^ {H} (z, 1; W (t)) = \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (y, \hat {y} (x; W (t))) \varphi_ {L} ^ {\prime} \left(H _ {L} (x, 1; W (t))\right), \\ \Delta_ {i - 1} ^ {H} (z, c _ {i - 1}; W (t)) = \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i}} \left[ \Delta_ {i} ^ {H} (z, C _ {i}; W (t)) w _ {i} (t, c _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}) \varphi_ {i - 1} ^ {\prime} (H _ {i - 1} (x, c _ {i - 1}; W (t))) \right], \qquad i = L,..., 2, \\ \Delta_ {i} ^ {w} (z, c _ {i - 1}, c _ {i}; W (t)) = \Delta_ {i} ^ {H} (z, c _ {i}; W (t)) \varphi_ {i - 1} \left(H _ {i - 1} (x, c _ {i - 1}; W (t))\right), \qquad i = L,..., 2, \\ \Delta_ {1} ^ {w} (z, c _ {1}; W (t)) = \Delta_ {1} ^ {H} (z, c _ {1}; W (t)) x. \\ \end{array}
$$

As a reminder, the data  $Z = (X,Y) \sim \mathcal{P}$  and  $C_i \sim P_i$ . To recap, given a neuronal ensemble  $(\Omega, P)$ , for each initialization  $W(0)$ , we have defined a MF limit  $W(t)$ .

# 7.3 Global convergence and bidirectional diversity

We begin the study of global convergence of the network (11) with an analysis of its MF limit, which is the focus of this section. To measure the learning quality, we consider the loss averaged over the data  $Z \sim \mathcal{P}$ :

$$
\mathcal {L} (F) = \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \hat {y} (X; F)\right) \right],
$$

where  $F = \{f_{i} : i = 1, \dots, L\}$  a set of measurable functions  $f_{1} : \Omega_{1} \to \mathbb{R}^{d}$ ,  $f_{i} : \Omega_{i-1} \times \Omega_{i} \to \mathbb{R}$  for  $i = 2, \dots, L$ .

Recall that in our framework, the finite-sized neural network is formally connected with its MF limit via a neuronal embedding. Here without making explicit this connection, one can study the MF limit that is defined on the basis of a given neuronal embedding  $\left(\Omega, P, \{w_i^0\}_{i \leq L}\right)$ , where  $w_1^0: \Omega_1 \to \mathbb{R}^d$ ,  $w_i^0: \Omega_{i-1} \times \Omega_i \to \mathbb{R}$  for  $i = 2, \dots, L$ . In particular, we make the following assumptions:

Assumption 8. Consider a neuronal embedding  $\left(\Omega, P, \{w_i^0\}_{i \leq L}\right)$ , recalling  $\Omega = \prod_{i=1}^{L} \Omega_i$  and  $P = \prod_{i=1}^{L} P_i$  with  $\Omega_L = \{1\}$ . Consider the MF limit associated with the neuronal ensemble  $(\Omega, P)$  with initialization  $W(0)$  such that  $w_1(0, \cdot) = w_1^0(\cdot)$  and  $w_i(0, \cdot, \cdot) = w_i^0(\cdot, \cdot)$ . We make the following assumptions:

1. Initialization: The functions  $\{w_i^0\}_{i < L}$  satisfy:

$$
\sup  _ {m \geq 1} \frac {1}{\sqrt {m}} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left| w _ {1} ^ {0} (C _ {1}) \right| ^ {m} \right] ^ {1 / m} \leq K, \quad \sup  _ {m \geq 1} \frac {1}{\sqrt {m}} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left| w _ {i} ^ {0} (C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}) \right| ^ {m} \right] ^ {1 / m} \leq K, \quad i = 2,..., L.
$$

2. Diversity: The functions  $\{w_i^0\}_{i\leq L}$  satisfy:

$\bullet$  supp  $(w_{1}^{0}(C_{1}),w_{2}^{0}(C_{1},\cdot)) = \mathbb{R}^{d}\times L^{2}(P_{2}),$  
- supp  $\left(w_{i}^{0}\left(\cdot ,C_{i}\right),w_{i + 1}^{0}\left(C_{i},\cdot\right)\right) = L^{2}\left(P_{i - 1}\right)\times L^{2}\left(P_{i + 1}\right)$  for  $i = 2,\dots,L - 1$

(Remark: we write  $w_{i}^{0}(\cdot, C_{i})$  to denote the random mapping  $c \mapsto w_{i}^{0}(c, C_{i})$ , and similar for  $w_{i+1}^{0}(C_{i}, \cdot)$ .)

3. Regularity: We assume that:

-  $\varphi_{i}$  is  $K$ -bounded for  $1 \leq i \leq L - 1$ ,  $\varphi_{i}'$  is  $K$ -bounded and  $K$ -Lipschitz for  $1 \leq i \leq L$ , and  $\varphi_{L}'$  is non-zero everywhere,  
-  $\partial_2\mathcal{L}(\cdot ,\cdot)$  is  $K$  -Lipschitz in the second variable and  $K$  -bounded,  
-  $|X| \leq K$  with probability 1,  
- the learning rate schedule  $\xi_{i}$  is  $K$ -bounded and  $K$ -Lipschitz for  $1 \leq i \leq L$ .

4. Convergence: There exist a coupling  $\pi_t$  of  $\prod_{i=1}^{L} P_i$  and itself such that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} _ {\pi_ {t}} \left[ \left| w _ {i} \left(t, C _ {i - 1} ^ {\prime}, C _ {i} ^ {\prime}\right) - \bar {w} _ {i} \left(C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}\right) \right| \prod_ {j = i + 1} ^ {L} | \bar {w} _ {j} \left(C _ {j - 1}, C _ {j}\right) | \right] \to 0, \quad i = 2, \dots , L, \\ \mathbb {E} _ {\pi_ {t}} \left[\left| w _ {1} (t, C _ {1} ^ {\prime}) - \bar {w} _ {1} (C _ {1}) \right| \prod_ {j = 2} ^ {L} | \bar {w} _ {j} (C _ {j - 1}, C _ {j}) | \right]\rightarrow 0, \\ \end{array}
$$

as  $t\to \infty$  , where  $(C_1,\ldots ,C_L,C_1',\ldots ,C_L')\sim \pi_t$  . Furthermore,

$$
\operatorname {e s s - s u p} \left| \frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {L} (t, C _ {L - 1}, 1) \right|\rightarrow 0.
$$

(Here we take  $\prod_{j = i + 1}^{L} = 1$  for  $i = L$ .)

5. Universal approximation: The set  $\{\varphi_1(\langle u,\cdot \rangle):u\in \mathbb{R}^d\}$  has dense span in  $L^2 (\mathcal{P}_X)$  (the space of square integrable functions w.r.t. the measure  $\mathcal{P}_X$ , which is the distribution of the input  $X$ ). Furthermore, for each  $i = 2,\ldots ,L - 1$ ,  $\varphi_{i}$  is non-obstructive in the sense that the set  $\{\varphi_i\circ f:f\in L^2 (\mathcal{P}_X)\}$  has dense span in  $L^2 (\mathcal{P}_X)$ .

It is easy to see that this set of assumptions satisfies Assumptions 1-3 and 5. As a consequence, by Theorem 7, there exists a unique solution  $W$  to the MF ODEs on  $t \in [0,\infty)$ .

Theorem 38. Consider a neuronal embedding  $\left(\Omega, P, \{w_i^0\}_{i \leq L}\right)$  and the MF limit as in Assumption 8. Assume  $\xi_L(\cdot) = 1$ . Then:

- Case 1 (convex loss): If  $\mathcal{L}$  is convex in the second variable, then:

$$
\lim  _ {t \to \infty} \mathcal {L} \left(W (t)\right) = \inf  _ {F} \mathcal {L} (F) = \inf  _ {\tilde {y}: \mathbb {R} ^ {d} \to \mathbb {R}} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \tilde {y} (X)\right) \right].
$$

- Case 2 (generic non-negative loss): Suppose that  $\partial_2\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$  implies  $\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$ . If  $y = y(x)$  is a function of  $x$ , then  $\mathcal{L}(W(t)) \to 0$  as  $t \to \infty$ .

The assumptions here are similar to those made in Theorems 31 and 34 of Section 6. Similar to the settings of Section 6, the regularity assumption can be satisfied for several common setups and loss functions; for example, this holds when  $\varphi_{i}$  is sigmoid or tanh for  $i\leq L - 1$ ,  $\varphi_{L}$  is the identity, and  $\mathcal{L}$  is the Huber loss. The convergence assumption here is also similar to the convergence assumption in Assumption 6 or Assumption 7. In particular, the first part of the convergence assumption is essentially a Wasserstein-type convergence; it follows from the convergence of  $(w_{i}(t))_{i = 1}^{L}$  to  $(\bar{w}_i)_i^L$  in an appropriate Wasserstein distance. The fifth assumption is again natural and can be satisfied by common activations. For example,  $\varphi_{i}$  can be tanh for  $i = 1,\dots,L - 1$ . Indeed, whenever  $\{\varphi_i(\langle u,\cdot \rangle):u\in \mathbb{R}^d\}$  has dense span in  $L^2 (\mathcal{P}_X)$ ,  $\varphi_{i}$  is non-obstructive since

$$
\operatorname {s p a n} \left( \right.\left\{ \right.\varphi_ {i} \left( \right.\langle u, \cdot \rangle : u \in \mathbb {R} ^ {d} \left. \right\}\left. \right) \subseteq \operatorname {s p a n} \left(\left\{\varphi_ {i} \circ f: f \in L ^ {2} \left(\mathcal {P} _ {X}\right)\right\}\right).
$$

The diversity assumption is new: it refers to an initialization scheme that introduces correlation among the weights. In particular, i.i.d. initializations do not satisfy this assumption for  $L \geq 3$ .

The second assumption is the counterpart of the diversity assumption made in Theorems 31 and 34, but there is a special difference. In Section 6, the diversity assumption refers to a full support condition of only the first layer's initial weight, which is in the Euclidean space. Here our diversity assumption refers to a particular full support condition for all layers. At a closer look, the condition is in the function space and reflects certain bidirectional diversity. In particular, this assumption implies both  $w_{i}^{0}(\cdot ,C_{i})$  and  $w_{i}^{0}(C_{i - 1},\cdot)$  have full supports in  $L^2 (P_{i - 1})$  and  $L^2 (P_i)$  respectively (which we shall refer to as forward diversity and backward diversity, respectively).

High-level idea of the proof. The proof proceeds with several insights that have already appeared in Section 6. The novelty of our present analysis lies in the use of the aforementioned bidirectional diversity. To clarify the point, let us give a brief high-level idea of the proof. At time  $t$  sufficiently large, we expect to have:

$$
\left| \frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {L} (t, c _ {L - 1}, 1) \right| = \left| \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} (X; W (t))) \varphi_ {L} ^ {\prime} (H _ {L} (X, 1; W (t))) \varphi_ {L - 1} (H _ {L - 1} (X, c _ {L - 1}; W (t))) \right] \right| \approx 0
$$

for  $P_{L-1}$ -almost every  $c_{L-1}$ . If the set of mappings  $x \mapsto H_{L-1}(x, c_{L-1}; W(t))$ , indexed by  $c_{L-1}$ , is diverse in the sense that  $\operatorname{supp}(H_{L-1}(\cdot, C_{L-1}; W(t))) = L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$ , then since  $\varphi_{L-1}$  is non-obstructive, we obtain

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} (X; W (t))) | X = x \right] \varphi_ {L} ^ {\prime} \left(H _ {L} (x, 1; W (t))\right) \approx 0
$$

and consequently

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} (X; W (t))) | X = x \right] \approx 0
$$

for  $\mathcal{P}_X$ -almost every  $x$ . The desired conclusion then follows.

Hence the crux of the proof is to show that  $\operatorname{supp}(H_{L-1}(\cdot, C_{L-1}; W(t))) = L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$ . In fact, we show that this holds for any finite time  $t \geq 0$ . This follows if we can prove the forward diversity property of the weights, in which  $w_i(t, \cdot, C_i)$  has full support in  $L^2(P_{i-1})$  for any  $t \geq 0$  and  $2 \leq i \leq L-1$ , and a similar property for  $w_1(t, C_1)$ . Interestingly to that end, we actually show that bidirectional diversity, and hence both forward diversity and backward diversity, hold at any time  $t \geq 0$ , even though we only need forward diversity for our purpose. The full proof is deferred to Section 7.5.

A converse for global convergence. Similar to Section 6, we also have a converse relation between global convergence and the essential supremum condition in Assumption 8.4. The proof is presented in Appendix F.

Proposition 39. Consider the MF limit corresponding to the network (11), such that they are coupled together by the coupling procedure in Section 4.1 with a neuronal embedding  $\left(\Omega, P, \{w_i^0\}_{i \leq L}\right)$ . Suppose that the initialization and regularity assumptions (i.e. the first and third assumptions) of Assumption 8 hold, and that  $\mathcal{L}(y, \hat{y}) \to \infty$  as  $|\hat{y}| \to \infty$  for each  $y$ . Further assume  $\xi_L(\cdot) = 1$ . Then the following hold:

- Case 1 (convex loss): If  $\mathcal{L}$  is convex in the second variable and  $\mathcal{L}(W(t)) \to \inf_F \mathcal{L}(F)$  as  $t \to \infty$ , then it must be that

$$
\sup  _ {c _ {L - 1} \in \Omega_ {L - 1}} \left| \frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {L} (t, C _ {L - 1}, 1) \right| \to 0 \quad a s   t \to \infty .
$$

- Case 2 (generic non-negative loss): Suppose that  $\partial_2\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$  implies  $\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$ , and  $y = y(x)$  is a function of  $x$ . If  $\mathcal{L}(W(t)) \to 0$  as  $t \to \infty$ , then the same conclusion also holds.

# 7.4 Connection to the network (11)

Theorem 38 concerns with the global convergence of the MF limit. To make the connection with a finite-width neural network (11), we recall the neuronal embedding  $\left(\Omega, P, \{w_i^0\}_{i \leq L}\right)$ , as well as the coupling procedure in Section 4.1. We however present a twist to the procedure. We first choose the neuronal embedding  $\left(\Omega, P, \{w_i^0\}_{i \leq L}\right)$  and then perform the following two steps:

1. We form the MF limit  $W(t)$  (for  $t \in \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ ) associated with the neuronal ensemble  $(\Omega, P)$  by setting the initialization  $W(0)$  to  $w_{1}(0,\cdot) = w_{1}^{0}(\cdot)$ ,  $w_{i}(0,\cdot,\cdot) = w_{i}^{0}(\cdot,\cdot)$  and running the MF ODEs.  
2. We independently sample  $C_i(j_i) \sim P_i$  for  $i = 1, \dots, L$  and  $j_i = 1, \dots, n_i$ . We then form the neural network initialization  $\mathbf{W}(0)$  with  $\mathbf{w}_1(0, j_1) = w_1^0(C_1(j_1))$  and  $\mathbf{w}_i(0, j_{i-1}, j_i) = w_i^0(C_{i-1}(j_{i-1}), C_i(j_i))$  for  $j_i \in [n_i]$ . We obtain the network's trajectory  $\mathbf{W}(k)$  for  $k \in \mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}$  for the network (11), with the data  $z(k)$  generated independently of  $\{C_i(j_i)\}_{i \leq L}$  and hence  $\mathbf{W}(0)$ .

That is, instead of starting with a given initialization law of  $\mathbf{W}(0)$  as done in Section 4.1, here we first start with a chosen neuronal embedding. We then form the MF limit  $W(t)$  and the neural network initialization  $\mathbf{W}(0)$ , and hence the dynamics  $\mathbf{W}(k)$ , based on this neuronal embedding. In other words, the initialization law of  $\mathbf{W}(0)$  is deduced from the chosen neuronal embedding. Obviously this procedure ensures that  $\bar{\eta}$ -independence is satisfied (Assumption 4).

In summary, in the present context, the neuronal embedding forms the basis on which the finite-width neural network is realized. Furthermore the neural network and its MF limit are coupled. Then using Theorem 38 and Corollary 17, one can obtain the following result on the optimization efficiency of the neural network with SGD:

Corollary 40. Consider the neural network (10) as described by the coupling procedure with the aforementioned twist. Under the same setting as Theorem 38, in Case 1,

$$
\lim  _ {t \rightarrow \infty} \lim  _ {\{n _ {i} \} _ {i \leq L}} \lim  _ {\epsilon \rightarrow 0} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \hat {\mathbf {y}} (X; \mathbf {W} (\lfloor t / \epsilon \rfloor))\right)\right] = \inf  _ {F} \mathcal {L} (F) = \inf  _ {\tilde {y}} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {L} (Y, \tilde {y} (X)) \right]
$$

in probability, where the limit of the widths is such that  $n_{\mathrm{min}} \to \infty$  and  $n_{\mathrm{min}}^{-c} \log n_{\mathrm{max}} \to 0$  for any  $c > 0$ , with  $n_{\mathrm{min}} = \min \left\{n_i : 1 \leq i \leq L - 1\right\}$  and  $n_{\mathrm{max}} = \max \left\{n_i : 1 \leq i \leq L - 1\right\}$ . In Case 2, the same holds with the right-hand side being 0.

# 7.5 Proof of Theorem 38

Proof of Theorem 38. We divide the proof into several steps.

Step 1: Diversity of the weights. We show that  $\mathrm{supp}(w_1(t,C_1)) = \mathbb{R}^d$  and  $\mathrm{supp}(w_i(t,\cdot ,C_i)) = L^2 (P_{i - 1})$  for  $i = 2,\ldots ,L - 1$ , for any  $t\geq 0$ . We do so by showing a stronger statement, that the following bidirectional diversity condition holds at any finite training time:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {s u p p} \left(w _ {1} \left(t, C _ {1}\right), w _ {2} \left(t, C _ {1}, \cdot\right)\right) = \mathbb {R} ^ {d} \times L ^ {2} \left(P _ {2}\right), \\ \operatorname {s u p p} \left(w _ {i} (t, \cdot , C _ {i}), w _ {i + 1} (t, C _ {i}, \cdot)\right) = L ^ {2} \left(P _ {i - 1}\right) \times L ^ {2} \left(P _ {i + 1}\right), \quad i = 2, \dots , L - 1, \\ \end{array}
$$

for any  $t\geq 0$

We prove the first statement. Given a MF trajectory  $(W(t))_{t\geq 0}$  and  $u_{1}\in \mathbb{R}^{d}$ ,  $u_{2}\in L^{2}(P_{2})$ , we consider the following flow on  $\mathbb{R}^d\times L^2 (P_2)$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\partial}{\partial t} a _ {2} ^ {+} (t, c _ {2}; u) = - \xi_ {2} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H} (Z, c _ {2}; W (t)) \varphi_ {1} (\left\langle a _ {1} ^ {+} (t; u), X \right\rangle) \right], \\ \frac {\partial}{\partial t} a _ {1} ^ {+} (t; u) = - \xi_ {1} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {2}} \left[ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H} (Z, C _ {2}; W (t)) a _ {2} ^ {+} (t, C _ {2}; u) \right] \varphi_ {1} ^ {\prime} \left(\left\langle a _ {1} ^ {+} (t; u), X \right\rangle\right) X \right], \tag {12} \\ \end{array}
$$

for  $u = (u_{1}, u_{2})$ , with the initialization  $a_{1}^{+}(0; u) = u_{1}$  and  $a_{2}^{+}(0, c_{2}; u) = u_{2}(c_{2})$ . Existence and uniqueness of  $(a_{1}^{+}, a_{2}^{+})$  follows similarly to Theorem 7. We next prove for all finite  $T > 0$  and  $u^{+} = (u_{1}^{+}, u_{2}^{+}) \in \mathbb{R}^{d} \times L^{2}(P_{2})$ , there exists  $u^{-} = (u_{1}^{-}, u_{2}^{-}) \in \mathbb{R}^{d} \times L^{2}(P_{2})$  such that

$$
a _ {1} ^ {+} \left(T; u ^ {-}\right) = u _ {1} ^ {+}, \qquad a _ {2} ^ {+} \left(T, \cdot ; u ^ {-}\right) = u _ {2} ^ {+}.
$$

We consider the following auxiliary dynamics on  $\mathbb{R}^d\times L^2 (P_2)$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\partial}{\partial t} a _ {2} ^ {-} (t, c _ {2}; u) = \xi_ {2} (T - t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H} (Z, c _ {2}; W (T - t)) \varphi_ {1} (\langle a _ {1} ^ {-} (t; u), X \rangle) \right], \\ \frac {\partial}{\partial t} a _ {1} ^ {-} (t; u) = \xi_ {1} (T - t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {2}} \left[ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H} (Z, C _ {2}; W (T - t)) a _ {2} ^ {-} (t, C _ {2}; u) \right] \varphi_ {1} ^ {\prime} (\langle a _ {1} ^ {-} (t; u), X \rangle) X \right], \tag {13} \\ \end{array}
$$

initialized at  $a_1^- (0;u) = u_1$  and  $a_2^- (0,c_2;u) = u_2(c_2)$ , for  $u = (u_1,u_2)\in \mathbb{R}^d\times L^2 (P_2)$ . Existence and uniqueness of  $(a_1^{-},a_2^{-})$  follow similarly to Theorem 7. Observe that the pair

$$
\tilde {a} _ {1} ^ {-} (t) = a _ {1} ^ {-} \left(T - t; u ^ {+}\right), \qquad \tilde {a} _ {2} ^ {-} (t, c _ {2}) = a _ {2} ^ {-} \left(T - t, c _ {2}; u ^ {+}\right)
$$

solves the system

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\partial}{\partial t} \tilde {a} _ {2} ^ {-} (t, c _ {2}) = - \frac {\partial}{\partial t} a _ {2} ^ {-} (T - t, c _ {2}; u ^ {+}) = - \xi_ {2} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} [ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H} (Z, c _ {2}; W (t)) \varphi_ {1} (\langle \tilde {a} _ {1} ^ {-} (t), X \rangle) ], \\ \frac {\partial}{\partial t} \tilde {a} _ {1} ^ {-} (t) = - \frac {\partial}{\partial t} a _ {1} ^ {-} (T - t; u ^ {+}) = - \xi_ {1} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} [ \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {2}} [ \Delta_ {2} ^ {H} (Z, C _ {2}; W (t)) \tilde {a} _ {2} ^ {-} (t, C _ {2}) ] \varphi_ {1} ^ {\prime} (\langle \tilde {a} _ {1} ^ {-} (t), X \rangle) X ], \\ \end{array}
$$

initialized at  $\tilde{a}_2^-(0, c_2) = a_2^-(T, c_2; u^+)$  and  $\tilde{a}_1^-(0) = a_1^-(T; u^+)$ . Thus, by uniqueness of the solution to the ODE (12),  $(\tilde{a}_1^-, \tilde{a}_2^-)$  forms a solution of the ODE (12) initialized at

$$
\tilde {a} _ {1} ^ {-} (0) = a _ {1} ^ {-} \left(T; u ^ {+}\right), \qquad \tilde {a} _ {2} ^ {-} (0, c _ {2}) = a _ {2} ^ {-} \left(T, c _ {2}; u ^ {+}\right).
$$

In particular, the solution  $(\tilde{a}_1^-, \tilde{a}_2^-)$  of the ODE (12) with this initialization satisfies

$$
\tilde {a} _ {1} ^ {-} (T) = a _ {1} ^ {-} \left(0; u ^ {+}\right) = u _ {1} ^ {+}, \qquad \tilde {a} _ {2} ^ {-} (T, \cdot) = a _ {2} ^ {-} \left(0, \cdot ; u ^ {+}\right) = u _ {2} ^ {+}.
$$

Let  $u_1^- = a_1^- (T;u^+)$  and  $u_2^- = a_2^- (T,\cdot ;u^+)$ . Then we have  $a_1^+ (T;u^-) = u_1^+$  and  $a_2^+ (T,\cdot ;u^-) = u_2^+$  as desired.

Using this, by continuity of the map  $u \mapsto \left(a_1^+ (T;u),a_2^+ (T,\cdot ;u)\right)$ , for every  $\epsilon >0$ , there exists a neighborhood  $U$  of  $u^{-}$  such that for any  $u\in U$ ,  $\big|\big(a_1^+ (T;u),a_2^+ (T,\cdot ;u)\big) - u^{+}\big| \leq \epsilon$ . Notice that the MF trajectory  $W(t)$  satisfies

$$
w _ {1} \left(t, c _ {1}\right) = a _ {1} ^ {+} \left(t; w _ {1} \left(0, c _ {1}\right), w _ {2} \left(0, c _ {1}, \cdot\right)\right), \qquad w _ {2} \left(t, c _ {1}, \cdot\right) = a _ {2} ^ {+} \left(t, \cdot ; w _ {1} \left(0, c _ {1}\right), w _ {2} \left(0, c _ {1}, \cdot\right)\right).
$$

Then since  $(w_{1}(0,C_{1}),w_{2}(0,C_{1},\cdot))$  has full support in  $\mathbb{R}^d\times L^2 (P_2)$ , for any finite  $T > 0$ , we have  $(w_{1}(T,C_{1}),w_{2}(T,C_{1},\cdot))$  has full support in  $\mathbb{R}^d\times L^2 (P_2)$ , proving the first statement.

The other statements can be proven similarly by considering the following pairs of flows on  $L^2(P_{i-1}) \times L^2(P_{i+1})$ , for  $u = (u_1, u_2) \in L^2(P_{i-1}) \times L^2(P_{i+1})$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\partial}{\partial t} a _ {i} ^ {+} (t, c _ {i - 1}; u) = - \xi_ {i} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {i} ^ {a} \left(Z, a _ {i} ^ {+} (t, \therefore ; u), a _ {i + 1} ^ {+} (t, \therefore ; u); W (t)\right) \varphi_ {i - 1} \left(H _ {i - 1} (X, c _ {i - 1}; W (t))\right) \right], \\ \frac {\partial}{\partial t} a _ {i + 1} ^ {+} (t, c _ {i + 1}; u) = - \xi_ {i + 1} (t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {i + 1} ^ {H} (Z, c _ {i + 1}; W (t)) \varphi_ {i} \left(H _ {i} ^ {a} (Z, a _ {i} ^ {+} (t, \cdot ; u); W (t))\right) \right], \\ \end{array}
$$

initialized at  $a_{i}^{+}(0, c_{i-1}; u) = u_{1}(c_{i-1})$  and  $a_{i+1}^{+}(0, c_{i+1}; u) = u_{2}(c_{i+1})$ , and

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\partial}{\partial t} a _ {i} ^ {-} (t, c _ {i - 1}; u) = \xi_ {i} (T - t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {i} ^ {a} (Z, a _ {i} ^ {-} (t, \cdot ; u), a _ {i + 1} ^ {-} (t, \cdot ; u); W (T - t)) \varphi_ {i - 1} (H _ {i - 1} (X, c _ {i - 1}; W (T - t))) \right], \\ \frac {\partial}{\partial t} a _ {i + 1} ^ {-} (t, c _ {i + 1}; u) = \xi_ {i + 1} (T - t) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \Delta_ {i + 1} ^ {H} (Z, c _ {i + 1}; W (T - t)) \varphi_ {i} \left(H _ {i} ^ {a} (Z, a _ {i} ^ {-} (t,.; u); W (T - t))\right) \right], \\ \end{array}
$$

initialized at  $a_{i}^{-}(0, c_{i-1}; u) = u_{1}(c_{i-1})$  and  $a_{i+1}^{-}(0, c_{i+1}; u) = u_{2}(c_{i+1})$ , in which we define:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \Delta_ {i} ^ {a} (z, f, g; W (t)) = \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i + 1}} \left[ \Delta_ {i + 1} ^ {H} (z, C _ {i + 1}; W (t)) g (C _ {i + 1}) \varphi_ {i} ^ {\prime} \left(H _ {i} ^ {a} (z, f; W (t))\right) \right], \\ H _ {i} ^ {a} (z, f; W (t)) = \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i - 1}} [ f (C _ {i - 1}) \varphi_ {i - 1} (H _ {i - 1} (x, C _ {i - 1}; W (t))) ], \\ \end{array}
$$

for  $f \in L^{2}(P_{i - 1})$  and  $g \in L^2 (P_{i + 1})$ .

Step 2: Diversity of the pre-activations. We show that  $\operatorname{supp}\left(H_{i}(\cdot, C_{i}; W(t))\right) = L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{X})$  for any  $t \geq 0$ , for  $i = 2, \ldots, L - 1$  by induction.

Firstly consider the base case  $i = 2$ . Recall that

$$
H _ {2} \left(x, c _ {2}; W (t)\right) = \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {1}} \left[ w _ {2} \left(t, C _ {1}, c _ {2}\right) \varphi_ {1} \left(\langle w _ {1} (t, C _ {1}), x \rangle\right) \right] \equiv \mathcal {H} _ {2} \left(t, x, w _ {2} \left(t, \cdot , c _ {2}\right)\right).
$$

Observe that the set  $\operatorname{cl}\left(\left\{\mathcal{H}_2(t,\cdot,f):f\in L^2(P_1)\right\}\right)$  is a closed linear subspace of  $L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$ . Hence this set is equal to  $L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$  if it has dense span in  $L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$ , which we show now. Indeed, suppose that for some  $g\in L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$  such that  $|g|\neq 0$ , we have  $\mathbb{E}_Z[g(X)\mathcal{H}_2(t,X,f)] = 0$  for all  $f\in L^2(P_1)$ . Equivalently,

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {C _ {1}} \left[ f (C _ {1}) \mathbb {E} _ {Z} [ g (X) \varphi_ {1} (\langle w _ {1} (t, C _ {1}), X \rangle) ] = 0, \right.
$$

for all  $f \in L^{2}(P_{1})$ . As such, for  $P_{1}$ -almost every  $c_{1}$ ,

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ g (X) \varphi_ {1} \left(\langle w _ {1} (t, c _ {1}), X \rangle\right) \right] = 0.
$$

Since  $\operatorname{supp}(w_1(t, C_1)) = \mathbb{R}^d$  and that the mapping  $u \mapsto \varphi_1(\langle u, x \rangle)$  is continuous, by the universal approximation assumption for  $\varphi_1$ , we then obtain  $g(x) = 0$  for  $P_X$ -almost every  $x$ , which is a contradiction. We have thus proved that  $\operatorname{cl}\left(\left\{\mathcal{H}_2(t, \cdot, f): f \in L^2(P_1)\right\}\right) = L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$ . Note that  $f \mapsto \mathcal{H}_2(t, x, f)$  is continuous, and  $\operatorname{supp}(w_2(t, \cdot, C_2)) = L^2(P_1)$ , we then have  $\operatorname{supp}(H_2(\cdot, C_2; W(t))) = L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$  as desired.

Now let us assume that  $\operatorname{supp}(H_{i-1}(\cdot, C_{i-1}; W(t))) = L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$  for some  $i \geq 3$  (the induction hypothesis). We would like to show  $\operatorname{supp}(H_i(\cdot, C_i; W(t))) = L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$ . This is similar to the base case. In particular, recall that

$$
H _ {i} \left(x, c _ {i}; W (t)\right) = \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i - 1}} \left[ w _ {i} \left(t, C _ {i - 1}, c _ {i}\right) \varphi_ {i - 1} \left(H _ {i - 1} \left(x, C _ {i - 1}; W (t)\right)\right) \right] \equiv \mathcal {H} _ {i} \left(t, x, w _ {i} \left(t, \cdot , c _ {i}\right)\right).
$$

Now suppose that for some  $g \in L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$  such that  $|g| \neq 0$ , we have  $\mathbb{E}_Z[g(X) \mathcal{H}_i(t, X, f)] = 0$  for all  $f \in L^2(P_{i-1})$ . Then, for  $P_{i-1}$ -almost every  $c_{i-1}$ ,

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ g \left(X\right) \varphi_ {i - 1} \left(H _ {i - 1} \left(X, c _ {i - 1}; W \left(t\right)\right)\right) \right] = 0.
$$

Recall the induction hypothesis  $\operatorname{supp}(H_{i-1}(\cdot, C_{i-1}; W(t))) = L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$ . Since  $\varphi_{i-1}$  is non-obstructive and continuous, we obtain  $g(x) = 0$  for  $P_X$ -almost every  $x$ , which is a contradiction. Therefore the set  $\operatorname{cl}\left(\{\mathcal{H}_i(t, \cdot, f): f \in L^2(P_{i-1})\}\right)$  has dense span in  $L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$ , and again, this implies it is equal to  $L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$ . Since  $f \mapsto \mathcal{H}_i(t, x, f)$  is continuous and  $\operatorname{supp}(w_i(t, \cdot, C_i)) = L^2(P_{i-1})$ , we have  $\operatorname{supp}(H_i(\cdot, C_i; W(t))) = L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$ .

Step 3: Concluding. Let  $\mathbb{E}_Z[\partial_2\mathcal{L}(Y,\hat{y} (X;W(t)))|X = x]\varphi_L'(H_L(x,1;W(t))) = \mathcal{H}(x,W(t))$ . From the last step, we have  $\operatorname {supp}\left(H_{L - 1}\left(\cdot ,C_{L - 1};W(t)\right)\right) = L^2\left(\mathcal{P}_X\right)$  for any  $t\geq 0$ . Recall that

$$
\frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {L} (t, c _ {L - 1}, 1) = - \mathbb {E} _ {Z} [ \mathcal {H} (X, W (t)) \varphi_ {L - 1} (H _ {L - 1} (X, c _ {L - 1}; W (t))) ].
$$

By the convergence assumption, for any  $\epsilon > 0$ , there exists  $T(\epsilon) > 0$  such that for any  $t \geq T(\epsilon)$ , for  $P_{L-1}$ -almost every  $c_{L-1}$ ,

$$
\left. \right.\left| \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {H} (X, W (t)) \varphi_ {L - 1} \left(H _ {L - 1} (X, c _ {L - 1}; W (t))\right)\right]\right| \leq \epsilon .
$$

We claim that  $\mathcal{H}(x,W(t))\to \mathcal{H}\left(x,\{\bar{w}_i\}_{i\leq L}\right)$  in  $L^1 (\mathcal{P}_X)$  as  $t\rightarrow \infty$ . Assuming this claim and recalling that  $\varphi_{L - 1}$  is  $K$ -bounded by the regularity assumption, we then have that for some  $T^{\prime}(\epsilon)\geq T(\epsilon)$ , for any  $t\geq T^{\prime}(\epsilon)$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left. \operatorname {e s s} - \sup  \left| \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {H} \left(X, \{\bar {w} _ {i} \} _ {i \leq L}\right) \varphi_ {L - 1} \left(H _ {L - 1} \left(X, C _ {L - 1}; W (t)\right)\right) \right] \right| \right. \\ \leq K \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \left| \mathcal {H} (X, \{\bar {w} _ {i} \} _ {i \leq L}) - \mathcal {H} (X, W (t)) \right| \right] + \operatorname {e s s - s u p} | \mathbb {E} _ {Z} [ \mathcal {H} (X, W (t)) \varphi_ {L - 1} (H _ {L - 1} (X, C _ {L - 1}; W (t))) ] | \\ \leq K \epsilon . \\ \end{array}
$$

Since  $\operatorname{supp}\left(H_{L-1}(\cdot, C_{L-1}; W(t))\right) = L^2(\mathcal{P}_X)$  and  $\varphi_{L-1}$  is continuous,

$$
\left| \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {H} \left(X, \{\bar {w} _ {i} \} _ {i \leq L}\right) f (X) \right] \right| \leq K \epsilon \quad \forall f \in S,
$$

for  $S = \{\varphi_{L - 1}\circ g:g\in L^2 (\mathcal{P}_X)\}$ . Since  $\epsilon >0$  is arbitrary,

$$
\left| \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {H} \left(X, \{\bar {w} _ {i} \} _ {i \leq L}\right) f (X) \right] \right| = 0 \quad \forall f \in S.
$$

Furthermore, since  $\varphi_{L - 1}$  is non-obstructive,  $S$  has dense span in  $L^2 (\mathcal{P}_X)$ . Therefore  $\mathcal{H}\left(x,\{\bar{w}_i\}_{i\leq L}\right) = 0$  for  $\mathcal{P}_X$ -almost every  $x$ . Since  $\varphi_L^\prime$  is non-zero everywhere,

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \hat {y} \left(X; \{\bar {w} _ {i} \} _ {i \leq L}\right)\right) \mid X = x \right] = 0
$$

for  $\mathcal{P}_X$ -almost every  $x$ .

In Case 1, due to convexity of  $\mathcal{L}$ , for any measurable function  $\tilde{y}$ :

$$
\mathcal {L} \left(y, \tilde {y} \left(x\right)\right) - \mathcal {L} \left(y, \hat {y} \left(x; \{\bar {w} _ {i} \} _ {i \leq L}\right)\right) \geq \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} \left(y, \hat {y} \left(x, \{\bar {w} _ {i} \} _ {i \leq L}\right)\right) \left(\tilde {y} \left(x\right) - \hat {y} \left(x, \{\bar {w} _ {i} \} _ {i \leq L}\right)\right).
$$

Taking expectation, we get  $\mathbb{E}_Z[\mathcal{L}(Y,\tilde{y} (X))]\geq \mathcal{L}\left(\{\bar{w}_i\}_{i\leq L}\right)$

In Case 2, we have  $\partial_2\mathcal{L}\left(y(x),\hat{y}\left(x;\{\bar{w}_i\}_{i\leq L}\right)\right) = 0$  and hence  $\mathcal{L}\left(y(x),\hat{y}\left(x;\{\bar{w}_i\}_{i\leq L}\right)\right) = 0$ , for  $\mathcal{P}_X$ -almost every  $x$ , since  $y$  is a function of  $x$ .

This hence gives a result on  $\mathcal{L}\left(\{\bar{w}_i\}_{i\leq L}\right)$ , conditional on the claim that  $\mathcal{H}(x,W(t))\to \mathcal{H}\left(x,\{\bar{w}_i\}_{i\leq L}\right)$  in  $L^1 (\mathcal{P}_X)$  as  $t\rightarrow \infty$ . We now prove the claim. Recall the coupling  $\pi_t$  in Assumption 8.4. In the following, we let  $(C_1,\ldots ,C_L,C_1',\ldots ,C_L')\sim \pi_t$ . For brevity, we denote

$$
\delta_ {i} \left(t, x, c _ {i}, c _ {i} ^ {\prime}\right) = \left| H _ {i} \left(x, c _ {i} ^ {\prime}; W (t)\right) - H _ {i} \left(x, c _ {i}; \{\bar {w} _ {i} \} _ {i \leq L}\right) \right|.
$$

First observe that by the regularity assumption, for  $2 \leq i \leq L$ :

$$
\delta_ {i} \left(t, x, c _ {i}, c _ {i} ^ {\prime}\right) \leq K \mathbb {E} _ {C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i - 1} ^ {\prime}} \left[ \left| w _ {i} \left(t, C _ {i - 1} ^ {\prime}, c _ {i} ^ {\prime}\right) - \bar {w} _ {i} \left(C _ {i - 1}, c _ {i}\right) \right| + \left| \bar {w} _ {i} \left(C _ {i - 1}, c _ {i}\right) \right| \delta_ {i - 1} \left(t, x, C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i - 1} ^ {\prime}\right) \right],
$$

$$
\delta_ {1} \left(t, x, c _ {1}, c _ {1} ^ {\prime}\right) \leq K \left| w _ {1} \left(t, c _ {1} ^ {\prime}\right) - \bar {w} _ {1} \left(c _ {1}\right) \right|.
$$

This thus gives:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \left| \mathcal {H} (X, W (t)) - \mathcal {H} (X, \{\bar {w} _ {i} \} _ {i \leq L}) \right| \right] \\ \leq K \mathbb {E} _ {Z} [ \delta_ {L} (t, X, 1, 1) ] \\ \leq K ^ {L} \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {L} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left| w _ {i} \left(t, C _ {i - 1} ^ {\prime}, C _ {i} ^ {\prime}\right) - \bar {w} _ {i} \left(C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}\right) \right| \prod_ {j = i + 1} ^ {L} | \bar {w} _ {j} \left(C _ {j - 1}, C _ {j}\right) | \right] \\ + K ^ {L} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left| w _ {1} \left(t, C _ {1} ^ {\prime}\right) - \bar {w} _ {1} \left(C _ {1}\right) \right| \prod_ {j = 2} ^ {L} | \bar {w} _ {j} \left(C _ {j - 1}, C _ {j}\right) | \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

By the convergence assumption, the right-hand side tends to 0 as  $t \to \infty$ . This proves the claim.

Finally let us connect  $\mathcal{L}$  ( $W(t)$ ) with  $\mathcal{L}\left(\{\bar{w}_i\}_{i\leq L}\right)$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathcal {L} (W (t)) - \mathcal {L} (\{\bar {w} _ {i} \} _ {i \leq L}) \right| = \left| \mathbb {E} _ {Z} [ \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} (X; W (t))) - \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} (X; \{\bar {w} _ {i} \} _ {i \leq L})) ] \right| \\ \leq K \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \left| \hat {y} (X; W (t)) - \hat {y} (X; \{\bar {w} _ {i} \} _ {i \leq L}) \right| \right] \\ \leq K \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \delta_ {L} (t, X, 1, 1) \right], \\ \end{array}
$$

which again tends to 0 as  $t\to \infty$  . This concludes the proof.
