# 8 Convergence to Global Optimum under Morse-Sard Assumptions

In this section, we show that global convergence is guaranteed under a different set of convergence assumptions, namely convergence in moments of the MF limit and certain Morse-Sard assumptions. This generalizes the global convergence mechanism of [CB18b] for two-layer networks to settings where the loss  $\mathcal{L}$  is not necessarily convex and the depth  $L\geq 2$

# 8.1 The two-layer case

Consider the two-layer setting of Section 6.1. We make the following assumption.

Assumption 9. There exist limits  $\overline{w}_1$  and  $\overline{w}_2$  such that the following hold:

1. (Wasserstein-type convergence.) There exists a coupling  $\pi_t$  of  $P_1$  and itself such that

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {\pi_ {t}} \left[ | \overline {{w}} _ {2} (C _ {1}) | \left| w _ {1} (t, C _ {1} ^ {\prime}) - \overline {{w}} _ {1} (C _ {1}) \right| + \left| w _ {2} (t, C _ {1} ^ {\prime}, 1) - \overline {{w}} _ {2} (C _ {1}) \right|\right]\rightarrow 0
$$

as  $t\to \infty$  , where  $(C_1,C_1^{\prime})\sim \pi_t$

2. (Morse-Sard in the limit.) With  $\overline{W} = \{\overline{w}_1, \overline{w}_2\}$ , the mapping

$$
u _ {1} \mapsto \overline {{\mathcal {F}}} (u _ {1}) := \left| \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} (X; \overline {{W}})) \varphi_ {2} ^ {\prime} (H _ {2} (X, 1; \overline {{W}})) \varphi_ {1} (\langle u _ {1}, X \rangle) \right] \right| ^ {2}
$$

satisfies the following property. As  $r \to \infty$ ,  $\tilde{u}_1 \mapsto \overline{\mathcal{F}}(r\tilde{u}_1)$  converges uniformly in  $C^1(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})$  to a function  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^\infty: \mathbb{S}^{d-1} \to \mathbb{R}$ , where  $\mathbb{S}^{d-1} = \{\tilde{u}_1 \in \mathbb{R}^d : |\tilde{u}_1| = 1\}$ . Furthermore, for any stationary point  $u_1^*$  of  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}$  with  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}(u_1^*) > 0$ , and for any  $\delta_0 > 0$ , there exists  $\delta \in (0, \delta_0)$  so that for  $S_\delta$  the connected component of the set  $\{u : \overline{\mathcal{F}}(u) > \overline{\mathcal{F}}(u_1^*) - \delta\}$  that contains  $u_1^*$ , there is  $\xi > 0$  such that  $|\nabla \overline{\mathcal{F}}(u)| > \xi$  for all  $u \in \partial \mathrm{cl}(S_\delta)$  the boundary of the closure of  $S_\delta$ . Similarly, for any stationary point  $\tilde{u}_1^*$  of  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^\infty$  with  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^\infty(\tilde{u}_1^*) > 0$  and for any  $\delta_0 > 0$ , there exists  $\delta \in (0, \delta_0)$  so that for  $\tilde{S}_\delta$  the connected component of the set  $\{\tilde{u} \in \mathbb{S}^{d-1} : \overline{\mathcal{F}}^\infty(\tilde{u}) > \overline{\mathcal{F}}^\infty(\tilde{u}_1^*) - \delta\}$  that contains  $\tilde{u}_1^*$ , there is  $\xi > 0$  such that  $|\nabla \overline{\mathcal{F}}^\infty(\tilde{u})| > \xi$  for all  $\tilde{u} \in \partial \mathrm{cl}(\tilde{S}_\delta)$ .

The convergence condition in the above assumption is actually the first part of Assumption 6.4, and hence the same remark for Assumption 6.4 applies; i.e. one can deduce this condition from the convergence of  $(w_{1}(t,\cdot),w_{2}(t,\cdot ,1))$  to  $(\overline{w}_1,\overline{w}_2)$  as  $t\to \infty$  in the Wasserstein-2 distance.

Theorem 41. Consider the MF limit corresponding to the network (9), such that they are coupled together by the coupling procedure in Section 4.1. Under Assumptions 6.1, 6.2, 6.3, 6.5 and 9, the following hold:

- Case 1 (convex loss): If  $\mathcal{L}$  is convex in the second variable, then:

$$
\lim  _ {t \to \infty} \mathcal {L} \left(W (t)\right) = \inf  _ {f _ {1}, f _ {2}} \mathcal {L} \left(f _ {1}, f _ {2}\right) = \inf  _ {\tilde {y}} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \tilde {y} (X)\right) \right].
$$

- Case 2 (generic non-negative loss): Suppose  $\partial_2\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$  implies  $\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$ . If  $y = y(x)$  is a function of  $x$ , then  $\mathcal{L}(W(t)) = 0$  as  $t \to \infty$ .

Let us make a comparison with the two-layer setting in Section 6.1, and in particular, Assumption 6. We see that the convergence assumption in Assumption 6 is replaced by Assumption 9. More specifically the uniform convergence condition of  $\frac{\partial}{\partial t} w_2(t,C_1,1)$  in Assumption 6 is replaced by the Morse-Sard condition of Assumption 9.

Similar to the proof of Theorem 31 in Section 6.1, the role of the Morse-Sard condition is - together with the full support property supp (Law  $(w_{1}(t,C_{1})) = \mathbb{R}^{d}$  by Lemma 55 - to affirm that

$$
\overline {{\mathcal {F}}} (u _ {1}) = \left| \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} (X; \overline {{W}})) \varphi_ {2} ^ {\prime} (H _ {2} (X, 1; \overline {{W}})) \varphi_ {1} (\langle u _ {1}, X \rangle) \right] \right| ^ {2} = 0 \quad \forall u _ {1} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {d},
$$

after which universal approximation is invoked to yield the desired global convergence. The main idea is the following: should the above not hold, there exists a region of  $u_{1}$  where  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}(u_1) > 0$ . Since  $\operatorname{supp}(\operatorname{Law}(w_1(t,C_1))) = \mathbb{R}^d$ , at any time  $t$ , for a non-negligible mass on  $C_1$ ,  $w_{1}(t,C_{1})$  fully occupies the region. The Morse-Sard condition ensures that the interaction over time between the two layers  $w_{1}(t,C_{1})$  and  $w_{2}(t,C_{1},1)$  in this region would however force the dynamics to diverge so long as  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}(u_1) > 0$ .

The proof of Theorem 41 is deferred to Appendix G. We also refer to Section 9 for further discussions. In the following, we extend this result to the multilayer case and present its proof.

# 8.2 The multilayer case

One can obtain a multilayer analog of Theorem 41. The key idea behind the Morse-Sard condition is similar to the two-layer case. Here the advantage of our framework becomes clearer since it easily accommodates the idea in the multilayer setup.

Recall the setting of Section 7. We make the following assumption which is a direct analogue of Assumption 9 in the two-layer case.

Assumption 10. There exist limits  $\{\overline{w}_i\}_{i\leq L}$  such that the following hold:

1. (Wasserstein-type convergence.) There exist couplings  $\pi_t$  of  $\prod_{i=1}^{L} P_i$  and itself such that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} _ {\pi_ {t}} \left[ \left| w _ {i} (t, C _ {i - 1} ^ {\prime}, C _ {i} ^ {\prime}) - \overline {{w}} _ {i} (C _ {i - 1}, C _ {i}) \right| ^ {2} \prod_ {j = i + 1} ^ {L} | \overline {{w}} _ {j} (C _ {j - 1}, C _ {j}) | ^ {2} \right] \to 0, \quad i = 2, \dots , L, \\ \mathbb {E} _ {\pi_ {t}} \left[ \left| w _ {1} (t, C _ {1} ^ {\prime}) - \overline {{w}} _ {1} (C _ {1}) \right| ^ {2} \prod_ {j = 2} ^ {L} | \overline {{w}} _ {j} (C _ {j - 1}, C _ {j}) | ^ {2} \right] \to 0, \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
a s t \rightarrow \infty , w h e r e (C _ {1}, \dots , C _ {L}, C _ {1} ^ {\prime}, \dots , C _ {L} ^ {\prime}) \sim \pi_ {t}.
$$

2. (Morse-Sard in the limit.) With  $\overline{W} = \{\overline{w}_i\}_{i\leq L}$ , the mapping  $u_{L - 1}\in L^{2}(P_{L - 2})\mapsto \overline{\mathcal{F}} (u_{L - 1})$  defined by

$$
\overline {{\mathcal {F}}} (u _ {L - 1}) := \left| \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, y (X; \overline {{W}})) \varphi_ {L} ^ {\prime} (H _ {L} (X, 1; \overline {{W}})) \varphi_ {L - 1} \left(\left\langle u _ {L - 1}, H _ {L - 2} (X, \cdot ; \overline {{W}}) \right\rangle_ {L ^ {2} (P _ {L - 2})}\right) \right] \right| ^ {2}
$$

satisfies the following property. As  $r \to \infty$ ,  $\tilde{u}_{L-1} \mapsto \overline{\mathcal{F}}(r\tilde{u}_{L-1})$  converges uniformly in  $C^1\left(\mathbb{S}\left(L^2(P_{L-2})\right)\right)$  to a function  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^\infty: \mathbb{S}\left(L^2(P_{L-2})\right) \to \mathbb{R}$ , where  $\mathbb{S}\left(L^2(P_{L-2})\right) = \{u \in L^2(P_{L-2}): |u|_{L^2(P_{L-2})} = 1\}$ . Furthermore, for any stationary point  $u_{L-1}^*$  of  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}$  and for any  $\delta_0 > 0$ , there exists  $\delta \in (0, \delta_0)$  so that for  $S_\delta$  the connected component of the set  $\{u: \overline{\mathcal{F}}(u) > \overline{\mathcal{F}}(u_{L-1}^*) - \delta\}$  that contains  $u_{L-1}^*$ , there is  $\xi > 0$  such that  $|\nabla \overline{\mathcal{F}}(u)| > \xi$  for all  $u \in \partial \mathrm{cl}(S_\delta)$  the boundary of the closure of  $S_\delta$ . Similarly, for any stationary point  $\tilde{u}_{L-1}^*$  of  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^\infty$  and for any  $\delta_0 > 0$ , there exists  $\delta \in (0, \delta_0)$  so that for  $S_\delta$  the connected component of the set  $\{u: \overline{\mathcal{F}}(u) > \overline{\mathcal{F}}^\infty(\tilde{u}_{L-1}^*) - \delta\}$  which contains  $r\tilde{u}_{L-1}^*$  for all  $r$  sufficiently large, there is  $\xi > 0$  such that  $|\nabla \overline{\mathcal{F}}(u)| > \xi$  for all  $u \in \partial \mathrm{cl}(S_\delta)$ .

The convergence condition in the above assumption is actually the first part of Assumption 8.4, and hence the same remark for Assumption 8.4 applies; i.e. one can deduce this condition from the convergence of  $(w_{i}(t))_{i = 1}^{L}$  to  $(\bar{w}_i)_{i = 1}^L$  in an appropriate Wasserstein distance. We now state the theorem. The proof is deferred to Section 8.3.

Theorem 42. Consider a neuronal embedding  $\left(\Omega, P, \{w_i^0\}_{i \leq L}\right)$  and the MF limit described in Section 7, and in particular, under Assumptions Assumptions 8.1, 8.2, 8.3, 8.5 and 10. Assume  $\xi_L(\cdot) = \xi_{L-1}(\cdot) = 1$ . Then:

- Case 1 (convex loss): If  $\mathcal{L}$  is convex in the second variable, then:

$$
\lim  _ {t \to \infty} \mathcal {L} \left(W (t)\right) = \inf  _ {F} \mathcal {L} (F) = \inf  _ {\tilde {y}: \mathbb {R} ^ {d} \to \mathbb {R}} \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \mathcal {L} \left(Y, \tilde {y} (X)\right) \right].
$$

- Case 2 (generic non-negative loss): Suppose that  $\partial_2\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$  implies  $\mathcal{L}(y,\hat{y}) = 0$ . If  $y = y(x)$  is a function of  $x$ , then  $\mathcal{L}(W(t)) \to 0$  as  $t \to \infty$ .

# 8.3 Proof of Theorem 42

Proof of Theorem 42. In the following, for  $f, g \in L^{2}(P_{L - 2})$ , let us write  $\langle f, g \rangle$  in place of  $\langle f, g \rangle_{L^2 (P_{L - 2})}$  for brevity. Let us define

$$
\overline {{H}} _ {i} (x, C _ {i}) = H _ {i} (x, C _ {i}; \overline {{W}}), \qquad \overline {{H}} _ {L} (x) = \overline {{H}} _ {L} (x, 1), \qquad \bar {y} (x) = \hat {y} (x; \overline {{W}}),
$$

and for  $u_{L - 1}\in L^2 (P_{L - 2})$

$$
\overline {{G}} _ {L} (u _ {L - 1}) = \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, \overline {{y}} (X)) \varphi_ {L} ^ {\prime} (\overline {{H}} _ {L} (X)) \varphi_ {L - 1} (\langle u _ {L - 1}, \overline {{H}} _ {L - 2} (X, \cdot) \rangle) \right],
$$

$$
\overline {{G}} _ {L - 1} (u _ {L - 1}) = \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, \bar {y} (X)) \varphi_ {L} ^ {\prime} (\bar {H} _ {L} (X)) \varphi_ {L - 1} ^ {\prime} (\langle u _ {L - 1}, \bar {H} _ {L - 2} (X, \cdot) \rangle) \bar {H} _ {L - 2} (X, \cdot) \right],
$$

$$
G _ {L} (t, u _ {L - 1}) = \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} (t, X)) \varphi_ {L} ^ {\prime} \left(H _ {L} (t, X, 1)\right) \varphi_ {L - 1} \left(\left\langle u _ {L - 1}, H _ {L - 2} (t, X, \cdot) \right\rangle \right], \right.
$$

$$
G _ {L - 1} (t, u _ {L - 1}) = \mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} (t, X)) \varphi_ {L} ^ {\prime} (H _ {L} (t, X, 1)) \varphi_ {L - 1} ^ {\prime} (\langle u _ {L - 1}, H _ {L - 2} (t, X, \cdot) \rangle) H _ {L - 2} (t, X, \cdot) \right].
$$

Note that  $\overline{G}_{L - 1}(u_{L - 1}), G_{L - 1}(t, u_{L - 1}) \in L^2(P_{L - 2})$  which follows easily from Assumptions 8.3 and 10.1 and Lemma 8. Similar to the proof of Theorem 41, with Assumptions 8.3 and 10.1, we obtain that as  $t \to \infty$ ,

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \left| \overline {{H}} _ {L} (X) - H _ {L} (t, X, 1) \right| ^ {2} \right] \to 0, \qquad \mathbb {E} \left[ | \bar {y} (X) - \hat {y} (t, X) | ^ {2} \right] \to 0,
$$

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ | \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} (t, X)) - \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, \overline {{y}} (X)) | ^ {2} \right]\rightarrow 0,
$$

and uniformly in  $u_{L - 1}$

$$
\left| G _ {L - 1} (t, u _ {L - 1}) - \overline {{G}} _ {L - 1} (u _ {L - 1}) \right| ^ {2} \to 0, \qquad \left| G _ {L} (t, u _ {L - 1}) - \overline {{G}} _ {L} (u _ {L - 1}) \right| ^ {2} \to 0.
$$

Consider the limit potential given by

$$
\overline {{\mathcal {F}}} (u _ {L - 1}) = \frac {1}{2} \left| \overline {{G}} _ {L} (u _ {L - 1}) \right| ^ {2}.
$$

By Assumption 8.3,  $u_{L - 1} \mapsto \overline{\mathcal{F}}(u_{L - 1})$  is continuous. Notice that

$$
\nabla \overline {{\mathcal {F}}} (u _ {L - 1}) = \frac {1}{2} \cdot 2 \overline {{G}} _ {L} (u _ {L - 1}) \nabla (\overline {{G}} _ {L} (u _ {L - 1})) = \overline {{G}} _ {L} (u _ {L - 1}) \overline {{G}} _ {L - 1} (u _ {L - 1}).
$$

Let  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\infty}:\mathbb{S}\left(L^{2}(P_{L - 2})\right)\to \mathbb{R}$  be defined by  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\infty}(\tilde{u}_{L - 1}) = \lim_{r\to \infty}\overline{\mathcal{F}} (r\tilde{u}_{L - 1})$  , which exists by Assumption 10. We shall argue that  $\overline{\mathcal{F}} (u_{L - 1}) = 0$  for all  $u_{L - 1}\in L^2 (P_{L - 2})$  , by contradiction. To that end, let us

assume that  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}(u_{L-1}) \neq 0$  for some  $u_{L-1}$ . Note that  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}$  is bounded by a constant by Assumption 8.3. Thus, either there is a local maximizer  $u_{L-1}^*$  of  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}$  with  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}(u_{L-1}^*) > 0$  or there is a local maximizer  $\tilde{u}_{L-1}^*$  of  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^\infty$  with  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^\infty(\tilde{u}_{L-1}^*) > 0$ .

First consider the case that  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}$  has a local maximizer  $u_{L - 1}^{*}$  with  $\overline{\mathcal{F}} (u_{L - 1}^{*}) > 0$ . Under Assumption 10, there exists  $\delta \in \left(0,\overline{\mathcal{F}}\left(u_{L - 1}^{*}\right)\right)$  arbitrarily small so that for  $S_{\delta}$  the connected component of the set  $\{u:\overline{\mathcal{F}} (u) > \overline{\mathcal{F}} (u_{L - 1}^{*}) - \delta \}$  that contains  $u_{L - 1}^{*}$ , there is  $\xi >0$  such that  $\left|\nabla \overline{\mathcal{F}} (u_{L - 1})\right| > \xi$  for all  $u_{L - 1}\in \partial \operatorname {cl}(S_{\delta})$ . Let  $T_0$  be sufficiently large so that for  $t\geq T_0$ , we have if  $u_{L - 1}\in \partial \operatorname {cl}(S_{\delta})$ ,  $\left|\overline{G}_{L - 1}(u_{L - 1}) - G_{L - 1}(t,u_{L - 1})\right|\leq \xi /\sqrt{8\overline{\mathcal{F}}(u_{L - 1}^{*})}$ , which - similar to the proof of Theorem 41 - implies

$$
\left\langle \overline {{G}} _ {L - 1} \left(u _ {L - 1}\right), G _ {L - 1} (t, u _ {L - 1}) \right\rangle > \frac {\xi^ {2}}{4 \bar {\mathcal {F}} \left(u _ {L - 1} ^ {*}\right)}. \tag {14}
$$

Also, we further enlarge  $T_0$  so that for  $t \geq T_0$  and any  $u_{L-1} \in \operatorname{cl}(S_\delta)$ ,  $\left| \overline{G}_L(u_{L-1}) - G_L(t, u_{L-1}) \right| \leq \frac{1}{2} \sqrt{\overline{\mathcal{F}}(u_{L-1}^*) - \delta}$  and hence

$$
G _ {L} (t, u _ {L - 1}) \geq \bar {G} _ {L} \left(u _ {L - 1}\right) - \frac {1}{2} \sqrt {\bar {\mathcal {F}} \left(u _ {L - 1} ^ {*}\right) - \delta} > \bar {G} _ {L} \left(u _ {L - 1}\right) - \frac {1}{2} \sqrt {\bar {\mathcal {F}} \left(u _ {L - 1}\right)} = \bar {G} _ {L} \left(u _ {L - 1}\right) - \frac {1}{2} \left| \bar {G} _ {L} \left(u _ {L - 1}\right) \right|, \tag {15}
$$

$$
G _ {L} (t, u _ {L - 1}) \leq \bar {G} _ {L} \left(u _ {L - 1}\right) + \frac {1}{2} \sqrt {\bar {\mathcal {F}} \left(u _ {L - 1} ^ {*}\right) - \delta} <   \bar {G} _ {L} \left(u _ {L - 1}\right) + \frac {1}{2} \sqrt {\bar {\mathcal {F}} \left(u _ {L - 1}\right)} = \bar {G} _ {L} \left(u _ {L - 1}\right) + \frac {1}{2} \left| \bar {G} _ {L} \left(u _ {L - 1}\right) \right|. \tag {16}
$$

Furthermore notice that

$$
\frac {\partial}{\partial t} \overline {{G}} _ {L} \left(w _ {L - 1} (t, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})\right) = - w _ {L} (t, C _ {L - 1}, 1) \left\langle \overline {{G}} _ {L - 1} \left(w _ {L - 1} (t, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})\right), G _ {L - 1} (t, w _ {L - 1} (t, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})) \right\rangle . \tag {17}
$$

Let  $\tilde{\Omega}_{L - 1}$  be the subset of  $\Omega_{L - 1}$  consisting of  $c_{L - 1}$  where  $|w_{L}(0,c_{L},1)| < 1$ . As shown in Step 1 of the proof of Theorem 38, for any  $t\geq 0$ , we have

$$
\operatorname {s u p p} \left(w _ {L - 1} (t, \cdot , C _ {L - 1}), C _ {L - 1} \in \tilde {\Omega} _ {L - 1}\right) = L ^ {2} \left(P _ {L - 2}\right),
$$

and therefore for any open subset  $B$  of  $L^2 (P_{L - 2})$ , there exists a positive mass of  $C_{L - 1}\in \tilde{\Omega}_{L - 1}$  such that  $w_{L - 1}(t,\cdot ,C_{L - 1})\in B$ . In the following, we consider  $C_{L - 1}\in \tilde{\Omega}_{L - 1}$ . We further divide the argument into two cases:  $\overline{G}_L(u_{L - 1}^*) > 0$  and  $\overline{G}_L(u_{L - 1}^*) < 0$ .

Let us consider the case that  $\overline{G}_L(u_{L - 1}^*) > 0$ . Then we can choose sufficiently small  $\delta$  such that  $\overline{G}_L(u_{L - 1}) > 0$  for all  $u_{L - 1}\in \operatorname {cl}(S_{\delta})$ . Furthermore consider the scenario that there exists  $T\geq T_0$  such that a positive mass of  $(w_{L - 1}(T,\cdot ,C_{L - 1}),w_L(T,C_{L - 1},1))$  on  $C_{L - 1}\in \tilde{\Omega}_{L - 1}$  has  $w_{L - 1}(T,\cdot ,C_{L - 1})\in S_{\delta}$  and  $w_{L}(T,C_{L - 1},1) < 0$ . Note that if  $w_{L - 1}(t,\cdot ,C_{L - 1})\in S_{\delta}$ ,

$$
\frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {L} (t, C _ {L - 1}, 1) = - G _ {L} (t, w _ {L - 1} (T, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})) \leq - \left(\overline {{G}} _ {L} (w _ {L - 1} (T, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})) - \frac {1}{2} | \overline {{G}} _ {L} (w _ {L - 1} (T, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})) |\right) <   0
$$

by Eq. (15). Define  $T_{1} = \inf \left\{t \geq T : w_{L-1}(t, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \notin S_{\delta}\right\}$ . Then  $t \mapsto w_{L}(t, C_{L-1}, 1)$  is decreasing on  $t \in [T, T_{1})$ . Let us argue that  $T_{1} = \infty$ . Indeed, suppose  $T_{1}$  is finite. We then have, by continuity,  $w_{L-1}(T_{1}, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in \partial \operatorname{cl}(S_{\delta})$  and  $w_{L}(T_{1}, C_{L-1}, 1) \leq w_{L}(T, C_{L-1}, 1)) < 0$ . As such,  $\frac{\partial}{\partial t} \overline{G}_{L}(w_{L-1}(T_{1}, \cdot, C_{L-1})) > 0$  by Eq. (14) and (17). By continuity, for some  $\gamma > 0$ ,  $\frac{\partial}{\partial t} \overline{G}_{L}(w_{L-1}(T_{1} + t, \cdot, C_{L-1})) > 0$  for all  $t \in [0, \gamma]$ . But then  $\overline{G}_{L}(w_{L-1}(T_{1} + t, \cdot, C_{L-1})) \geq \overline{G}_{L}(w_{L-1}(T_{1}, \cdot, C_{L-1})) \geq \sqrt{2(\overline{\mathcal{F}}(u_{L-1}^{*}) - \delta)}$ , and hence  $w_{L-1}(T_{1} + t, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in S_{\delta}$  for all  $t \leq \gamma$ , contradicting the definition of  $T_{1}$ . Therefore  $T_{1} = \infty$ , i.e. for

$t \geq T$  and  $C_1 \in \tilde{\Omega}_1$  with  $w_{L-1}(T, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in S_\delta$  and  $w_L(T, C_{L-1}, 1) < 0$ , we have  $w_{L-1}(t, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in S_\delta$ , which implies

$$
G _ {L} (t, w _ {L - 1} (t, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})) \stackrel {(a)} {\geq} \frac {1}{2} \overline {{G}} _ {L} (w _ {L - 1} (t, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})) = \sqrt {\frac {1}{2} \overline {{\mathcal {F}}} (w _ {L - 1} (t , \cdot , C _ {L - 1}))} \geq \sqrt {\frac {1}{2} (\overline {{\mathcal {F}}} (u _ {L - 1} ^ {*} - \delta)},
$$

where  $(a)$  is by Eq. (15) and the fact  $\overline{G}_L(u_{L - 1}) > 0$  for all  $u_{L - 1}\in \operatorname {cl}(S_\delta)$ . In particular, there is a positive mass of  $(w_{L - 1}(T,\cdot ,C_{L - 1}),w_L(T,C_{L - 1},1))$  with  $G_{L}(t,w_{L - 1}(t,\cdot ,C_{L - 1}))\geq \sqrt{(\overline{\mathcal{F}}(u_{L - 1}^{*}) - \delta) / 2}$  for all  $t\ge T$ . Noting that

$$
\frac {d}{d t} \mathbb {E} \left[ \mathcal {L} (Y, \hat {y} (t, X)) \right] \leq - \mathbb {E} \left[ | G _ {L} (t, w _ {L - 1} (t, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})) | ^ {2} \right],
$$

we obtain  $\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(Y,\hat{y} (t,X))]$  being bounded above by a strictly negative constant for all  $t\geq T$ , which is a contradiction since  $\mathcal{L}$  is bounded below.

Next consider the scenario that for all  $t \geq T_0$ , the probability that  $w_{L-1}(t, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in S_\delta$  and  $w_L(t, C_{L-1}, 1) < 0$  on  $C_{L-1} \in \tilde{\Omega}_{L-1}$  is zero. Let us argue that for any  $t \geq T_0$  and for a.e.  $C_{L-1} \in \tilde{\Omega}_{L-1}$  with  $w_{L-1}(t, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in S_\delta$ , we have  $w_{L-1}(s, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in S_\delta$  for all  $s \in [T_0, t]$ . Indeed, consider  $t$  and  $C_{L-1} \in \tilde{\Omega}_{L-1}$  such that  $w_{L-1}(t, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in S_\delta$  and  $w_{L-1}(T', \cdot, C_{L-1}) \notin S_\delta$  for some  $T' \in [T_0, t)$ . Let  $t' = \sup \{s \in [T', t] : w_{L-1}(s, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \notin S_\delta\} < t$ . By continuity,  $w_{L-1}(t', \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in \partial \operatorname{cl}(S_\delta)$  and so by Eq. (14),

$$
\left\langle \overline {{G}} _ {L - 1} (w _ {L - 1} (t ^ {\prime}, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})), G _ {L - 1} (t ^ {\prime}, w _ {L - 1} (t ^ {\prime}, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})) \right\rangle > \frac {\xi^ {2}}{4 \overline {{\mathcal {F}}} (u _ {L - 1} ^ {*})}.
$$

By continuity, there exists  $t'' \in (t', t)$  such that for all  $s \in [t', t'']$ ,

$$
\left\langle \overline {{G}} _ {L - 1} (w _ {L - 1} (s, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})), G _ {L - 1} (s, w _ {L - 1} (s, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})) \right\rangle \geq \frac {\xi^ {2}}{1 0 0 \overline {{\mathcal {F}}} (u _ {L - 1} ^ {*})}.
$$

By definition of  $t'$ , we also have  $w_{L-1}(s, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in S_{\delta}$  and therefore  $w_{L}(s, C_{L-1}, 1) \geq 0$  for any  $s \in (t', t]$ . Then by Eq. (23),  $\frac{\partial}{\partial t} \overline{G}_{L}(w_{L-1}(s, \cdot, C_{L-1})) \leq 0$  for all  $s \in (t', t'']$  and therefore

$$
\overline {{G}} _ {L} \left(w _ {L - 1} \left(t ^ {\prime \prime}, \cdot , C _ {L - 1}\right)\right) \leq \overline {{G}} _ {L} \left(w _ {L - 1} \left(t ^ {\prime}, \cdot , C _ {L - 1}\right)\right) = \sqrt {2 \left(\overline {{\mathcal {F}}} \left(u _ {L - 1} ^ {*}\right) - \delta\right)},
$$

where the equality follows from  $w_{L-1}(t', \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in \partial \operatorname{cl}(S_{\delta})$ . However this contradicts with  $w_{L-1}(t'', \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in S_{\delta}$ . Therefore it holds that for any  $t \geq T_0$ , for a.e.  $C_{L-1} \in \tilde{\Omega}_{L-1}$  with  $w_{L-1}(t, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in S_{\delta}$ , we have  $w_{L-1}(s, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in S_{\delta}$  and therefore  $w_{L}(s, C_{L-1}, 1) \geq 0$  for all  $s \in [T_0, t]$ . Since  $w_{L-1}(t, \cdot, C_{L-1})$  on  $C_{L-1} \in \tilde{\Omega}_{L-1}$  has full support at any  $t \geq 0$ , we have for any  $t_0 \geq T_0$ , there is a positive mass on  $C_{L-1} \in \tilde{\Omega}_{L-1}$  such that  $w_{L-1}(t_0, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in S_{\delta}$  and hence, as shown,  $w_{L-1}(s, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in S_{\delta}$  and  $w_{L}(s, C_{L-1}, 1) \geq 0$  for all  $s \in [T_0, t_0]$ . Note that we have  $w_{L}(T_0, C_{L-1}, 1) \leq M(T_0)$  for some finite  $M(T_0) > 0$  for  $C_{L-1} \in \tilde{\Omega}_{L-1}$  (which follows from the fact  $|\frac{\partial}{\partial t} w_L(t, \cdot, 1)| \leq K$  by Assumption 6.3 and that  $w_{L}(0, C_{L-1}, 1) < 1$ ). Also note that for  $w_{L-1}(s, \cdot, C_{L-1}) \in S_{\delta}$  and  $s \geq T_0$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\partial}{\partial t} w _ {L} (s, C _ {L - 1}, 1) = - G _ {L} (s, w _ {L - 1} (s, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})) \stackrel {(a)} {\leq} - \frac {1}{2} \overline {{G}} _ {L} (w _ {L - 1} (s, \cdot , C _ {L - 1})) \\ = - \sqrt {\frac {1}{2} \overline {{\mathcal {F}}} (w _ {L - 1} (s , \cdot , C _ {L - 1}))} \leq - \sqrt {\frac {1}{2} (\overline {{\mathcal {F}}} (u _ {L - 1} ^ {*}) - \delta)} \\ \end{array}
$$

a strictly negative constant, where  $(a)$  is by Eq. (15) and the fact  $\overline{G}_L(u_{L - 1}) > 0$  for all  $u_{L - 1}\in \operatorname {cl}(S_{\delta})$ . As such, for any  $t_0\geq T_0$  such that

$$
M \left(T _ {0}\right) - \left(t _ {0} - T _ {0}\right) \sqrt {\frac {1}{2} \left(\overline {{\mathcal {F}}} \left(u _ {L - 1} ^ {*}\right) - \delta\right)} <   0,
$$

there is a positive mass on  $C_{L - 1} \in \tilde{\Omega}_{L - 1}$  such that firstly  $w_{L}(s, C_{L - 1}, 1) \geq 0$  for all  $s \in [T_0, t_0]$  and secondly there exists  $t \in [T_0, t_0]$  in which

$$
w _ {L} (t, C _ {L - 1}, 1) \leq M (T _ {0}) - (t - T _ {0}) \sqrt {\frac {1}{2} (\overline {{\mathcal {F}}} (u _ {L - 1} ^ {*}) - \delta)} <   0.
$$

We again obtain a contradiction.

The case  $\overline{G}_L(u_{L - 1}^*) < 0$  can be treated similarly, with the use of Eq. (15) replaced by Eq. (16). Both cases lead to a contradiction, ruling out the possibility that there is a local maximizer  $u_{L - 1}^{*}$  of  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}$  with  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}(u_{L - 1}^{*}) > 0$ .

Next consider the case  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}$  does not have any local maximizer in  $L^2 (P_{L - 2})$  but  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^\infty$  has a local maximizer  $\tilde{u}_{L - 1}^{*}$  with  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^\infty (\tilde{u}_{L - 1}^*) > 0$ . Under Assumption 10, there exists  $\delta \in (0,\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\infty}(\tilde{u}_{L - 1}^{*}))$  arbitrarily small so that for  $S_{\delta}$  the connected component of the set  $\{u:\overline{\mathcal{F}} (u) > \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\infty}(\tilde{u}_{L - 1}^{*}) - \delta \}$  which contains  $r\tilde{u}_{L - 1}^{*}$  for all  $r$  sufficiently large, there is  $\xi >0$  such that  $\left|\nabla \overline{\mathcal{F}} (u)\right| > \xi$  for all  $u\in \partial \operatorname {cl}(S_{\delta})$ . The rest of the argument can be repeated as before to yield a contradiction.

In short, we have shown that  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}(u_{L-1}) = \frac{1}{2}\left|\overline{G}_L(u_{L-1})\right|^2 = 0$ , and equivalently,

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {Z} \left[ \partial_ {2} \mathcal {L} (Y, \overline {{y}} (X)) \varphi_ {L} ^ {\prime} (\overline {{H}} _ {L} (X)) \varphi_ {L - 1} (\langle u _ {L - 1}, \overline {{H}} _ {L - 2} (X, \cdot) \rangle) \right] = 0
$$

for all  $u_{L - 1} \in L^2 (P_{L - 2})$ . The proof can now be completed similar to the proof of Theorem 38.

![](images/7a171e04a2d9f162a61f652f5b8404b20d09e92d49fe64b04f28a904e55234d5.jpg)

# 9 Further discussions

Having presented our neuronal embedding framework for multilayer MF neural networks and proven several results concerning i.i.d. initializations and global convergence under various settings, we now place the discussion of our work in the context of related works.

# 9.1 Two-layer neural networks

The MF view on the training dynamics of neural networks has gathered significant interests in the recent literature, starting with the two-layer case [NS17, MMN18, CB18b, RVE18, SS18]. In this case, it is known that convergence to global optimum is possible for gradient descent or SGD [MMN18, CB18b, RVE18], with a potentially exponential rate [JMM19] and a dimension-independent width [MMM19]. This line of works has also inspired research into new training algorithms [WLLM19, RJBVE19, NWS20], stability properties of the trained networks [SM19], other architectures which are compositions of multiple MF neural networks [WMW20, LML $^{+}$ 20] and MF neural networks in other machine learning contexts [AL20, Ngu21]. Most works focus on fully-connected networks on the Euclidean space and utilize certain convexity properties to study optimization efficiency. The MF formulation of the two-layer case in these works enjoys the wealth of the mathematics of optimal transport and gradient flows in measure spaces [AGS08].

Our work, on the other hand, considers general Hilbert spaces which can be infinite-dimensional (Section 2) and does not rely critically on convexity (Theorems 31, 34, 38, 41 and 42). Our framework departs from the Wasserstein gradient flow viewpoint, and while being in the early stage of technical foundations, it is demonstrated to give useful results including and beyond the two-layer case.

# 9.2 Multilayer neural networks

As mentioned in the introduction, the multilayer case poses a major conceptual challenge. Prior to our work, several ideas have been proposed independently. [Ngu19] puts forth the idea that a neuron is represented by a stochastic (Markov) kernel and gives a heuristic derivation, where the MF limit is described by a certain evolution of measures over the space of stochastic kernels. [AOY19] rigorously derives the MF limit as an evolution of a measure on paths through layers. In [SS19], the network is

viewed as a time-dependent function of its initialization and this function simplifies upon concentrations over the randomness of the initialization. All three works employ scalings with respect to the widths, in which normalizations are applied at every layers, not just the last layer, together with compensating learning rates. This thereby ensures nonlinear evolution at all layers.

Working under the same scalings, our framework gives a new perspective via a central question: how does one describe an ensemble of an arbitrary number of neurons? Answering this question, our idea of a neuronal embedding allows one to describe the MF limit in a clean and rigorous manner. In particular, it avoids extra assumptions made in [AOY19, SS19]: unlike our work, [AOY19] assumes untrained first and last layers and requires non-trivial technical tools; [SS19] takes an unnatural sequential limit of the widths and proves a non-quantitative result, whereas we prove a quantitative bound that essentially requires only the minimum of the widths to be large. An advantage of our framework comes from the fact that while MF formulations in [AOY19, SS19] are specific to and exploit i.i.d. initializations, our formulation does not and thereby allows to study i.i.d. initializations as well as interesting non-i.i.d. initialization schemes. Compared to [Ngu19], while a certain step of our analysis takes an inspiration from the idea of stochastic kernels in [Ngu19], our framework circumvents its technical cumbersomeness and gives a rigorous and clean mathematical treatment.

After our first preprint, the work [FLYZ20] takes another view on this challenge. In particular, considering a finite set of training data, [FLYZ20] encodes each neuron by its pre-activation values, computed over the entire training data, at initialization. As a specific interpretation by [FLYZ20], the pre-activation values at initialization capture a certain sense of "features" seen by the neurons. Meanwhile our framework identifies neuron  $j_{i}$  at layer  $i$  via the sample  $C_i(j_i)$  drawn from the space  $(\Omega_i, P_i)$  (Section 4.1) and remains general about this space. One may observe the following connection: a specific choice of the neuronal embedding can be built over random variables that are defined by the pre-activation values at initialization. The generality of our framework maintains freedom over choices of the neuronal embedding, including this specific choice. For example, when the training data size is infinite - an idealized situation commonly assumed in theoretical studies, then if one follows [FLYZ20], each pre-activation becomes a function over an infinite domain, instead of a finite-dimensional vector. This potentially poses technical complications, which can be avoided simply by a different choice of the neuronal embedding in our framework.

# 9.3 Degeneracy with i.i.d. initializations

As shown in Section 5, i.i.d. initializations cause strong degeneracy for a network depth at least four. The work [AOY19] is the first to realize and take advantage of this phenomenon to formulate the MF limit; in particular, the measure on the paths in [AOY19] admits a product structure, signifying the mutually independent nature of the evolutions of weights at different layers in the infinite width limit. Note however that [AOY19] explicitly exploits this degeneracy phenomenon to formulate the MF limit. In contrast, our framework is general and upon specializing to the case of i.i.d. initializations, it allows to derive this phenomenon in greater details and simultaneously remove certain technical assumptions in [AOY19]. In particular, we remove the technical conditions of random input and output features and no biases of [AOY19]. In addition, one can use Corollary 28 to immediately verify that in the setting of no biases,  $L \geq 5$  and untrained first and last layers  $(\xi_1^{\mathbf{w}}(\cdot) = \xi_L^{\mathbf{w}}(\cdot) = 0)$ , the weights and activations in the limit satisfy the McKean-Vlasov equation in [AOY19].

Such degeneracy is generally undesirable. The fact that our framework is not specific to i.i.d. initializations allows for an escape from this situation. In this aspect, our framework follows closely the spirit of the work [Ngu19], whose MF formulation is also not specific to i.i.d. initializations. Through the language of stochastic kernels, [Ngu19] envisions a scenario in which evolutions of the weights at different layers are stochastically coupled. The usefulness of such scenario is realized by our global convergence guarantee for multilayer networks with arbitrary depths in Sections 7 and 8 (Theorems 38 and 42), with the novel idea of bidirectional diversity for non-i.i.d. initialization.

# 9.4 Global convergence

Optimization efficacy has been one major question that sets the MF literature apart from other theoretical studies of neural networks, where one witnesses new involvement of sophisticated mathematical tools and insights. As mentioned, the two-layer case has enjoyed numerous efforts to establish global convergence (see e.g. [MMN18, CB18b, RVE18, JMM19, RJBVE19, WLLM19, Chi19, Woj20]). Our work is the first to obtain global convergence guarantees in the MF regime for the multilayer case.

Two-layer networks: comparison to [CB18b]. Closely relevant to our thread of results is the work [CB18b]. This work treats the two-layer case under certain convergence and Morse-Sard assumptions and convex losses. To make a direct comparison with [CB18b], let us first focus on the two-layer case, and in particular, Theorem 31 together with its accompanying Assumption 6. Several elements in our analysis are inspired by this work; we also differ in crucial ways. Similar to [CB18b], our proof also hinges on the insight that a certain diversity property is held throughout the course of training. We assume a universal approximation property (Assumption 6.5), which is natural in neural network learning, and dispense with convexity of the loss, whereas [CB18b] does not utilize universal approximation and requires convex losses. In our convergence assumption - Assumption 6.4 - the moment convergence condition is similar to the convergence assumption in [CB18b]. We differ from [CB18b] fundamentally in the uniform convergence condition ess-sup  $\left| \frac{\partial}{\partial t} w_2(t,C_1,1) \right| \to 0$  of the second layer's weight. On one hand, this condition replaces the Morse-Sard condition in [CB18b], which is difficult to verify in general. On the other hand, it is a natural assumption to make: as shown in Proposition 33, if this uniform convergence condition fails, global convergence cannot be attained. In shorts, using the insight on diversity, together with universal approximation, we uncover a new mechanism for global convergence without the need for convex losses.

Multilayer networks. While [CB18b] is specific to two-layer networks, we further the insight on diversity to the multilayer case, where we introduce the new notion of bidirectional diversity. In the context of two-layer networks, diversity refers to that the first layer's weight distribution has full support in the Euclidean space. In the multilayer case, this notion no longer resides in the Euclidean space, but is realized in function spaces that are naturally described by the neuronal embedding framework. Moreover, as noted in Section 7.3, it highlights an interesting dynamical mechanism, in which adjacent layers interact with each other over time in such a way that diversity is preserved through the depth of the network and at any time, roughly speaking.

Similar to the two-layer case, in place of the Morse-Sard assumption in [CB18b], we show global convergence under uniform convergence of the gradient update at a certain layer (Theorems 34 and 38). Again we note per Propositions 36 and 39, there is a converse relation between this uniform convergence and global convergence; if the former fails, so does the latter.

Several of these insights are utilized in the recent work [FLYZ20] that proves a global convergence guarantee for a residual MF neural architecture under the uniform convergence assumption of the gradient update. In this architecture, a skip connection is introduced to route the first layer directly to the second last one. Thanks to this skip connection, diversity is essentially transferred directly from the first layer to the second last layer. In short, in [FLYZ20], diversity is maintained with the help of architectural imposition. In contrast, in our global convergence result for the multilayer case, diversity is maintained automatically by the training dynamics.

The work [LML $^{+}$ 20], which studies a type of composition of many two-layer MF networks, and a recent update of [SS19], which studies the three-layer case, establish conditions of stationary points to be global optima with certain overlapping ideas. However they require essentially a certain diversity assumption on the limit point (i.e. at convergence  $t = \infty$ ). We do not need to make this assumption: the remark in Section 6.2.1 highlights the dynamical nature of the proof where diversity is assumed at initialization  $t = 0$  only and proven to hold at any finite training time  $t < \infty$ . As explained in Section 6.2.1, diversity may not hold at  $t = \infty$  and global convergence can still be attained regardless.

Let us mention again that global convergence results in those works are proven under the convex loss assumption. On the other hand, our results allow for removal of this assumption and our proofs do not make use of convexity in any crucial way.

Convergence under Morse-Sard assumptions. Our framework is able to give a self-contained proof of global convergence under the Morse-Sard assumption, without the aforementioned uniform convergence assumption (Theorems 41 and 42).

Let us place this discussion in the two-layer context, particularly Theorem 41 and its accompanying Assumption 9. Observe that Assumption 9.2 follows immediately if  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}$  and  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\infty}$  satisfy Morse-Sard type regularity, i.e., the sets of regular values of  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}$  and  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\infty}$  are dense (hence the name "Morse-Sard"). Indeed, assume that  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}$  and  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\infty}$  satisfy Morse-Sard type regularity. Let  $\widehat{S}_{\delta} = \{u:\overline{\mathcal{F}} (u) > \overline{\mathcal{F}} (u_1^*) - \delta \}$ . In that case, for any stationary point  $u_{1}^{*}$  of  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}$  with  $\overline{\mathcal{F}} (u_1^*) > 0$ , and for any  $\delta_0 > 0$ , there exists  $\delta \in (0,\delta_0)$  so that any  $u\in \partial \mathrm{cl}(\widehat{S}_{\delta})$  satisfies  $\nabla \overline{\mathcal{F}} (u)\neq 0$ . Over a bounded connected component  $S_{\delta}$  of  $\widehat{S}_{\delta}$ , this immediately implies the existence of  $\xi >0$  such that  $|\nabla \overline{\mathcal{F}} (u)| > \xi$  for all  $u\in \partial \mathrm{cl}(S_{\delta})$ . Over an unbounded connected component  $S_{\delta}$  of  $\widehat{S}_{\delta}$ , whenever  $\overline{\mathcal{F}} (u_1^*) - \delta$  is a regular value of  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\infty}$ , there is  $\xi >0$  such that  $|\nabla \overline{\mathcal{F}} (u)| > \xi$  for  $u\in \partial \mathrm{cl}(S_{\delta})\setminus \mathbb{B}(0,r)$  for some  $r$  sufficiently large, where  $\mathbb{B}(0,r)$  is the ball around 0 with radius  $r$ . Since  $\overline{\mathcal{F}} (u_1^*) - \delta$  is a regular value of  $\overline{\mathcal{F}}$ , by making  $\xi >0$  smaller if needed, we can guarantee that  $|\nabla \overline{\mathcal{F}} (u)| > \xi$  for  $u\in \partial \mathrm{cl}(S_{\delta})\cap \mathbb{B}(0,r)$ , and hence  $|\nabla \overline{\mathcal{F}} (u)| > \xi$  for all  $u\in \partial \mathrm{cl}(S_{\delta})$ .

Thus our Morse-Sard condition is similar to (and slightly weaker than) the Morse-Sard assumption of [CB18b]. As stated, it is sufficient for this condition to hold w.r.t. the limit  $\overline{W} = \{\overline{w}_1,\overline{w}_2\}$ . A counterpart statement of the Morse-Sard assumption of [CB18b] would impose the condition on a generic class of pairs of functions  $\tilde{W} = \{\tilde{w}_1,\tilde{w}_2\}$  that contains  $\overline{W}$  and as such trivially imply our assumption.

As explained in Section 8.1, the Morse-Sard condition forces the interaction over time between the weights of the two layers in a specific way that guarantees global convergence. This idea was realized by [CB18b] in the language of Wasserstein gradient flows for a convex loss function  $\mathcal{L}$  and two-layer neural networks. Here in the two-layer case, firstly Theorem 41 extends the result to generic losses; secondly and more importantly, it demonstrates that the same idea could be naturally executed in our framework without the use of Wasserstein gradient flows.

Theorem 42 demonstrates further the applicability of our argument to the multilayer case, which the Wasserstein gradient flow formulation has difficulty with.

# 9.5 Empirical findings and other infinite-width scalings

Mathematical ideas aside, one important aspect is how well one can observe the MF limiting behavior in multilayer networks with finite but large widths, normalized under the MF scaling. This has been demonstrated positively in the work [Ngu19]. In particular, [Ngu19] performs experiments on several real-life machine learning tasks and finds that the evolution curves of certain performance metrics, such as the training loss and the classification accuracy, are almost insensitive to the widths - provided sufficiently large - and hence they exhibit a limiting behavior. As [Ngu19] shows, this occurs as soon as the widths are on the order of just a few hundreds, which is common in practice.

The MF scaling is not the only infinite-width scaling with interesting properties. Another popular scaling regime is the neural tangent kernel (NTK) scaling [JGH18, CB18a, DZPS19, ZCZG18, AZLS18,  $\mathrm{LXS^{+}19}$ ]. In the NTK scaling, the weights do not move and the learning dynamics becomes linearized, although several interesting properties such as convergence to the global optimum are attainable. For this reason, it is often said that the NTK-scaled infinite-width neural networks do not perform feature learning. This NTK-like behavior is not what is observed in practical neural networks with finite but large widths. In contrast, the MF-scaled networks have nonlinear dynamics and weights moving away from initialization, and are thus said to perform feature learning in the literature.

The MF scaling is not necessarily the only scaling with feature learning (see e.g. [Gol20, YH21, HCG]). It is known that in the standard scaling that matches with the usual practice, the networks are NTK-like in

the infinite-width limit [MMM19, YH21]. Consequently any infinite-width scalings with feature learning are only proxies of practical finite-width neural networks. Despite this fact, we note that [Ngu19] demonstrates on several real-life machine learning tasks that a MF multilayer network, without heavy hyperparameter tuning, can achieve realistic performances, comparable to practical neural networks that are similar in architectural designs and training procedures;  $\left[\mathrm{LML}^{+}20\right]$  demonstrates an improved performance over strong and well-tuned practical neural networks by using the MF scaling. In other words, the MF scaling offers a good proxy, with potentially no loss in practical performances.

A few alternative scalings, accompanied by suitable initializations and learning rates, are proposed in [Gol20, YH21, HCG] to avoid the NTK-like behavior. Theoretical understanding of feature learning in these scalings is currently limited to just a single SGD step, unlike our work which studies the full learning trajectory of MF networks and proves the presence of meaningful learning via global convergence. As said, all these scalings are proxies of practical finite-width networks. Furthermore it is argued in [MMM19] that for two-layer infinite-width networks that are close to practical networks, the behavior near initialization is more NTK-like, while that in the long-time horizon is more MF-like. We expect a similar situation for the multilayer case, in which case it is insufficient to understand neural networks by analyzing only a few initial SGD steps. Our work also demonstrates the goodness of well-designed non-i.i.d. initializations, which thus far have been under-explored in the literature.

In a later follow-up work [PN21b], our neuronal embedding framework is extended to study a finite-width correction to the infinite-width MF limit and the implicit bias of gradient descent training in this finite-width regime, hence paving the path to address the aforementioned limitation of the infinite-width viewpoint.
